The Labour of Infrastructuring
An inquiry into participatory design in the public sector

1
ABSTRACT
Every organisation, cooperation, project or social movement is qu
intessentially a cluster of alignments between people, places and 
things. Through these alignments, networks are made, and through 
these networks action can be made possible or be constricted. These 
socio-material alignments, clusters and/or networks are understood 
within this thesis as infrastructures, and this thesis is an inquiry into 
how to mobilise infrastructures. Mobilising socio-material infra
structures over time is what I refer to as infrastructuring. 
 
Situated within the discipline of participatory design and the 
theoretical traditions of science and technology studies and feminist 
technoscience, this thesis investigates the issue of agency within the 
infrastructuring processes. The thesis departs from the notion that all 
agency is relational and made through relations. These relations may 
be material, power or affective. This concept poses a political imperati
ve to those infrastructuring practitioners-the individuals who labour 
to create new alignments and move the infrastructure forward-to 
consider the marginalised voices within the infrastructure. The labour 
performed to do this is not, the thesis argues, a prestigious, artisan 
work but rather a slow, caring and repetitive maintenance labour. 
Informed by the theories of Hannah Arendt, this thesis differentiates 
between this kind of labour and work. Arendt showed work, labour 
and action as three interictally intertwined yet distinct notions that 
define ways of being in the world-ways of acting politically. The th
ree notions reinforce and complement each other; however, this thesis 

places particular emphasis on labour. Labour is often made invisible, 
feminised and undervalued, and this thesis investigates labour within 
the infrastructuring processes and suggests methods to illuminate and 
support it.
 
The thesis draws upon three case studies located in Malmö 
and Lund, Sweden. All three projects were situated within public se
ctor work and within projects that emphasised citizen engagement and 
dialogue. The case studies have the commonality of infrastructuring: 
they are present both as a subject of study and as a method for both 
participants and researchers. Methodologically, the Ph.D. project has 
been conducted through practice-based, participatory, programmatic 
design research, which draws together the case studies into an enquiry. 
Finally, this thesis proposes three 'programmatic answers' that address 
the issue of agency within the infrastructuring processes. 
 
The first programmatic answer, feral infrastructures, re-for
mulates the initial worldview of the programme and articulates infra
structures as messy and unyielding to the organisers' attempts to cate
gorise them. The boundaries of the infrastructures stretch way beyond 
the socio-material borders of a defined project or organisation. The 
thesis argues that this poses an imperative to the infrastructuring prac
titioner to become sensitised to her terrain and to develop a reflexive 
praxis to interact with it. 
 
The second programmatic answer, affective infrastructuring, 
recognises affect as a matter of concern within the infrastructuring 
labour. Emotional labour and affective economies are raised here as 
factors that can make or break collaborative doings. This is discussed 
in an argument for ethics-of-care. 
 
The third and final programmatic answer, collaborative anec
dotalization, is a proposed method for interacting with the messy, af
fective terrain of infrastructures. Anecdotalization is presented here as 
a reciprocal practice beyond mere descriptions: holding within it the 
ability of defining social realities, re-telling and challenging them and 
furthering and re-aligning them. The notion of collaborative anecdo
talization suggests that no one actor can hold a complete overview of 
an infrastructure, and without collaborative descriptions, it is impos
sible to identify, understand and create those alignments that infra
structuring practitioners seek.

 
This thesis uses anecdotes as situated, embodied accounts of 
empirical data. The stories re-told in this book have been selected to 
invite the reader into the practical work, which underpins the concepts 
presented above, and, in congruence with the project's methodology, 
calls into consideration that any event or interaction can be viewed 
from multiple perspectives and tell multiple tales.

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
 
This section, although placed first, is the last thing I write before 
the thesis goes to print. I have a draft of the thesis next to me, covered in 
post-its and bleeding with notes in the margins, and it is a strange thing to 
think that these pages are the result of a life as much as of a project. I used 
to joke with my Ph.D. colleagues that the dissertation itself would never 
happen. 'I'll never manage to clean all of this up' I'd say, exasperated 'I will 
have to show up on the day of my defence with a untidy pile of post-its and 
say taa-daa'. And now, a few years later, I'm sitting here wondering if per
haps I should have handed in that pile of notes, as it is in many ways a more 
accurate representation of the work, the process, the life. I worry that it is 
on those post-it notes that the beating heart of this life/project showed, and 
that no carefully structured academic passages can reflect this. Perhaps the 
reader will never fully appreciate the impact that you - family, friends, and 
comrades alike - have had on the finished product. But I know, and I am 
very grateful to all of you who remain nameless here, but who have added 
the colour and the flair to what would have otherwise been a dull and dry 
thesis, and (most importantly) life. Thank you.
 
 
I have been fortunate to have two such kind, knowledgable, and in
spiring supervisors as Professor Per-Anders Hillgren and Dr Kristina Lind
ström. Per-Anders Hillgren has been exceptionally generous with both time 
and patience, and has met every question with interest. He taught me to 
not be dutiful in my approach research, but to meet it with humility and 
curiosity. 
 
My second supervisor, Dr Kristina Lindström, has the astoun

9
ding ability to ask questions with impeccable precision and timing. 
Thank you, Kristina, for your thoroughness, thoughtful comments, 
and always insightful advice. I'm very grateful to have had this chan
ce to work with and learn from you both.

through. Some of these I have barely made note of, others I have 
crashed right into with a spectacular lack of grace. My discussants 
during the mile-stone seminars have all been of great help in anti

have provided me with encouraged to keep working. Thank you 
for reading, commenting, and lending me your eye on the work.

PROLOGUE
 
In 2010, in the aftermath of the global financial crisis, I be
gan my journey into academia and into the world of design. I mo
ved to London to study a combined honours Bachelors' degree in 
Graphic Design and Illustration. I stood on shaky legs on the tube, 
suitcase in hand, without really knowing where to get off. The uni
versity became my safe port in the big city, and my studies became 
my vessel. As I write this, over a decade has passed, but those two 
things remain true, and I remain very grateful to have had a safe port 
in my life and a vessel that has served me well. As a first-generation, 
working-class student, I have never taken the university for granted, 
as I know it is not a space designed for me. 
 
Those first years in London were tumultuous, and as the 
student movement against raised fees and education cuts rolled over 
the country, I became an activist and part of a socialist group. It 
was an exciting time to be a student, to be in London and to be a 
socialist. Rising to the surface in these years was the notion that the 
neoliberal logic is fundamentally unsustainable: We cannot increase 
employment by lowering taxes on the rich; postponing retirement 
ages does not increase youth employment; privatisation and market 
competition are not the best guarantees of quality for schools and 
public services; loans will not set us free (Berardi, 2012, p. 44-45). 
The system as we knew it-and as many of us had relied on growing 
up-was collapsing in front of our eyes. We were living in the co
ming of no future (ibid., p. 42). I had, as mentioned, arrived to study 
graphic design and illustration, but would end up spending most 

13
of my waking hours protesting, organising or thinking about pro
testing and organizing. During the student movement I was taught 
Marxism in action, I learned the invaluable lesson that politics is so
mething that we do. During those years, we discussed political phi
losophy, strategy and contemporary politics inside police kettles. We 
organised caucuses, arranged picket lines and wrote manifestos. We 
linked our fight to on-going strikes: the electricians' strike, the NHS, 
the teachers' strike, the underground transport workers strike-un
derstanding that the struggles that we were facing were linked to a 
larger attack on the welfare state. When the Egyptian Revolution 
took place, we took to the streets once again: the struggle knew no 
borders. 
 
In the meantime, I often found that my designer skills would 
come in handy within the movement. Aside from being an adequate 
banner maker and making myself useful in the office for socialist 
publications that my group produced, I found that the doing of po
litics very often required the same skill as the doing of design. We 
would be presented with a situation, and we would, on our feet, 
figure out how to move forward. As we grew more experienced, we 
would have rehearsed some of these moves and could better anti
cipate which actions would generate which results. Being an acti
vist-designer was never the issue. This issue arose when I sought to 
apply politics to design. In the ivory halls of my design education, 
politics was deemed superfluous. Never explicitly part of the brief 
and rarely an encouraged detour. 
 
In 2014, I moved up to Glasgow and invested two masters 
into the intersection between sociological theories of citizenship and 
design innovation. Here, I sought to understand what a designer-ac
tivist might be. This space opened up design to me and afforded 
me a vocabulary to speak of design in ways I had not been able to 
before. I was still troubled by design innovation's devotion to the 
corporate world, which I viewed-and still do-as a contradiction 
to discourses around social and environmental sustainability (and 
beyond). This tension became my primary focus during my time in 
Glasgow. What I found, however, at the end of my first master's de

14
gree, was that I had talked myself into a corner. With my new, fancy 
sociological vocabulary, I was able to critique the ethical position of 
my designer actions, and had done so to such an extent that I could 
no longer move. Every step I took was problematic, so I declared 
that I was no longer a designer and would never design again. Stifled 
and stunted, I began my second master's degree, around which time 
I was introduced to participatory design. Drawn to the ideological 
basis of PD and to the discipline's ability to combine theory and 
practice, I began making smaller democratic design experiments in 
my otherwise Actor-Network Theory (ANT) oriented study. By the 
end of this master's degree, I applied to Malmö University, and con
ducted my interview over an unreliable Skype connection. I remem
ber frantically waving a dog-eared copy of Making Futures (Ehn et 
al., 2014) at the camera and thinking that if, despite everything, I 
was to be a designer, then this was how it was supposed to be done. 
The idea that the political comes first and that design ought to be a 
tool (or vessel, to not mix metaphors) with which we can handle/
cope/master it, has shaped the outset of this Ph. D. The research 
has also undeniably been shaped by both the fear of acting inherited 
from academia and my ideological belief that design has something 
to do in the world where refraining would be indefensible. The issue 
of agency, as is the underlying imperative of this Ph. D., was born 
out of these tensions. The issue of agency, therefore, is not merely 
the ability to act or the privilege to refrain from acting. It is the un
derstanding that the actions of one has a bearing on their extended 
network, as agency is relational. There is, in short, a politico-ethical 
notion in when, how and why we as designers and researchers choo
se to act, and when, how and why we choose to hold back. 
 
This thesis aims to make a contribution to the field of parti
cipatory design and aims to provide the reader with two takeaways. 
First, it aims to provide a discussion around the issue(s) of agency 
within the field, and suggests that the practices of maintaining and 
managing a collaborative doing of infrastructures is a labour in the 
Arendtian understanding of the word. The thesis aims to exemplify 
such infrastructuring practices within the public sector and present 

15
these within an Arendtian framework. Second, the thesis proposes a 
sensitised methodological approach to infrastructuring. This is done 
through the presentation of three theoretical concepts-drawn from 
empirical practice-based research-that each present a perspective 
on the issue of agency (why, what and how).

16
1. BRIEF OVERVIEW OF THE THESIS
 
The primary concern of this dissertation is to investigate the issues 
of agency within democratic design experiments in the public sector. The 
issues of agency lie at the core of participatory design practice, as it has 
evolved around questions of the inherent power relations between desig
ners and users in the design process. The discipline, which has been for
malised since the 1970s, has an extensive repertoire of methods guided by 
the principles of inclusion and democratisation. This thesis departs from 
the understanding that participatory design is first and foremost a political 
project and therefore, considering it within its contemporary social context 
is imperative. Democratic design experiment 'is a tentative formation of an 
issue and a public in mutual emergence' (Binder et al., 2015, p. 162). De
mocratic design experiments within the public sector reveal a new dimen
sion beyond the democracy at work (Ehn, 1988a): Beyond the actors from 
the workplace and the designers, citizen-actors are now also involved. This 
three-way entanglement requires a highly sensitised practice that recognises 
the extended networks of each participating actor. 
 
Infrastructuring is a notion that has been used within participatory 
design to signify a sensitised practice (Star & Ruhleder, 1996; Suchman, 
2002). This thesis defines infrastructuring as the long-term mobilisation of 
infrastructures over time. Infrastructuring not only entails the building of 
an infrastructure but also works iteratively with the imbrication of layers 
in an organisation, such as seeing the invisible currents of power and con
trol and their consequences (Star, 2002). The term draws upon science and 
technology studies (STS), in which infrastructures are defined as socio-ma

17
terial networks. Star and Ruhleder (1996) expanded the notion and 
suggested that an infrastructure is a living thing-that it is constant
ly changing and growing dialectally in relation to its members and 
to other parallel infrastructures. An individual member or an infra
structure 'is often a member of multiple communities of practice 
which use technologies differently, and which thus have different 
demands on their flexible-standard requirements' (Star & Ruhleder, 
1996, p. 112). Accordingly, infrastructuring places emphasis on the 
network as an active verb, which is a participatory practice (Karasti, 
2014). It follows that the question of how we do/activate/perform 
infrastructures is relevant to participatory design; these concepts are 
political interventions that often hold a dual nature of empowering 
and disempowering. Understanding infrastructuring as a political 
action requires that we recognise both its constraints and possibili
ties and its constructive, as well as destructive, power (Star, 2007). 
Studying the underlying behind-the-scene notions of infrastructures 
also means connecting the materiality of a situation with the prac
tices that are made and made from it (Star, 2002). This approach 
allows us to learn about the intricate nature of membership, partici
pation and exclusion within organisations. 
 
While design has often been considered an artisan practice, 
infrastructuring uses much of the design's tools, repertoire and affor
dances in ongoing care and maintenance labour. In particular, when 
tied to public sector work, where the common or collective 'good' 
is and should be a factor, the practice of infrastructuring demands 
negotiations and compromises on behalf of the many. The artisan 
designer may produce the most beautiful, most prestigious or the 
most ingenious solution to a problem; however, the collective desig
ner's priorities must always be to develop situated designs. To make 
a distinction between artisan design work and collective care work 
through design, we borrowed the concepts of work and labour from 
Arendt's (1958) writings in political philosophy. 
Arendt's (1958) The Human Condition defined the public sphere as 
the common spaces and places that humans share. Altering shared 

18
spaces is political and may be done physically or intellectually. We 
remould the shared public sphere either through material changes or 
through the act of speaking. By doing so, we change the conditions 
not just for ourselves but also for the people around us. Through 
work, we produce artefacts that change/create shared spaces. The 
beautiful ceramic vase of the craftsman, the great novel of the cen
tury, the engineer's new bridge, the politician's motion for a new 
law-all these works change the world around others and alter the 
living conditions for more than the craftsperson. This work is im
portant and prestigious because it contributes to the common and 
creates a world that we all share. However, the world, in all its glo
ry, cannot sustain itself in the future-making work alone. Behind 
the scenes, in the private realm, resides labour. Labour is the futile 
everyday strive. The endless and repetitive efforts to keep us alive: 
to feed us and to keep us warm. The objects and relations of the 
man-made world require maintenance and care, which is a heavy 
task that lacks the prestige of work. The collaborative designer who 
practices infrastructuring often takes a deliberate step back from 
producing objects in favour of caring for the relationships in the 
common. 
This thesis will often refer to an 'infrastructuring practitioner' to 
emphasise that the practice of infrastructuring does not only belong 
to co-designers but also to different mobilisers and organisers. In 
this thesis, many of the actors that I will refer to as infrastructuring 
practitioners are co-designers by training, but others are civil ser
vants, city planners, librarians, community organisers, activists, sa
fety coordinators and so on. These actors share the ambition of cre
ating and furthering alignments (Ehn, 2008) within an infrastructure 
with the intention of mobilising it over time. This plan may be done 
using methods and strategies from participatory design; however, 
these schemes are not a sui generis part of the infrastructuring prac
titioner's repertoire. 
 
One defining feature of the practice is that by recognising 
oneself as part of an infrastructure, the infrastructuring practitioner 
sacrifices some of her own agency to the collective. Agency, which 

19
is the ability to act, is relational. The ability to act individually is 
almost always available to us: We are rarely bound and gagged to 
such an extent that no movement or speech is possible. However, the 
ability to act comes with social and systemic ties that grant us more 
or less freedom to act meaningfully. As citizens, the most severe pu
nishment we can receive is imprisonment. In jail, our movements are 
constricted, and our ability to speak to whomever we want is res
tricted. By contrast, certain positions have more freedom than most 
citizens do not enjoy. A president has the agency to make decisions 
on behalf of the people and has tools and privileges that most of us 
can only dream of. A president of a nation has power over others, 
granted to him or her by the agency of his or her appointment. This 
feature is an important distinction to make because while we speak 
of agency, we also speak of power-however, these are not inter
changeable. On the one hand, power can have its source in violence; 
it can be taken and exercised without the consent of others. Agency, 
on the other hand, suggests a social contract. In most organisations, 
the subject positions are made clear: Leadership comes with signifi
cant power but also with responsibilities. As citizens, we are affor
ded certain agencies and are denied others. In a way, it is a distribu
ted agency (Bennett, 2010). In meetings between the public sector 
and private citizens, subject positions clash, and the social contracts 
that exist on each side may result in a conflict or misunderstanding 
between the parties. Foucault (1982) argued that an individual man 
or woman are not only made subjects (through mechanisms, such as 
1 
In Foucault's (1982) essay entitled Why Study Power? The Question of the 
Subject, he argued that the subject position is the core in his writings-fore
grounding even his theory of power. This issue is because subject positions are 
essential to the struggle for power and therefore, also of governance. Foucault 
(1982) wrote that 'A relationship of confrontation reaches its term, its final 
moment (and the victory of one of the two adversaries), when stable mechanisms 
replace the free play of antagonistic reactions. Through such mechanisms one 
can direct, in a fairly constant manner and with reasonable certainty, the con
duct of others. For a relationship of confrontation, from the moment it is not a 
struggle to the death, the fixing of a power relationship becomes a target-at one 
and the same time its fulfillment and its suspension. And in return, the strategy 
of struggle also constitutes a frontier for the relationship of power, the line at 
which, instead of manipulating and inducing actions in a calculated manner, one 
must be content with reacting to them after the event. It would not be possible 
for power relations to exist without points of insubordination' (p. 794).

20
language or the sciences), but they also make themselves into sub
jects1 (such as when a person subject themselves to a sexuality). La
tour (1999) polemically argued that 'Society is constructed, but not 
socially constructed' (p. 198). In Latour's (1993) book We Have Ne
ver Been Modern, he suggested that postmodernism is a symptom, 
not a solution (ibid, p. 74). 'Are you not fed up', Latour asks, 'at 
finding yourself forever locked into language alone, or imprisoned 
in social representations alone, as so many social scientists would 
like you to be? We want to gain access to things themselves, not only 
to their phenomena' (ibid, p. 90). In this thesis, I selected to follow a 
relational understanding of agency on the grounds that I view each 
participant as more than one position, such as when we consider 
that the public servant is also a citizen when she leaves work or that 
the programmer may also be the user of the system she develops. 
This perspective on power resonates with the theories around dist
ributed agency. Distributed agency is a term most often used within 
STS and actor-network-theory (ANT) discourse. This term not only 
affords a crucial understanding of the relationality of agency but has 
also been criticised for pacifying inherently political connections and 
replacing the vocabulary of activation with a vocabulary of analysis 
(Bessire & Bond, 2014; Knox, 2017; Martin, 2014). Within parti
cipatory design and discourses around infrastructuring, recognising, 
amplifying and mobilising the marginal voices within a given cons
tellation have always been emphasised. By contrast, ANT is an acti
ve political engagement with the situation. ANT seeks to understand 
agency, and participatory design seeks to redistribute it. 
 
The understanding of agency as relational has formed the 
bedrock of the ontology that this thesis departs from. It is an on
tology informed by STS and feminist technoscience, which crassly 
suggests that neutrality is not an option-not for the researchers, 
not for the designer and certainly not for the participatory desig
ner. We cannot move among other actors without affecting them or 
without being affected ourselves. Arendtian framing suggests that 
this-to move and be moved-is what it means to act in the public 
sphere and to perform a political act. Refraining from acting, and 

21
in turn refraining from affecting and being affected, is a possibility 
for those who consider themselves unaffected by the shared public 
sphere. This thesis is not written for them. It has been written for 
the participatory designer, who not only sees the urgency of political 
action but also recognises the ethical tension that resides in each ac
tion. This tension has been the focal point of this thesis, and around 
which the programme has taken shape.
1.1 BRIEF OVERVIEW OF THE RESEARCH PROJECT
 
This research has been conducted through programmatic 
design research, where the programme acts as a 'frame and foun
dation for carrying out a series of design experiments and interven
tions' (Brandt et al., 2011, p. 19). The programme differs from a 
conventional research enquiry in that it is often not phrased as a 
question or a thesis to be tested but as a worldview. According to 
Redström (2017), programmatic design 'maintains a "hard core" 
of basic assumptions or theories that are never really questioned. 
Indeed, accepting the hard core is necessary for participating in the 
programme. Development over time, therefore, primarily happens 
in the "protecting belt" surrounding the hard core, and as long as 
this development continuously leads to new ideas, new theories, and 
so on, the programme remains progressive' (Redström, 2017. p. 
87). Meanwhile, the programme becomes obsolete when the protec
tive belt only serves to protect the hard core from being challenged 
by new ideas or alternative approaches (ibid.). It follows that the 
above-mentioned ontology has been a key influence in the choice 
of methods: It is the worldview in which the programme has been 
shaped. The methods were selected reflexively to drive the program
me forward, and this decision was always informed by an ethics of 
care.
 
The basis of this programme has been formed with three 
projects: Amiralsstaden, The Life Concept (in Swedish Livs
konceptet) and The Democracy Ambassadors (in Swedish Demo

22
kratiambassadörerna). These projects were the primary case studies 
and the primary sources of empirical data. However, the programme 
also draws upon three case studies, which are referred to as contex
tual-the Malmö Innovation Arena (MIA), the Orkney wind-energy 
study and the meta-study of the researcher herself. The reason for 
including the latter three perspectives is to emphasise the 'bigger 
picture'. The three primary case studies will be presented as just 
that, case studies, for clarity; however, the secondary cases are inclu
ded to remind the reader that the cases did not exist within a void 
and that they were always embedded within a broader entanglement 
of ideas, needs, biases, histories, processes and material structures. 
The three primary case studies were all situated within public se
ctor work and conducted alongside Malmö Stad2. The case studies 
have the commonality of infrastructuring and are presented both 
as a subject of study and as a method for both participants and 
researchers. Each study tells us something about infrastructuring. 
In Amiralsstaden, the notions of power, marginalised voices and 
agency were prominent through the project's experimental use of the 
method of knowledge alliances (Stigendal & Alwall, 2015). By chal
lenging the often invisible knowledge hierarchies of public sector 
work, Amiralsstaden illuminated not only what was missing but 
also suggested how we might improve the situation. Within Livskon
ceptet, the ant-like trailing through an unfamiliar terrain, as done 
by the representatives (the so-called safety coordinators) from Save 
the Children, affords us insight into the labour that infrastructuring 
often entails. By learning from their experience, I have realised that 
infrastructuring is a care work and for maintenance and restraint, 
as well as for action and interventions. Demokratiambassadörerna 
(The Democracy Ambassadors), through mere presence and action, 
2 
Malmö Stad in Swedish literally translates to Malmö City; however, this term 
is commonly used to refer to the Malmö Municipality. In Sweden, the local 
municipalities (a total of 290 municipalities) have a degree of autonomy, which 
functions as a local self-government directed by a municipal executive board 
composed of elected representatives. The municipality is responsible for seve
ral-if not most-public services, such as schools, social services, health and 
environment, urban planning and so on. These matters are managed by working 
departments, such as, the education department, social services department or 
the urban planning department. 

23
became a spotlight on marginalised or neglected voices within the 
city as an infrastructure. 
 
My role within these projects has been reflexive. I have been 
part of the above-mentioned projects from 2017 to 2019. However, 
my role has changed throughout this time. In the beginning, I was 
present as a critical friend. I would listen, map, ask questions and 
lead reflection workshops. Tentatively, over time, my role became 
more active, where I also took initiatives, probed and made inven
tions. A central issue within this Ph. D. project has been to question 
the legitimacy of the design researcher's presupposed authority in a 
given project; finding ways to reflect on my own presence with each 
group is imperative. This factor has been particularly true when in
vestigating infrastructuring, which, as mentioned above, has served 
in this project as both a subject of study and as a method of research 
and for designing. Starting from a broad framing of the issues of 
agency in infrastructuring processes, I entered into dialogue with the 
situation(s), and through an iterative process of constant compari
son (Glaser & Strauss, 1967), I began to develop a few theoretical 
concepts that allowed me to discuss the tensions, challenges and op
portunities that I found. 
1.2 READING THIS THESIS
 
In the following chapter, chapter 2, I will outline a literature 
review of the notion of infrastructuring. Here, I seek to peel the con
cept like an onion by the term by returning to its core-the notion of 
the infrastructure-before it became a verb. I will then trace it back 
to its current definition within participatory design and, in the pro
cess, discuss some of the concepts that have emerged in the junction 
between infrastructuring and participatory design. I will discuss the 
design thing and democratic design experiments, as these concepts 
are relevant within the design programme of this thesis. The pro
gramme, in turn, is the main focus of chapter 3, which is dedicated 
to the methods, research context and case studies formed in the Ph. 

24
D. project. As programmatic design research, which was introduced 
briefly in the introduction, accentuates the iterative nature of prac
tice-based enquiry, I will present three approaches through which I 
have conducted my research. Subsequently, these approaches have 
generated three programmatic answers-conclusions drawn from 
the iterative process of call-and-response between theory and empi
rical data. 
 
Chapters 4, 5 and 6 are theoretical chapters. Each chap
ter represents a programmatic answer. They are supported by field 
work through anecdotes, highlighted to the reader in the headline. 
By using anecdotes as a way of grounding the theory, I seek to keep 
my theories 'small'. I also seek to allow the situations that have in
spired the theoretical discussion to remain open and complicated in 
an attempt to avoid the researcher's privilege of defining and deter
mining social realities. Many of the short stories have nuances, as 
does life. 
 
Chapter 4 is titled 'Feral Infrastructuring' and should be 
read as an expanded research programme. As a concept, feral in
frastructuring summarises the stance of this thesis regarding infra
structures. It suggests that the installed base (Star & Ruhleder, 1996) 
of the infrastructure comes with ideological ties, which informs our 
understanding of who the actors and users are (and should be) and 
how they may act. Having conducted my field work both inside and 
outside an organisational setting and at the intersection of many 
different projects and processes, which I occasionally refer to as 'the 
city' as an affectionate overstatement, I found it to be far messier 
and less linear. Actors frequently move between projects and sit on 
several chairs simultaneously. Resources for a workshop are brought 
from home and uninvited guests sometimes provide groundbrea
king information. Feral infrastructuring provides an imperative to 
question assumptions of the 'right' way to participate. The chapter 
outlines the summary of the combined literature review and resear
ch programme: an ideological reading of infrastructuring suppor
ted by observations from the field work. The chapter presents the 

25
programmatic answer that infrastructures are considerably complex 
and distributed to be contained within one organisation or project 
and that they are comparable to a feral ecology. The chapter sug
gests that infrastructurers must practice maintenance and care work 
(a labour) as much as building and constructing (a work). 
 
Chapter 5 discusses one of the potential tensions that this 
new reading of infrastructuring entails and highlights, as the title 
'Affective Infrastructuring' suggests, the affective tier present in the 
infrastructuring process. This affective tier requires, as argued in 
this chapter, the infrastructuring practitioner to develop a sensitivity 
to the ways that affect moves and accumulate within a co-creative 
process and to illuminate the emotional labour required to maintain 
it-a labour that is often rendered invisible. Affective infrastructuring 
emerged as a concept after I observed the infrastructuring labour of 
actors within the Amiralsstaden and Livskonceptet projects. As a 
concept, it seeks to inspire two discussions. First, it seeks to high
light the presence of affect within socio-material networks. Emo
tions, affective ruptures (Knox, 2017), attachments and emotional 
labour (Ahmed, 2004, 2014; Hochschild, 1979, 2012) are an inte
gral part of building infrastructures, and as an act of labour, it is one 
that often goes unnoticed. In this thesis, I will highlight a few anec
dotes from the case studies that illustrate how affect, if left unat
tended, can accumulate and run amok within a constellation (Ah
med, 2014). Second, affective infrastructuring draws on the ANT 
notion of tracing or path-finding (Ingold, 2000), walking like an 
ant through the anthill (Latour, 2005) and developing a heightened 
understanding of one's complex surroundings. Law and Singleton 
(2012) suggested that ANT may perhaps 'best [be] understood as 
a sensibility, a set of empirical interferences in the world, a worldly 
practice or a craft' (p. 2). In this thesis, as suggested by Law and 
Singleton (2012), sensitising is defined as literacy, which is develo
ped as the actor moves through her situation. As with any kind of 
literacy, the level of fluidity necessary is determined by the situation 
(for example: do you need to be able to read road signs to find your 
way or to be able to discuss postmodern symbolism?). 

26
 
Chapter 6, which is titled 'Collaborative Anecdotalization', 
offers a theory-method package as a possible approach for the sen
sitised infrastructurer. In this thesis, I will describe it as a three-fold 
way of using anecdotes within infrastructuring processes: as a way 
of generating new knowledge, as a way of being within the infra
structure and as a way of 'telling tales'. The term anecdotalization 
(rather than anecdote) draws upon Michaels' (2012) anthology, the 
Inventive Methods, which states that the anecdote is more than just 
a narrative. It is, according to Michaels (2012), a narrative for the 
telling, designed to be circulated and retold. Historically, the anec
dote has not been considered within the sciences. The term anecdo
tal evidence does not evoke the academic rigour we ask from sci
entific methods. Michaels (2012) argued that it has a natural place 
within scholarly practices of responsive and performative research. 
Michaels (2012) said that 'The point is that the anecdote, unlike 
typical forms of auto-ethnography, can serve as a means for tra
cing the co-emergence of research, researcher and researched' (pp. 
26-27). I have found that the living anecdote can do more than 
this. Aligning myself with STS scholars, such as Law (2004, 2015), 
I wholeheartedly agree that ours is a many-world world. In addi
tion, this multiplicity of social realities creates an undeniable power 
imbalance inherent in whoever defines them. Thus, I refer again to 
the participatory design principle of collaboration as a reminder of 
the need, within democratisation processes, to pay special heed to 
marginalised voices. Collaborative anecdotalization emphasises the 
social and relationship-building aspect of anecdotalization. 
 
In congruence with collaborative anecdotalization, this the
sis will present its empirical material in the form of anecdotes dist
ributed throughout the dissertation. The purpose of these anecdotes 
is to present a situated account of the processes of infrastructuring-
bearing in mind that infrastructuring is a practice of plurality and 
therefore could not be presented from a god's eye view (Haraway, 
1988). These anecdotes have, as the thesis will further elaborate, not 
only been an important part of the research process as a way of re
lating and making sense of the events that occurred during my time 

27
in the field but also equally as a way of inviting more voices than 
my own into this academic work. Finally, in the closing Chapter 7, I 
summarise and reflect on the thesis. 

28
2. DEFINING THE INFRASTRUCTURE
 
This thesis defines infrastructuring as the long-term developme
nt of socio-material networks over time. The question of infrastructu
re has grown from STS discourse to other fields. For example, within 
architecture studies, it addresses notions of built environments and 
organisational theory (Boland & Collopy, 2004); within literary theo
ry, infrastructure may help surface shrouded accounts and narratives; 
within social geography, we may discuss the values and biases inherent 
in a map, as well as mapping practices; and within information science 
(Bowker & Star, 2000; Star & Bowker, 2006). Below, I seek to account 
for the notion of infrastructure and address its relevance to participatory 
design through a recognition and sensitisation towards the pluralistic 
dialectics of things. Notably, participatory design has always been about 
the situated, the place and the people at a particular point in time, whe
reas the infrastructure reveals their position and their ties to the world 
outside 'the situation'. These connections are inherently political and as 
such, participatory designers must recognize them. 
 
STS emerged between the 1950s and 1980s from several disci
plines and was solidified as a branch of science studies. It drew on the 
sociology of scientific knowledge (SSK), which is a discipline in reaction 
to traditional scientific means of knowledge creation. Science studies 
challenge the notion that scientific methods can ensure neutrality and 
objectivity in science and emphasise that knowledge creation is a prac
tice (Van House, 2004). Knowledge, and which kinds of knowledge are 
most valuable, is an ancient discussion and predates STS. Ancient Greek 

29
philosophers Socrates, Plato and Aristotle debated the distinctions 
between practical and theoretical knowledge. On the one hand, the 
'craft' knowledge of tradesmen is formed (i.e. farmers, shoemakers, 
etc.), and on the other hand, the theoretical 'knowledge of life' that 
scholars attained through mathematics, philosophy and other pre
stigious intellectual pursuits has also been established (Scharff & 
Dusek, 2014)3. Arguably, this factor established the virtue of the 
scientific fact and ranked against the knowledge of the everyday lay
man (or woman). This tradition is what STS seeks to study: How 
does the scientific facts shape society, and how does society shape 
the scientific fact? (Fleck, 1935). Kuhn (1962) developed the notion 
of the scientific paradigm, observing that the sciences, both natural 
and social, had matured, and many scientists have found that 'Ad
ditional research makes it harder, not easier, to answer questions' 
(Kuhn, 1962, p. 2). Kuhn (1962) suggested that communities inform 
and influence paradigms and that scientific revolutions happen as a 
consequence of shifts in scientific paradigms. Questioning the scien
tific truth opened up to further questioning of notions of rigour and 
neutrality in the sciences. Considering the long-standing histories of 
some of the many scientific fields that STS grew from (e.g. besides 
sociology, which may be a relatively 'young' field of study, other 
significantly older medical sciences are also considered), it could 
perhaps be seen as curious that the question of how society, politics 
and culture are shaping and being shaped by science and technology 
should rise to the surface in several academic 'silos' simultaneously. 
However, a curious historical change occurs that STS itself is well 
equipped to answer. 
3 
Technê is the term for craft, and notably, Plato (In Republic VIII) discussed it 
as a dialectic, as something which has been allowed to grow and develop, whe
reas Epistêmê-technê's polar-is defined as a more static knowledge. Epistêmê 
is concerned with what is necessary and '[H]ence it is eternal; for the things that 
are by unconditional necessity are all eternal' (Scharff & Dusek, 2014, p. 19). 
Epistêmê lacked the material base of Technê, as it was concerned with universal 
necessities, defined, amongst other virtues, by the principles that it would be 
teachable and learnable. The Greek dictionary also holds many more definitions 
of knowledge (Pronesis, Nous, Sophia, Aletheia, etc.); however, the relationship 
and early distinction between the knowlege of a practitioner and the knowledge 
of a scholar is particularly interesting when when look at terms, such as infra
structuring, that combines the two.

30
 
The disperse origins of STS as an interdisciplinary field have 
resulted in some distinct subdivisions, perhaps most accurately des
cribed as schools of thoughts4, which have specialised in illuminating 
specific perspectives within STS. Feminist technoscience is a school 
of thought that has been of particular importance to this thesis. This 
epistemological turn drew on the feminist struggles of its time, which 
had begun to shed light on the structural misogyny that exists in all 
aspects of society, from private relationships and family structures 
to educational and professional male biases and to the norm of the 
white male able-bodied citizen. Harding (1986), who wrote at the 
cusp of the 1980s, drew a line in the sand. She saw a past where the 
sciences had been owned by a distinctly white and male voice, and 
she called for a future of multiplicity. In doing so, she questioned not 
only the ethical and political bedrocks of science but also the legiti
macy of its outputs. Harding's (1986) writings made it clear that the 
sciences are constructed through society's norms, as much as society 
is constructed through science: an echo chamber of male perspecti
ves, with unbounded power in the Western world's science-centric 
morality. In summary, the biases of our time are more often than not 
reproduced through research (Haraway, 1988; Star, 1991; Suchman, 
1994b). Feminist technoscience makes a substantial contribution to 
the understanding of power within STS. 
2.1 THE CHARACTERISTICS OF AN INFRASTRUCTURE
 
The definition of infrastructure most central to this thesis 
follows the characteristics of an infrastructure, as defined by Star 
and Ruhleder (1996). These characteristics are often familiar (if not 
4 
The early days of SSK, at least, can be divided into the Edinburgh and the 
Bath school. The Edinburgh School took an interest in the macrosocial 'interest' 
approach, where membership in cultures or communities (i.e. class, professional 
identity, etc.) was a factor in the production of science. The Bath school instead 
focused on microsocial processes and proposed that controversies can be resol
ved through negotiation between actors (cf. Van House, 2004). 

31
common sense) to infrastructuring practitioners. By practitioners, I 
mean those whose work centres around mobilising things and pe
ople; in particular, those that I have met in relation to my field work 
and in my case studies, as well as those in my social circle, such as 
activists, researchers, designers and developers. Practitioners spend 
their everyday time in what Schön (1995) referred to as 'the swamp'. 
While the word 'infrastructuring' may not always be a part of their 
vocabulary, these practitioners seem to me have an innate under
standing of what the infrastructure is, or rather, what their infra
structure is. This aspect can span from knowing who to email for 
information to knowing that while the map reads that a particular 
street belongs to one area, its residents think of themselves as belon
ging to another. They know what inherent qualities their context 
has and how it behaves. To fully (or even partially) understand how 
something behaves is to know it beyond its static, intended func
tion. Many infrastructures are lucid entities beneath the surface, and 
their behaviour can vary with the changing seasons, depending on 
which day of the week and on which actor last activated it. The in
frastructuring practitioners understand the infrastructure's nuances, 
however contradictory, and often know instinctively how far their 
constellations reach.
 
From 1991 to 1994, Star and Ruhleder conducted an ethno
graphic study of the Worm Community System (WCS)-a 'hyper
library' developed to afford formal and informal communication 
and data access across many different labs and sites where biologists 
study gene structures. They observed the use of the software and 
the routine work around it. They also gathered information on the 
social and professional practices that occurred when the WCS was 
developed. They interviewed designers, biologists, computer deve
lopers and scientists to understand the complex emergence of in
frastructure. They wrote that '[A]nalytically, infrastructure appears 
only as a relational property, not as a thing stripped of use. [...] 
The shift de-emphasises things or people as simply causal factors in 
the development of such a system; rather, changes in infrastructural 
relations become central ́ (Star & Ruhleder, 1996, p. 113). Star and 

32
Ruhleder (1996) suggested that infrastructures emerge with the fol
lowing dimensions: 
 
Embeddedness
Star and Ruhleder (1996) wrote about digital communication 
in the mid 90s and proposed the notion that infrastructure 
exists as embedded within other structures, for example, adhoc networks, within social arrangements or technological 
constellations. In the moment of writing, almost three de
cades later, this observation is easily demonstrated. We only 
need to open our smartphone to find numerous apps that 
connect us to people, services and objects in both the physi
cal and virtual world to realise that the smartphone conta
ins a myriad of infrastructures embedded within the phone 
structure. 
 
Transparency 
The notion of transparency is perhaps the most conflic
ting of the infrastructure traits suggested by Star and Ruh
leder(1996). This fact is because, as we have already seen, 
infrastructure is 'sunk into' other networks, systems or cons
tellations and therefore not always apparent to onlookers or 
even users. However, Star and Ruhleder (1996) argued that 
it is transparent in the sense that 'it does not have to be re
invented each time or assembled for each task, but invisibly 
supports these tasks' Star & Ruhleder, 1996, p. 113). 
 
Reach or Scope 
The spatial or temporal reach of infrastructure goes beyond 
that of a single event. 
 
Learned as Part of a Membership
Star and Ruhleder (1996) wrote that '[S]trangers and outsi
ders encounter infrastructure as a target object to be learned 
about. New participants acquire a naturalised familiarity 
with the object as they become members' (Star & Ruhleder, 

33
1996, p. 113) Infrastructure creates a community of prac
tice (Wenger, 1998) around itself, as those who use it share 
a domain of interest that subsequently creates relationships 
and bonds. The members of a community of practice often 
develop a shared repertoire: vocabularies, tools and expe
riences. Many practitioners who have been members of a 
community take this innate knowledge for granted or write 
it of as instinct or gut feeling. Later in the thesis, I will argue 
that practitioners need to rehearse and develop awareness of 
the rules, repertoire and praises of their situation and mem
bership. I will refer to this as 'sensitisation'.
 
Links with Conventions of Practice
The communities of practice that form around an infra
structure consist of actors who already belong to multiple 
communities of practice. For example, think of how the 
conventions of computer programmers have been shaped by 
their use of QWERTY keyboards-the latter a de facto stan
dard that has followed us from typewriters-to computers 
to touchscreens.
 
Embodiment of Standards
Star and Ruhleder (1996) wrote that 'Modified by scope 
and often by conflicting conventions, infrastructure takes on 
transparency by plugging into other infrastructures and tools 
in a standardised fashion' (p. 113).
 
Built on an Installed Base 
'Infrastructure does not grow de novo; it wrestles with the 
inertia of the installed base and inherits strengths and limi
tations from that base' (i Star & Ruhleder, 1996, p. 113). As 
shown above, this 'installed base' is socio-material. 
 
Becomes Visible upon Breakdown
As infrastructure is embedded and 'sunk into' structures 
and social arrangements, it is normally invisible. It beco

mes visible only when it breaks, and we notice how far its 
reach goes. Think, for example, of when a server goes out or 
during a blackout. 
 
The above characteristics paint a portrait of infrastructu
re as a dynamic entity, material yet alive, evolving and in constant 
need of maintenance and care. Bowker (1994) used the notion of 
infrastructural inversion to talk about how infrastructures remain as 
invisible backdrops, and the characteristics illuminate the social and 
political structures and practices from which they emerged. Bowker 
(1994) illustrated this claim with an example of how computer 
machinery developed as a consequence of changes in office organisa
tions, rather than being causative of them. As suggested by Star and 
Ruhleder (1996), it is, as seen above, one of the defining characte
ristics of an infrastructure that it becomes visible upon breakdown. 
Latour (2010), who is one of the formative thinkers behind ANT, 
illustrated this tendency of incidents and disasters not only to make 
assemblages visible but also to incite them into a new course. Latour 
(2011) stated that 'In its simplest but also in its deepest sense, the 
notion of network is of use whenever action is to be redistributed' 
(p. 797). Ruptures in infrastructure can tell us more than what went 
wrong. Latour used the Columbia shuttle disaster as an example, 
where a NASA space shuttle dramatically exploded in 2003, as a 
critical incident that illuminated the connection between the object 
in the sky (the shuttle) and the 'swarm of entities' of the complex 
organisational body of NASA on the ground. Ruptures in an infra
structure tell us more than merely what went wrong; they attest to 
specific material relations, as demonstrated by, for example, the ar
rival of electricity (Boyer, 2015), the railroad (Bowker & Star, 2000; 
Hughes, 1983) and the appearance of a road (Harvey & Knox, 
2015). However, ruptures may also tell us more about the issues of 
the infrastructure-even those issues that do not cause the system to 
collapse or the space shuttle to explode. As stated by Neuman and 
Star (1996), 'Whether the system is "working" or "not working" 
may be much less clear than whether enough "water is coming out 
of the tap"' (p. 231). Sensitising means that the practitioner opens 
36

35
up to the grey zone between 'on' and 'off', where the ruptures not 
uncommonly point to5. 
 
Infrastructuring, as well as ANT, invites a sensitivity towards 
plurality. Grappling with plurality, as scientists, designers and infra
structurers, is not a small feat. Oftentimes, 'plurality' comes with 
contradictory needs, directions and priorities. Tracing the inverted 
(Bowker, 1994) co-becomings of politics, culture and the social 
through the lens of innovation, technology and science is particular
ly difficult, as the infrastructures that hold them have a tendency to 
sink into their surroundings (Star & Bowker, 2006), simultaneously 
expanding and blurring the boundaries of the project/organisation. 
The socio-material co-becoming of infrastructures affords us a sur
face through which socio-political and socio-cultural aspects may 
be decoded and understood (Harvey et al., 2017, p. 3). Recognising 
complexity within the infrastructure and acknowledging the poli
tical significance of plurality are imperative to the infrastructure, 
but it also begs the question: What rights do we have, as participa
tory designers or infrastructurers stepping into a situation, often as 
strangers to the group and the scene, to make interventions? 
2.2 THE INFRASTRUCTURE AS A NETWORK
 
As mentioned briefly in the previous chapter, STS and ANT 
have developed a repertoire that affords the decoding of messy plu
ralisms. However, it has also been criticised for pacifying inherently 
political connections and, as previously mentioned, for replacing the 
5 Notably, Akama (2015) discussed the space(s) between a Japanese philo
sophy called Ma (間). The concept invites us to consider the 'in-between' or 
'between-ness'. Akama (2015) stated that 'Ma is a strong presence in Japanese 
culture, for example, manifesting in spaces that are reconfigurable by sliding, 
semi-opaque screens in traditional houses. Corridors and entrances link inside 
and outside, blurring such boundaries. From this, we see that Ma as a betweenness is a merging of distinctions-a greyness in-between black and white-and 
implies a relational sensitivity' (p. 263).

36
vocabulary of activation with a vocabulary of analysis (Bessire & 
Bond, 2014; Knox, 2017; Martin, 2014). This aspect does not say 
that notions of power do not have a natural place within STS and 
ANT studies. The fact is quite the contrary: Power and agency have 
been at the very centre of the discourse around science, technology 
and society. Latour (1987) famously said that 'Science is politics by 
other means'; however, ANT still primarily affords a way of illumi
nating the power exercised within sciences and not a way of enga
ging with it. 
 
I remember the first time I watched someone present an 
ANT study. I had already sat through numerous presentations that 
afternoon and my mind had started to drift. But when I heard the 
presenter mention ANT, I woke up and was at the edge of my seat. 
I had come across the term before and was keen to understand it 
better. The presenter walked us through a painstakingly detailed 
account of the bike traffic outside her office and showed us all the 
imaginable connections that could be drawn from it. She had done a 
fantastic job, but at the end of the presentation, I was disappointed. 
'So what?' I thought, not understanding what I could do with the in
formation that ANT presented me with. The multiplicity, which the 
previous section of this text suggested that we, as researchers, desig
ners and activists, must develop a sensitivity towards, can be stun
ting. Once sensitised to the endless complexity of networks and the 
inevitable ripple effects of every action into such a network, acting 
becomes conceit. For example, buying a piece of Atlantic salmon for 
dinner also means engaging with the biologist classifications; with 
the domestication of Norwegian fishing industries; with the standar
dised fish manufacturing; with the state of the ocean(s); the metric 
system; with salmon lice and anaesthetics; and with the computer 
software that weighs the fish and translates it into biomass as to turn 
it into tradable goods for the fish market; it means engaging with 
Nasdaq (Lien & Law, 2011). If you instead turn around and say, 
'No thank you, I will refrain from having salmon today' and instead 
opt for a nice matsutake mushroom, you will soon find yourself equ
ally entangled in multi-species assemblages and 'more-than human 

37
dramas' (Tsing, 2015). 
 
Engaging with broad ecologies, such as the Atlantic salmon 
(Lien & Law, 2011) or the matsutake mushroom (Tsing, 2015), 
means recognising the infinite relationally of all things. Tracing the 
connections, as an horizontal archaeology (Pétursdóttir & Olsen, 
2017) of sorts, affords an understanding of the dialectics of the 
worlds we inhabit. A sensitisation towards the world of the resear
cher, designer and activist is necessary, but it does not invite action. 
In the next section of the text, I will account for the crucial suffix 
-ing, indicating a doing, and how participatory design uses the no
tion of infrastructuring. 
2.3 WHAT PARTICIPATORY DESIGN BROUGHT TO 
DISCOURSES ON INFRASTRUCTURES
 
The notion of infrastructure as something relational, practi
cal and situated, as suggested by Star and Ruhleder (1996), emphas
ises contextualised doing. Infrastructures are made, maintained and 
used. Illuminating this work requires studying infrastructures 'in the 
making' (Star & Bowker, 2003) and 'practicing "infrastructural in
version"-the foregrounding of aspects that warrant the functioning 
of infrastructure rather than those it invisibly supports' (Karasti, 
2014, p. 142). This aspect means that the infrastructure in and of 
itself is the focus of the study not the practitioners. This practice of 
infrastructure- infrastructuring-has supported the notions of the 
thing within participatory design and is congruent with the latter's 
understanding of both user, practitioner and material artefacts. In 
this section, I will attempt to highlight the aspect of doing that ma
kes participatory design's contribution to infrastructuring and the 
contribution of infrastructuring to participatory design. I will per
form this plan because the two practices are not interchangeable but 
congruent.
 
Before infrastructuring was introduced to PD, Ehn (1988a) 
wrote in Work Oriented Design of Computer Artefacts that 'A com

38
puter artifact is what it is being used for in practice. In designing 
computer artefacts, we should not primarily be concerned with me
eting technical specifications for efficient use, but with understan
ding conditions for the future user's readiness-to-hand in using the 
artefacts being designed' (p. 198). Ehn (1988a) wrote this in the 
80s, roughly two decades into participatory design history, and at 
a time when workplace democracy first and foremost referred to 
the industrial workplace. Participatory design emerged alongside 
the worker's movement in the 1960s and 1970s, drawing from the 
values of the-at the time-growing welfare state: The ideas of local 
knowledge production and the critical pedagogies of Freire (Frei
re, 1970; Kensing & Greenbaum, 2012) influenced Nygaard et al.'s 
collaboration with the Norwegian Metal Workers Union (NJMF) 
in 1970 (Ehn, 2017; Nygaard & Bergo, 1973). This work posed a 
radical alternative to the industrial design tradition of top-down de
sign, where designers would create tools, industrial shop floors and 
routines for the workers with minimal or no dialogue. In the indu
stries, with their lingering Fordism, the production line was divided, 
and each worker was responsible for only a small part of the whole, 
which is sometimes referred to as method-time management, and 
consequently often trapped in repetitive movements. Ehn and Sand
berg observed the working conditions of the Volvo factory floors 
in the 1970s and approached the factory as a social system. They 
saw management strategies that increased efficiency and lowered the 
necessary skill level of the workers. Less skilled workers are both 
cheap and easy to replace. The workers' movement, instead, sought 
to increase the workers' influence in their workplace, and many of 
participatory design's core principles are derived from their logic. 
PD asks, for example, if the users have decision power and 'does 
a design method, tool or process recognise and encourage partici
pants' abilities to learn' (Roberson & Wagner, 2013, p. 82).
6 Folkbildning is a Swedish term that lacks a direct English translation. It is 
used to describe a free "adult education", where the students are actively shap
ing the education, and where the goal is learning. The studies are not usually 
rewarded any formal merits as they are not tested or graded. 

39
 
Ties between the notion of mutual learning and democratic 
learning within PD and the Scandinavian welfare model of educa
tion, which is referred to as Folkbildning6 in Swedish (lit. A Peoples' 
Education), are noteworthy. It is an umbrella term that shields seve
ral initiatives for free education, often situated outside the academic 
paradigm. Honing to its ideological roots, folkbildning also draws 
from an unapologetically ideological theoretical frame-both peda
gogically (c.f. Freire, 1970) and through its position within the theo
ry of science (Wicks et al., 2008). By recognising and including the 
knowledge that rests outside academia, folkbildning challenges the 
conviction of one social truth and reveals other kinds of wisdoms (P. 
H. Collins, 2000), as well as multiple social realities. Within both 
PD and folkbildning, the notions of the 'expert'/teacher as the sole 
source of knowledge and as the only one with the authority to define 
the dichotomies between right or wrong and good or bad are clearly 
being challenged. However, this aspect also means that the conven
tional methods for generating knowledge (or scientific truths), which 
are typically seeking to be transparent, unbiased and replicable, may 
need to be exchanged for more democratic methods. Participatory 
design's democratic principles (Kensing & Greenbaum, 2012; Schu
ler & Namioka, 1993) seek to engage with the workplaces both 
practically, in terms of democratising the development of artefacts, 
and as a critical pedagogy of mutual learning. 
 
Democratic principles and notions of mutual learning are 
part of PD's contribution towards infrastructuring. Infrastructuring, 
in turn, has brought PD a vocabulary of distributed agency and 
plurality. The intersection between participatory design and infra
structuring has brought forward a 'bases for a politics of professi
onal design practice' (Suchman, 2002, p. 91). In Suchman's (2002) 
paper Located Accountabilities in Technology Production, she drew 
upon the feminist technoscience, which was mentioned in the pre
vious section of this chapter, that 'reframes the locus of objectivity 
from an established body of knowledge not produced or owned by 
anyone, to knowledges in dynamic production, reproduction and 

40
transformation, for which we are all responsible' (p. 92). The paper 
argued that the development of systems and devices means an entry 
into socio-material networks and that this aspect requires an entang
lement with the network's concepts and politics. Suchman (2002) 
discussed three positions for design: a view from nowhere, a de
tached intimacy and a located accountability. She drew on Haraway 
(1988) in her definition of located accountability as an embodied 
and responsible position: 'The only possible route to objectivity on 
this view is through collective knowledge of the specific locations 
of our respective visions. By extension, the only possibility for the 
creation of effective objects is through collective knowledge of the 
particular and multiple locations of their production and use' (Such
man, 2002, p. 96).
 
In this thesis, participatory design principles (Kensing & 
Greenbaum, 2012) have been the foundation for the design program
me, and infrastructuring has been treated both as a methodological 
approach and as a subject of enquiry. In the section above, I have 
sought to lay out the dual heritage of infrastructuring that not only 
makes this approach possible but also defensible. Infrastructuring is 
doing within a mess, and the only congruent way of engaging with 
such a doing is to get your hands dirty. Infrastructuring teaches prac
titioners to shift their focus from the artefact to the socio-material 
context. This feature is a sensitivity that requires constant attention: 
continuous labour rather than a one-time tick-box exercise. 
2.4 CHANGING NOTIONS OF PARTICIPATION WITHIN 
INFRASTRUCTURING
 
The meeting between participatory design and the STS no
tion of infrastructure occurred in the mid 90s. As a situated practice, 
it follows that those discourses around it have been affected by po
litical currents, technological developments, cultural exposure and 
economic tides. As a concept, infrastructuring has not only remained 
rigid with the changes of time but has also evolved. Over the past 

41
25 years, we have altered the way we think of networks and plat
forms (Lovink, 2019), and indeed, the very understanding of design 
has been reconfigured (Latour, 2008). Most notably, for the sake of 
this thesis, the shift of participatory design's early days of interven
tion from industry and industrial workers to engagements with the 
public sector and public sector workers has occurred (Bjögvinsson et 
al., 2012). In the last section, in this overview of infrastructuring, I 
will account for some of the theoretical and practical turns that have 
occurred over the past two decades in the disciple of participatory 
design. According to Walker (1989), disciplines 'can briefly be des
cribed as the ensemble of assumptions, concepts, theories, methods 
and tools employed by a particular group of scientists and scholars' 
(p. 1). In addition, mature disciplines more often than not have de
veloped a sense of self-awareness, which allows their practitioners 
and scholars to define it autonomously (Dilnot, 1984a). However, as 
Walker (1989) further points out, attempting to construct a unified 
and homogenous history of disciplines as young as the above-men
tioned design disciplines would be problematic: History always 
holds contradictory perspectives. As stated by Walker (1989): 
'[A]n analogy of map-making may be useful: several maps 
can be drawn of the same country each focuses upon diffe
rent features of the terrain. The various maps do not con
tradict one another, instead they complement one another. 
Taken together they provide a more complete account of the 
terrain than taken singly' (Walker, 1989. p. 2-3). 
 
The increasing industrial production afforded an upsurge of 
design disciplines during the mid-to-end of the 20th century, but the 
disciplines are tied to the mass production of objects more so than 
any of the others (Heskett, 1980). However, intrinsically tied to the 
market demands of capitalism, industrial designers quickly required 
specialisation. The discipline today may be viewed as an umbrella 
for several other more pointed disciplines. Löwgren and Stolterman 
(2007) defined interaction design as 'the shaping of use-oriented qu
alities of a digital artefact for one or more clients' (p. 2). At the time 

42
they pinned this sentence, interaction design had roughly two deca
des of digital artefacts. Prior to the 1970s, technology was not yet 
advanced enough to require a user-centric design. Technology only 
began to develop this feature when they entered the homes in the 
shape of computers, telephones and the Internet of Things (for an 
overview, see Whitmore et al., 2015). The very terms that Löwgren 
and Stolterman (2007) used to describe the discipline have also been 
some of their most controversial. Terms such as 'use-oriented' and 
'clients' have been continuously dissected since the early days of the 
discipline (Briefs et al., 1983). While the industrial designer of the 
early and mid- 20th century made a clear distinction between the 
stakeholders in the design process (i.e. designers, clients, customers), 
the present-day interaction designers may not do the same. The term 
and role of customer (although often still true) have been replaced 
by the term and role of user, borrowing its terminology from, for 
example, computer programming (Dittrich et al., 2013). The term 
user, in turn, has been divided into innumerable sub-sections and 
with more significance and consequence than merely marking words; 
the view of the user changes the implications of the design project 
in its entirety: 'Which humans and non-humans should be invited 
to participate in contemporary design things? Who invites? Who is 
marginalised or excluded? What issues should be dealt with? Which 
designarly [sic] and parliamentary technologies should be invoked 
in prototyping futures? If the computer is to become a controversial 
thing, is that a well-grounded end of the user?' (Dittrich et al., 2013, 
p. 8).
 
Arguably, the view and the role of the user divide the nume
rous sub-divisions of interaction design. The speculative design app
roach suggests that the only way to tackle the large, wicked problems 
(Buchanan, 1992; Rittel & Webber, 1973) of today's age is through 
a speculative approach, meaning that no simple single answer can 
be found, as they say, 'create spaces for discussion and debate about 
alternative ways of being' (Dunne & Raby, 2013, p. 2). This feature 
leaves the user in a speculative design context in the role remini
scent of an audience: taking in a curated experience and reflecting 
upon questions posed by the designer7. This concept is drawn upon 

43
a foundational understanding of the user as a so-called consumer ci
tizen. Dunne and Raby (2013) stated that 'Usually when we discuss 
big issues we do so as citizens, yet it is as consumers that we help 
these realities take shape' (Dunne and Raby, 2013. p. 49). Dunne 
and Raby (2013) argued that the agency resident in the user is loca
ted in the act of purchasing a design (as a consumer) and has hence 
identified her as a passive stakeholder in relation to the design, even 
in speculative/critical design's approach to cultural probes, which is 
a method that seeks activation to a larger extent than the speculative 
design described above. The cultural probes, in seeking activation of 
the user, also required further sensitivity: 'Understanding the local 
cultures was necessary so that our designs wouldn't seem irrelevant 
or arrogant, but we didn't want the groups to constrain our designs 
unduly by focusing on needs or desires they already understood. 
We wanted to lead a discussion with the groups toward unexpected 
ideas, but we didn't want to dominate it' (Gaver et al., 1999, p. 22). 
In this iteration, the user is a far-more central figure in the design 
process, both as a consideration of the designers and also as an acti
ve contributor. When the question is posed towards design research, 
the division is conventionally done to position oneself within the pa
rameters of research through, for or about design (Frayling, 1993). 
In terms of developing a more practice-oriented scale, the variables 
may stand between design for the sake of use and use for the sake of 
design. Speculative design, then, would land on the side of use for 
the sake of design.
 
Within participatory design, the notions of use-before-use 
and design-after-design both address the need to engage users beyond 
the conventional design process. As concepts, they seek to extend 
the design process into a long-term engagement with users. Use-be
fore-use seeks to engage users earlier in the process (Ehn et al., 2014; 
Redström, 2008), which in turn means opening the predetermined 
7 A similar critique has been expressed by Prado de O Martins (2014) in her 
feminist critique on speculative design. In this paper, she suggested that although 
the discipline often grounds its work in the tradition of critical theory and aspi
res towards a sociological critique of wicked problems-it falls short on challen
ging them. 

44
research question. Atelier (2011) stated that 'Participatory design 
becomes a way to meet the unattainable design challenge of fully 
anticipating or envisioning use before actual use takes place in pe
ople's life worlds' (p. 158). One of the city planners I met within the 
Amiralsstaden project remarked that citizen engagement was usual
ly done towards the end of a project. He said, 'Rather than asking 
what kind of area or house people would want to live in, or how 
they want to live, we turn to them [the residents] after the streets and 
houses have been designed and ask what colour they would want 
us to paint their staircase in' (Author's field notes, 2018). Engaging 
people in the question of what kind of city they want to live in in 
the future is a far messier process than asking them what colour they 
like. The same is true for use-before-use; a prolonged engagement 
that seeks to address matters of concern (Latour, 2005) is going to 
be messier rather than 'solve a problem' is going to be messier. 
2.4.1 THE DESIGN THING
 
What does 'messy' mean in this context? I would like to 
illustrate the 'messy' effects of use-before-use and design-after-de
sign by borrowing an argument from Human-Computer Interaction 
(HCI). HCI differentiates between 'standardised' and 'configurable' 
products. A standardised product is a generalised solution designed 
for universality. As such, standardised products often require you to 
buy several solutions to match your particular needs. An example 
from everyday life might be when you buy a pair of headphones 
from one brand that do not fit the headset jack of your phone of 
another brand, and so you are forced to buy an adapter to make 
the two work together. A standardised solution, however, is easy 
to mass produce, and at a lower price, it may be available to more 
people. By contrast, a configurable solution is left modifiable for 
the users to finish and may therefore be tailored more closely to the 
users' needs. This product requires more of the user, who must be 
in possession of the right skills (technical or otherwise), to 'finish' 
the solution. Participatory design, by entering into a process without 

45
predetermining the outcome, provides the participants with an un
finished, configurable project. This process requires more of each 
participant in terms of technical or practical skill, and with that fol
lows the responsibility of the designer to facilitate and support the 
participants8. Design-after-design urges the designer to consider this 
responsibility beyond the conventional 'develop and deliver' of the 
design 'project'. The notions of use-before-use and design-after-de
sign challenge the common design form as a 'project' (i.e. with a set 
beginning and end), and together, they bring us closer to what PD 
refers to as the design thing (Bjögvinsson et al., 2012; Ehn, 2008; 
Ehn et al., 2014; Telier, 2011): 
'[T]he ecology of the assembly or thing, traditionally refer
red to as a design project, and its networks and relations. 
Rather than the qualities of an environment, we here focus 
on the "agency" of designers, users, objects, artefacts, design 
devices and other "actants"-the very thing itself, rather 
than the design of things only seen as objects' (Telier, 2011, 
p. 157). 
 
PD, in congruence with STS, acknowledges the networked 
body, or socio-material assemblage, of any design project-termed 
the design thing (Telier, 2011), a phrase with its etymological roots 
in the Scandinavian ting and in the German dinge (Latour, 2005b). 
The Scandinavian and Germanic ting is a parliament (think, for ex
ample, of the Norwegian Stortinget) and historically a regional le
gal, political and organisational body spanning back to the Viking 
days. However, a ting can also mean an object or a thing, often one 
imbued with a special sentimental or historic meaning. In Old Nor
se, Old Frisian and Old English, the word is þing and translates most 
closely to an assembly. 
 
The ting, as a regional governing and organisational body, 
presented an alternative to using force, violence or blood feuds as a 
8 I will return to the responsibilities towards participants in a discussion 
around Ethics of Care in Chapter 5.

46
way of resolving differences in the Viking days. It was a meeting pla
ce where the attendants would leave their weapons outside the hall 
or circle before they entered. Alhness (2020) stated that 'In addition 
to having many distinctive organisational features for its time, some 
of the Viking ting assembly's defining features would be familiar 
to contemporary audiences, include community assembly, swearing
