in, validity of witnesses, and the dismissal of leaders by vote. The 
existence of these features at regional assemblies set the course for 
persisting regional power and the importance of participatory po
litics in society' (p. 2). The solutions produced by a ting were not 
intended to be universal, but they were intended for those that the 
ting assembled.
 
The conceptualisation of the design thing is radical in that 
it diverges from the conventional focus on the final design product 
to an emphasis on the process of designing (e.g. Storni et al., 2015). 
Infrastructuring, as understood with PD, further accentuates the re
lationality of the assemblages that constitute the process and stres
ses their temporal aspect, and finally, it recognises the agency of 
each socio-material actor within the constellation. The challenge of 
PD, therefore, is not to design innocently but to further connections 
between actors in a thing to continuously be designing together (c.f. 
Binder et al., 2015). Similar to the tings of yore, the design thing 
does not produce universal solutions but situated experiments, ac
counts and solutions. 
 
Latour (2018) described a shift in the field of design from a 
'relooking', where the design entails giving a new look to something 
or rebranding it. According to Latour (2008), '"Design" in this 
old and limited meaning was a way to redress the efficient but so
mewhat boring emphasis of engineers and commercial staff. Design 
occurred by adding a veneer of form9 to their creations, some su
perficial features that could make a difference in taste and fashion. 
9 Excluded from Latour's (2008) argument, but worth mentioning, is the 
Marxist theory of value production. Design does not merely add form to func
tion philanthropically, but does so as an investment to increase the products, 
surplus value. This theory means that the product does not cost more to make, 
but generates more profit (Marx, 1970).

47
Even if design could be greatly admired, it was always taken as one 
branch of an alternative: look not only at the function, but also at 
the design' (p. 1). From this perspective, Latour (2008) now sees 
design as something that has been 'extended from the details of daily 
objects to cities, landscapes, nations, cultures, bodies, genes and, as 
I will argue, to nature itself-which is in great need of being re-de
signed. It is as though the meaning of the word has grown in what 
logicians refer to as "comprehension" and "extension"' (p. 2). In
frastructuring within PD entails a drawing together (Latour, 1985) 
of assemblies and a long-term engagement with matters of concern 
(Latour, 2005) that shape and are shaped by the infrastructure. The 
attentive reader will have observed a paradox amongst the eight li
sted characteristics (Star & Ruhleder, 1996) of infrastructure above. 
The paradox, as observed by Neuman and Star (1996) in their paper 
Making Infrastructure: The Dream of a Common Language, is that 
the 'good infrastructure is transparent to use, yet good participatory 
design makes the problematics visible' (p. 231). The paper marked 
a merging between participatory design and infrastructure discour
ses and asked if the principles of participatory design can be app
lied to large-scale infrastructure projects. They highlighted both the 
multiplicity inherent in participant commitments as well as within 
object worlds (Bucciarelli, 1994), which is an idealised and reductio
nist world within which engineers operate through a simplified logic 
of how object worlds interact. Neuman and Star (1996) said, 
'Typically each engineer has his or her own specific interest 
in a particular, singular design problem or thing. This is not 
so in the world of building infrastructure. There is much 
more space between team members and between teams. The 
scope is greater, as it is bringing together the object worlds 
of many groups, embodying their standards and practices' 
(p. 235). 
 
Drawing on Watson-Verran (1998), Neuman and Star 
(1996) suggested that the way to bring together a multiplicity of 
object worlds is through flexibility: 'Imaginaries and metaphors are 

48
used to translate between object world views. The attempt to bring 
so many dispersed and varying views and foci together in one unified 
project necessitates a lack of specificity which is achieved through 
the shared imaginary or all the diverse views and groups cannot be 
accommodated' (p. 236).
2.4.2 MOVEMENTS: QUESTIONS OF CITIZENSHIP AND 
AGENCY WITHIN PARTICIPATORY DESIGN 
 
Being together, working together, collaborating, making to
gether and moving forward together: The imperative of bringing 
multiple worlds into a design thing is far from simple. Despite the 
fact that collaboration is part of the human condition, we collide 
with other people every day and in every aspect of our lives, and 
these are skills that we must practice-a repertoire we must develop 
and lessons we must learn more than once. Earlier in this text, I no
ted that infrastructuring is not a tick-box exercise but a continuous 
labour, and I would like to add that this is true for participatory de
sign at large. Therefore, undeniably, participatory design theory has 
come to deal with questions about democracy and democratisation. 
It was a formative part during the early discourses when participa
tory design primarily referred to the emancipation of industrial wor
kers (Ehn, 1988a), and it was also a key aspect of participatory de
sign, particularly when working within the public sector. The latter 
requires, in part, an understanding of citizenship. From sociology, 
we may borrow Marshall's (1950) argument that citizenship can be 
understood through the struggle for rights: social rights, civil rights, 
political rights and industrial rights. If we accept this categorisation 
of rights, we begin to find the loci (albeit, more often than not, over
lapping) for other perspectives of citizenship offered by, for example, 
issues of power and governance (e.g. Foucault, 1977); feminist per
spectives on the right to equal participation in the political sphere, 
the reproduction of the national subject (Scott, 2003) or the impe
rative of the personal-political divide (Lister, 2007); and decolonial 
perspectives on citizenship and borders (Sassen, 2002, as cited in 

49
Isin & Turner, 2002), the absence of citizenship (e.g. Joppke, 2002, 
as cited in Isin & Turner, 2002) or questions around integration and 
assimilation of immigrants (Scott, 2003). Barbalet (1988, p. 100) 
stated that social movements, which are reciprocally shaping and are 
being shaped by society, must be viewed as agents within themsel
ves. The struggle for industrial rights poses an interesting example, 
as the intersection between the struggle for social, political and ci
vil rights (Barbalet, 1988. p. 74) and the capitalistic market values 
of the industry is clear. Citizenship, such as democracy, is a living 
thing. Without struggle and movement, they stagnate and lose their 
meaning. Participatory design theory has drawn on Mouffe's (2000, 
2013) notion of agonism as a central part of the democratic process 
(e.g. Bjögvinsson et al., 2012). This aspect is where the Marxist un
derstanding of emancipation makes itself invaluable to agency. The 
isolated person with sensitised agency literacy may be emancipated 
and must also care for those in their surroundings-their agency and 
their agency literacy. Mobility is understood within this thesis as the 
key to Marx's (1970) understanding of value through accumulation, 
it is the key to the very notion of citizenship through the constant 
re-negotiation of rights (Balibar, 2015; Barbalet, 1988), and it is the 
key to democracy through agonism (Mouffe, 2000, 2013). This Ph. 
D. suggests that participatory design's claim to democratization lies 
in its ability to generate sparks of momentum through meetings and 
necessary agonism (Mouffe, 2000). The issue of agency comes to the 
forefront here, as it speaks to the relational issues between participa
tory designers and participants, as well as to a lack of agency within 
public sector work. In the neoliberal labour market, industrial rights 
are not as readily defined by the shop floor as they once were. To 
initiate movement within such processes, for example, within the 
public sphere, it can be criticised as an illegitimate action of topdown governance (Dahlstedt, 2008). However, as Balibar (2015, p. 
93) argued, all political interventions may be deemed illegitimate. 
The question raised earlier in this chapter, 'What rights do we have 
as participatory designers to make interventions into 'alien' situa
tions?', becomes relevant again. Furthermore, does participation in 
and of itself give voice to participants and does it give equal voice to 

50
participants (Björgvinsson & Keshavarz, 2020)? PD, as well as dis
courses around infrastructuring, has been considered to hold a com
mitment towards recognising, amplifying and mobilising the mar
ginal voices within a given constellation (Bjögvinsson et al., 2012) 
and does so-in contrast to ANT-through an active political enga
gement with the given situation. ANT seeks to understand agency, 
whereas participatory design seeks to redistribute it. The issue of 
agency within participatory design understood here as the political 
and ethical dimension of active political engagements is the central 
focus of this Ph. D. thesis (see chapter 4). It asks: What does it mean 
to step into a situation-and what does it mean to refrain-to step 
back? In addition, we, as designers and academics, have an undeni
able privilege stemming from our paid positions, which is the inhe
rent power position as decision-makers and intervenors, indicating 
that being able to look away and refrain from action is imperative 
and an equal privilege. The feminist techno-scientific tradition has, 
as mentioned, reminded us that to critique power structures is not 
sui generis the same as challenging them (Martin, 2014). This thesis 
will explore sensitisation as a way of being within research, termed 
affective infrastructuring here, and will look specifically at the no
tion of anecdotalization (Michael, 2012a) as reflexive and inventive 
research to discuss voice, representation and mutual learning in the 
participatory design process. Affective infrastructuring should be 
read here as an added dimension of democratic design experiments 
(Binder et al., 2015).
2.4.3 DEMOCRATIC DESIGN EXPERIMENT 
 
In a paper from 2015 entitled Democratic Design Experi
ments: Between Parliament and Laboratory, Binder et al. (2015) 
responded to the question of where we draw the line between de
sign-as-making and design implementations. Should the two be 
separate (Bødker et al., 2004) or should we, for example, opt for 
open-ended implementations to encourage user appropriation (Hen
derson & Kyng, 1991; Telier, 2011)? In this paper, infrastructuring 

51
is described as 'an alternative to prototyping spanning both design 
and implementation' (Binder et al., 2015, p. 158) and is further de
fined as follows:
'What is foregrounded in participatory design is the evolving 
practices of collaboration, but at the expense of an appa
rent opacity of both the object of design and the agency that 
this object may unleash. Read through the lens of ANT the 
practices of participatory design may well be seen as labora
tories experimentally enacting networked practices of repre
sentation similarly to the scientific laboratories so delicately 
studied by ANT scholars. In participatory design as in the 
practices of scientific laboratory work, we will find permea
ble boundaries between the inside and the outside of the la
boratory, and we will also find knowledge and agency pro
duced through an on-going circulation of references (Latour, 
1987 op. cit.) that do not respect any clear-cut demarcation 
between the experiment and its effects'. (Binder et al., 2015, 
p. 158, as cited in Latour, 1987)
Participatory design is described here as an experimental practice 
of participation (Binder et al., 2015)-of drawing things together 
(Latour, 1985). The design thing evokes matters of concern, and 
the paper describes this as forming publics to unfold controversies, 
which may 'invigorate citizenship as a productive figure of agency in 
collaborative design' (Binder et al., 2015, p. 160). 
 
This thesis seeks to investigate this concept of how we may 
'invigorate citizenship' in collaborative design processes, particularly 
in the intersection between the civil and public sectors. In this chap
ter, I have sought to provide the reader with a number of building 
blocks that are necessary to follow the investigation. The chapter 
has accounted for the notion of an 'infrastructure' before describing 
the verb 'infrastructuring' as the practice of mobilising socio-mate
rial infrastructures over time. The terms, as they will be used in this 
thesis, stem from a hybrid heritage, from STS, feminist technoscien
ce, participatory design and more. This aspect may leave the term 

52
somewhat open to interpretation, and as such, I own a great dept to 
Star and Ruhleder (1996) for daring to offer us a very concrete de
finition of the characteristic of an infrastructure. It gives those who 
wish to discuss infrastructuring a solid foundation to depart from, at 
times to reinforce and at times to challenge. In the following chap
ters of this thesis, I will advocate the importance of care work within 
infrastructuring and contribute a discussion of affect as a force that 
not only creates alignments but also breaks them. 

53

54
3. THE PROGRAMME
 
Democratic design experiments (Binder et al., 2015) form a star
ting point for what I will describe below as my programme. Design, I 
deduced, is a capacity and a potency, and how we choose to wield it 
is an inherently political question. Furthermore, the design disciplines 
have historically navigated this political question of design through ca
pitalist logic, which determines the values of good design. As the do
minant ideology during these past centuries, capitalist logic may, to 
the naked eye, seem like a neutral position, whereas in reality, no such 
thing exists. Every action taken in the world holds consequences, and 
arguably we, as designers, have wielded design to become a key actor 
in the overconsumption that threatened to ruin our world (Papanek, 
1985) and graphic design as a motor in the materialistic apparatus of 
advertising and social media (Julier, 2008). Social innovation has also 
become an intricate part in the white-washing of capitalist injustices and 
wrongdoings. Furthermore, while examples of grassroot-based innova
tion work certainly exists10, these factors are arguably the exception to 
the rule (Costanza-Chock, 2020; Kanes Weisman, 1992). Many, for ex
ample, Costanza-Chock (2020) and DiSalvo (2012, 2022) have argued 
that the sociotechnical foundation of democracy has been designed, that 
10 Defining a 'good' radical project is of course highly subjective. DiSalvo 
(2012) listed a number of projects that fulfilled his criteria of projects that 'stri
ve to investigate an issue and raise questions concerning that issue' (p. 9), such 
as the Million Dollar Blocks (USA). Costanza-Chock (2020) praised several 'al
ly-organizations'- making a, for this thesis, noteworthy distinction between the 
activist organisations and design research bodies that seek to support them, such 
as the Inclusive Design Research Centre. 

55
is, the policy, election systems and the very codes that make up our 
information networks have been crafted. 
 
In summary, at the beginning of this Ph.D. project, I was 
conflicted about whether doing anything truly radical and political 
is possible through or with design that would not be mere upholding 
the status quo or in a manner that could be deemed 'problematic'. 
At the same time, the idea of refraining completely from acting or 
doing in a world with so much injustice seemed the most problema
tic of all. Something must be done, but how? 
 
Democratic design experiments (opt. cit) in particular and 
participatory design in general became my answer to that question. 
Breaking it down word-by-word, we might say that experimenta
tions, rather than production, brought out the best within design: 
the process of learning, exploring and knowledge generating. The 
democratic aspiration gave me an ideological counter-position to 
capitalist market logic. Democratic design told me that something 
must be done, and interventions be made, but not for my own sake 
or for that of a client, but rather through a long-term engagement 
with people and issues. 
 
These ideals have guided my research approach, methods, 
choice of case studies and theories. They were the beginning of my 
design programme. Programmatic design research (Brandt et al., 
2011) is characterised by the fact that it does not set out to an
swer any one specific question, but rather works iteratively with a 
framework and a worldview11. The programme should be open to 
'explorations, surprises and new insights' (Agger Eriksen, 2012, p. 
70; Brandt et al., 2011, p. 37). 
 
Design research is still a relatively new field within the sci
ences, and the term 'design research' still raises a lot of questions 
11 Research programmes are not unique to design research, for example, Laka
tos' (1968-1969) delineation of the Popper-Kuhn controversy, and how scien
tific paradigms have change with worldviews. Lakatos (1968-1969) wrote 'My 
concept of a "research-programme" may be construed as an objective, "thirdworld" reconstruction of Kuhn's concept of "paradigm": thus the Kuhnian "Ge
stalt-switch" can be performed without removing one's Popperian spectacles" (p. 
182).

56
(as well as eyebrows). What does the 'practice' in practice-based 
actually mean, for example? Programmatic design research is tailo
red to more closely reflect the practice of designers, where the desig
ner gains an understanding of their 'problem' (or solutions) through 
experiments and prototypes. The scope of a design programme, 
however, cannot be too large, according to Redström (2017). A 
scope that holds a question that is too large, he says, will render 
the programme impotent. For example, a research question that is 
too abstract will be unanswerable with scientific standards, and a 
design programme that holds too much will not be able to generate 
anything. In framing my starting position as investigating the issue 
of agency in participatory design, I have certainly fallen close to 
what could be deemed 'too large'. In my first two years, I was struck 
by the endlessness of agency as a sociological (taking heed of the 
tension between power as a dialectic between structural and indivi
dual agencies), political and organisational concept, that is, agency 
being everything and nothing at the same time. I am considering 
the issue of accountability, as a researcher, towards other research 
participants. It is a dilemma not unknown to action researchers, as 
you set certain expectations if/when you introduce yourself to the 
other research participants as a 'researcher'. The role of a researcher 
may, on the one hand, legitimate your presence (Whyte, 1991, p. 
163), but it may, on the other hand, influence the behaviour of the 
other participants or lead them to expect a particular output of the 
project. As such, it was not always me who struggled to stay with 
the open-ended research approach-sometimes other participants 
attempted to narrow my research focus for me. I am not accusing 
them of doing this maliciously, but likelier, this approach is simply 
what is expected from research and researchers. As suggested by 
Stengers (2018), science has traditionally opted for narrower ques
tions, and the public understanding of science is that it 'allows pro
blems to be "well-posed" and therefore amenable to being given 
the "right solutions"' (p. 11). A too broad question is considered 
'spreading oneself too thin', as a rigorous 'right' conclusion cannot 
be drawn. Stengers (2018) further argued that narrow scientific in
quiries are favoured in today's society, as they are more productive 

57
and readily managed. While Stengers' (2018) argument is congruent 
with the methodology that I have been working with, it still pre
sented a challenge of accountability towards other research partici
pants: I had to work very carefully to include them in defining the 
scope of the research. This was done, at first-during what I refer 
to as the open approach (described below)-by positioning the rese
arch as much in relation to the key elements of the programme (i.e. 
agency, infrastructuring and participatory design) as to the context 
(i.e. Malmö, the public sector, and city planning processes). As such, 
the other participants were part of narrowing down the focus with 
me and paddling the programme forward rather than doing it for me 
(or, indeed, me doing it over their heads)12. Within the experimental 
approach, I worked with collaborative anecdotalization as a method 
to meet this challenge. 
 
The initial scope has been informed, reinforced and/or chal
lenged by an inventive participatory methodology and the people 
and situations that it came into contact with during the field work. 
This dialogic development of theory and practice that has occurred 
between the scope and the programme has made infrastructuring 
relevant in this project as both a method and a research subject. 
Allowing my role within the projects to remain reflexive (Lury & 
Wakeford, 2012) and to change according to the projects' and my 
fellow participants' needs was necessary to initially keep the rese
arch open-ended. As my role as a researcher and the way I have 
related to the projects have changed over the years, the research has 
also generated diverse results. These ways of relating to the research 
contexts will be described in the subsequent sections of this chapter 
as 'approaches': an open approach, an experimentative approach 
and a reflective approach. Each approach offers a perspective on the 
issue of agency within infrastructuring, and each approach has gene
rated a programmatic answer. In the process of doing fieldwork the 
three perspectives have often been overlapping and blurred, but for 
12 In section 5.2.2 of this thesis, I will elaborate on the ethics of this work and 
refer to it as an ethics of care through case-by-case deliberations. 

58
the purpose of this thesis the open approach generated the program
matic answer to the 'what' perspective, affective infrastructuring; the 
experimental approach generated the more methodological 'how' 
within collaborative anecdotalization; and the reflective approach 
the 'why' of feral infrastructuring. 
 
The term programmatic answers is used to describe the out
come or conclusion drawn from the programme, or in this particular 
case, the answers are three-fold in direct correlation with the app
roaches. The programmatic answers must be read as a call-and-re
sponse output of the research programme's 'how', 'what' and 'why' 
approach, that is, an output congruent with the programme's open 
and experimentative methodology.
 
The components that make up the programme are as fol
lows: three approaches, three perspectives on agency in the infra
structuring process and three programmatic answers. The case stu
dies and experiments consist of the three approaches, which brought 
momentum to the programme. By approach, I suggest a methodo
logical turn that has affected my roles within the projects and in
terventions I have participated in. Krogh et al. (2015) articulated 
this notion of the researcher's presence as ways of 'drifting', that is, 
ways that the researcher is learning from findings and continuous
ly adjusting her approach accordingly. Each approach has its own 
'way of drifting'. It is an articulation of the research process, which 
hones Schön's (1982) definition of being in the 'swamp' and makes 
sense of it through tangible interventions and a dialogue with the 
situation. By moving through these 'swamps', insights have been 
generated, and concepts have been developed. The process has been 
informed by a constant dialogue with the world outside the pro
gramme: through literature and theoretical input, through the for
malised venues for feedback within the Ph. D. project (most nota
bly my milestone seminars at 30%, 50% and 90%), Ph. D. courses 
and conversations with my supervisors and with practitioners in my 
field; all these factors have been formative for the development of 
the programme.

59
 
In this chapter, I will first provide an overview of the pro
gramme's socio-geographic context. Second, I will deconstruct the 
programme and account for the programme's three methodological 
approaches. Third, I will then introduce the primary case studies, 
one by one, with which the methodological approaches are engaged. 
Finally, I will elaborate on the programmatic answers.
3.1 AN OVERVIEW OF THE PROGRAMME'S SOCIO-
GEOGRAPHICAL CONTEXT
 
The years between 1945 and 1986 have been referred to 
as välfärdsåren-the welfare years in the history of Sweden (Hägg, 
2019). It was an era of economic growth, but was first and foremost 
remembered as an era when the living standards improved signifi
cantly for the working class. The notion of Folkhemmet was a broad 
term that encapsulated a nation of equality as the Social Democratic 
Party, led as they were by Per Albin Hansson, who sought to build 
it. Folkhemmet literally translates to 'home of the people' and was 
a socio-political change to the structures of society put into practice 
through a number of social reforms introduced between 1930 and 
1950 (Hellberg, forthcoming). Planned housing and an expansion 
of the public sector were some concrete initiatives taken during this 
time that shaped the welfare state. Today, Folkhemmet holds a cer
tain nostalgia for many groups in Sweden as a time when the nation 
and the working class bloomed. Examples of this could certainly 
be given; however, although the era was socially progressive, it was 
never perfect: many groups were excluded from Folkhemmet13. 
 
If we look at professional appurtenance, the working class 
13 To exemplify this aspect, Hellberg's (forthcoming) doctoral dissertation outli
ned the marginal position women held within Folkhemmet, in particular in their 
roles as mothers. Molina's (1997) doctorial dissertation studied the housing seg
regation and institutional racism that was prevalent within Folkhemmet, and the 
doctoral dissertation of Runcis (1998) covered the notion of racial purity enac
ted through sterilisations on medical, racial hygiene and moral grounds during 
the 1930s and 1940s.

60
has diminished in Sweden since the 1970s, in part because many 
industries have moved their production abroad (Ahrne et al., 2021) 
and because the public sector has grown to become one of Sweden's 
leading workplaces. In Malmö, the number of people employed by 
the municipality grew from 16,000 to 33,000 between 1965 and 
1980 (Holgersen, 2017). The city that was once an industrial port, 
defined by a large working class, became a post-industrial city that 
sought to market itself as a 'knowledge city' (through the opening 
of Malmö University and through the proximity to the prominent 
Lund University), with a brand of sustainability and 'green thinking' 
(ibid.). The city is rapidly growing, with a population of 351,749 
inhabitants as of writing this thesis in 2017, to a predicted half a 
million inhabitants in 2047. 
 
Gramsci (1971) once described a crisis as a state in which 
the old is dying and the new has yet to be born. The remnants of the 
welfare years in Malmö, such as the Million Programme14, have been 
left waning, and many are today in need of significant upkeep. This 
phenomenon reflects a general trend in Sweden, a trend that coinci
ded with the general dismantling of the welfare state. Increasingly, 
private corporations have been moving into areas, such as housing, 
schools and healthcare-matters that were previously public, state 
owned and the responsibility of the public sector. At the same time, 
new efforts, such as new affordable housing, are necessary to keep 
up with the growing population and growing social segregation 
(Baeten et al., 2017)-a task that is increasingly left to neoliberal 
housing actors but contested by many activist groups (Listerborn 
et al., 2020). Simultaneously, the transition from the nation-state 
project towards the post-nation network society has left us in a sta
te of democratic deficit: a shift from governments to governance 
14 Miljonprogrammet or the Million Programme was a public housing pro
gramme initiated by the Swedish Social Democratic Party in 1964. With the am
bition that every person in Sweden should be able to live in acceptable standards 
to a reasonably low cost, 100 000 homes were built over the course of a decade. 
The houses came with a vision of the modern 'good citizen', and to some, this 
initiative has been seen as a part of the welfare model, offering an alternative 
to social housing. Others have criticised the programme for laying the founda
tion for some of Sweden's most vulnerable and segregated areas, especially since 
many of the houses were sold to private actors.

61
Fig. 1. Photo from Malmö street (August 2022) "No collaborators on our streets".

62
(Abrahamsson, 2016). Issues around citizenship and democracy are 
placed in a new light due to these transitions. 
 
Malmö, in the first decade of the 21st century, was a frag
mented and segregated city (Ek et al., 2015 Hansen, 2019; Har
ju & Sjölander, 2019; Listerborn et al., 2014). Large socio-econo
mic rifts occur between the more affluent areas of the city, such as 
Västra hamnen, and the most deprived areas, such as Rosengård 
and Amiralsstaden (Holgersen & Baeten, 2016; Listerborn et al., 
2014). These rifts are made quantifiable by looking at the quality 
of schools, houses and infrastructure. Furthermore, while the whole 
city has on occasion been branded as hot-spot for leftists, radical 
feminists, LGBTQs and solidaric young(ish) activists, this aspect is 
mostly true in specific areas, such as Möllan15, and notably not in 
the most socio-economic disadvantaged areas. Some have argued 
that the strong branding of Malmö as a solidaric community can at 
times be excluding (Hansen, 2019) from society's more marginali
zed groups. Malmö Stad has gradually and most notably, since the 
publication of the official guiding document for city policy by the 
Commission for a Socially Sustainable Malmö (Stigendal & Öster
gren, 2013), introduced citizen engagement into their work. This 
strategy lessens segregation and is not only unique to Malmö but 
also a general trend in Sweden (Wiberg, 2016).
 
 
The groups that I have come in contact with through my 
field work are not the 'usual suspects' of activists and those other
wise politically engaged. The people my case studies have sought to 
engage with are not the ones you will usually find at a rally or people 
who have experienced organizing. Participatory designers at Malmö 
University have been working in the blur of citizens, private com
panies, the public domain and academia (Björgvinsson et al., 2010) 
and finding ways to revitalise democratic practices since the early 
15 Malmö, as a whole, tends to vote red, with a majority for the Social De
mocratic Party, and an above average support for the parties further on the left, 
such as the Left Party and previously also Feminist Initiative. 

63
1990's, notably through the Malmö Living Labs (Ehn et al., 2014), 
which have gained traction in the participatory design community.
3.2.1 APPROACH A: THE OPEN APPROACH 
 
In the early stages of this Ph.D., I was able to enter into 
my first two case studies with an open-ended approach and was 
fortunate enough to be welcomed without immediately having to 
'declare myself' or define my purpose and role at the offset. This 
style allowed me to take a grounded approach to my research; to 
get to know the people, the matters of concern and the context of 
the projects I took part in; and to tailor my methods and my role 
accordingly. The primary case studies that I took part in were Ami
ralsstaden and Livskonceptet (see chapter 3.2); however, arguably, 
this open-ended approach has been used within at the launch of 
most, if not all, of the projects and cases of this Ph. D. As a way of 
drifting, I used an accumulative and expansive method (Krogh et 
al., 2015) here, gathering the data with the intent of broadening and 
opening the research programme. This approach diverges from the 
definition of design research as being 'problem-solving', as it was 
rather inventive problem-making (Wilkie, 2014). When working 
with infrastructuring, intricate structures must be checked before 
introducing interventions and experiments. As Star and Ruhleder 
(1996) suggested, one characteristic of the infrastructure is that it is 
learned as part of a membership: 'strangers and outsiders encounter 
infrastructure as a target object to be learned about. New partici
pants acquire a naturalised familiarity with its objects as they be
come members' (p. 113). Thus, the open approach became shaped 
by participation more than anything else-by becoming part of the 
operations in both formal and informal aspects before proceeding. 
This approach was informed by applied qualitative research prac
tices, such as ethnography and grounded theory (Charmaz, 2014; 
Glaser & Strauss, 1967). 
 
Grounded theory proposes an inductive approach to resear
ch. It encourages the researcher to go out into the wild and follow 
the data rather than merely testing a pre-proposed hypothesis (Gla
ser & Strauss, 1967; Hartman, 2001). As such, the theory produced 

64
is always grounded and to some extent situated. Grounded theory 
is usually conducted through three research phases: an open pha
se, a selective/experimental phase and a theoretical/literary phase. 
The purpose of the open phase, according to grounded theory, is 
to transport the research from its initial unrefined stage to the first 
checkpoint of coding: open coding (Glaser, 1978). Within this Ph. 
D., the open approach presented an opportunity to enter into the 
situation of the research without either a pre-determined role or a 
preferred outcome. Instead, these outcomes were developed in dia
logue with the people, places and situations that I work with. With 
the open approach, I documented my path through memos in accor
dance with grounded theory and at three points (i.e. October 2017, 
February 2018 and June-September 2018), I took a step back to go 
through the notes. While this helped me induce a 'next step' from 
the gathered data, it also highlighted the limitations of grounded 
theory within a design research framework. By the very nature of a 
design research programme, I had not originally set out with a hy
pothesis to test and as such, rounded theory can provide me with a 
route map of sorts. A scaffolding for the research design suggested 
how to move from an open approach to a generated theory. In as 
much the phases of grounded theory were an inspiration, but by the 
end of the open phase, I felt restrained. Design research and pro
grammatic design research are generative, and my particular subject 
area places an emphasis on collectivity and participation. Grounded 
theory is not designed to be a collective effort, nor is it constructive 
in the same way as design can be considered constructive. In ad
dition, grounded theory asks that the researcher to enter into the 
project blank and does not steer the process towards an outcome 
deliberately, which stands in direct contrast with the worldview at 
the core of the design research programme (Redström, 2017). Ac
cording to Johan Redström (2017): 
'[W]e want to look for support structures that somehow ca
ter to the need of a worldview, a hard core, but do so in a 
more nonprotective way. This could, for instance, happen 
by making sure there are always competing worldviews ad
vocated by other such support structures, and we make sure 
that the activities that unfold within our structure constantly 

65
challenge this worldview, searching for its breaking points 
rather than its comfort zones' (p. 95). 
As such, the second approach became an iteration of the open app
roach, and it was revised by moving towards explicit experimen
tation rather than mere observations. The experimental approach 
also borrowed more epistemologically from participatory action 
research (Whyte, 1991) than from grounded theory as a necessary 
consequence of the researcher occupying more space in the work. 
Imperatively, the responsibility of the 'good research' of 'good data' 
was placed upon who gathered the data and not on the research 
participants. 
 
Documentation and reflection were also imperative during 
the open approach. I used both day-to-day memo writing and weekly 
reflective summaries during my first year. On two occasions during 
the first year, I coded and analysed these notes into clusters (using 
the data analysis software Nvivo), searching for emerging themes 
and congruent theories. During the first year, I also conducted seven 
semi-structured interviews (within the Amiralsstaden, MIA and Livs
konceptet, as shown below) and facilitated five reflective workshops 
within Amiralstaden. The interviews were held primarily to afford 
me one-to-one time with the actors who were taking on organising 
and infrastructuring roles within the projects. The interviews were 
between 40 minutes and an hour long and were recorded for do
cumentation purposes. The questions were rule explanatory rather 
than critical and sought to encourage the interviewees to reflect on 
their own role within the project. However, the two interviews were 
different, which had an element of mapping timelines (see Fig. 2) 
and took on a more critical nature.
 
The timeline map shown in Fig. 2 is the result of an inven
tory that I together with one of the project's main organisers - Anja 
- conducted at the beginning of the Amiralsstaden project. We had 
been reading through the project description and found that many 
directives seem to come 'out of the blue', and we thought that if we 
could trace why the project had been given those directives, it would 
be easy to follow them. Thus, we went back over all documents av
ailable to us that contained previous iterations of the Amiralsstaden 

66
Fig. 2. Timeline mapping, material from interview (March 2018). 

67
project or adjacent projects. From this, we constructed a timeline, 
as the one seen in the print, of how-according to official documen
tation-the ideas behind our Amiralsstaden (2017-2019) had emer
ged. This scheme somewhat helped us, but to be safe, we ran it with 
one of the people involved with several projects on our map and 
asked her if she recognised the timeline. The handwritten notes on 
the map show her 'corrections'. Only with the involvement of the 
official timeline and the timeline of the public sector worker could 
we decipher the project's directions. The reason that I am telling this 
story here and now is because it is symptomatic of the open phase, in 
that we tentatively went forward, not always sure of what we were 
looking for. However, I am also telling this story because I think it il
lustrates something important about working with the municipality. 
As an organisation, the 'embodiment of standards' and 'links to con
ventions of practice' are certain, as Star and Ruhleder (1996) descri
bed the infrastructure. As we saw in the official documentation, an 
'official' story was told; however, a turn of events was experienced 
by someone who had been a part of the project. Furthermore, these 
things were not always aligned. The informal, messy side of the pro
ject work was something that the open approach sought to follow 
and very carefully illuminate. 
 
Reflective workshops were held within the first year within 
the projects Amiralsstaden, MIA and Livskonceptet. These reflective 
workshops sought to encourage collective sense-making within the 
respective groups. The groups had 6-23 participants. For two of the 
workshops, I brought in games as a venue for discussion, such as the 
reflective brick game seen in the image below. Within some projects, 
such as Amiralsstaden, the reflective workshops afforded the partici
pants, who came from highly diverse backgrounds (i.e. some present 
in a professional capacity but from various professional disciplines, 
others in a personal capacity; some were residents in the area and 
knew it well, others had never set foot in it; some were from dif
ferent social and economic backgrounds), a chance to rehearse a 
common language. Lave and Wenger (1998), as mentioned in the 
previous chapter, described how communities of practice develop a 
shared vocabulary and repertoire. These reflective workshops were 
a way for us to encourage this convergence. 

68
Fig. 3-4.Game designed for a reflective workshop within the 
Malmö Innovation Arena (2017)

69
 
The open approach provided me insight into numerous eve
ryday situations, routines and the strategies that infrastructuring 
practitioners used to navigate. Approaching agency through this 
lens brought affect to my attention. While at first glance, factors, 
such as protocols, socio-material connections and routines, appear 
to dominate the practitioners' actions and abilities to act, but a 
strong affective plane also occurs in their directions. This plane was 
present on an individual level but occurs even more on a collective 
level. The open approach afforded me the opportunity to observe 
how infrastructuring practitioners interact with their surroundings; 
however, notably, I was albe to do the labour of an infrastructuring 
practitioner by following them. As Star and Ruhleder (1996) sug
gested, labour is mostly visible when things break down one way 
or another. I began seeing the management of affect as one of the 
labours performed by infrastructuring practitioners, an observation 
that eventually grew into my first programmatic answer: affective 
infrastructuring. Both the concepts of affective infrastructuring and 
feral infrastructures (see 3.2.3) are perspectives on infrastructuring 
that not only help an infrastructuring practitioner understand the 
infrastructure(s) around them but also to navigate their process.
3.2.2 APPROACH B: THE EXPERIMENTATIVE APPROACH
 
While I found the open research approach and its role, the 
beginning of the projects was rewarding, but it eventually became 
saturated. After some time (in some cases, particularly in small pro
jects, the period was days or weeks, and in the case of large projects, 
such as Amiralsstaden, it was over a year), the learning curve began 
to flatten, and limited new theories emerged. The experimentative 
approach became an iteration of the first approach-a continuance. 
This approach served to step into some of the emerging theories 
that formed during the initial open approach. Understandably, seve
ral ideas and theories that emerged were proven to be false, insub
stantial or irrelevant. However, the notion of affective infrastructure 
was consistently reinforced, and I began experimenting with ways 
to work with the affective tiers of infrastructure. As another way 
of being within research, or a way of drifting, the experimentative 

70
approach was probing. Krogh et al. (2015) described probing as 'a 
personal motivation and engagement in the research pursuit, where 
the research activities are points of impact in a research field larger 
than what a single research project can be expected to cover' (p. 10). 
Probing is about exploring emergent design ideas within their situa
tion. 
 
Concretely, the experimental approach brought about lar
ge interventions, such as the Demokratiambassadörerna project, or 
simply guiding and probing workshops, such as future workshops. 
My role within the projects became one that facilitates, initiates and 
drives, as opposed to the more 'passive' role(s) of the open approach. 
Participatory action research (PAR) became a principal influence 
on the experimentative approach, as it distinguished itself from the 
open approach by being more hands-on and intervening. Probing, 
after all, means that the researcher is looking for something, steering 
away from or towards. It is not passive. PAR holds a commitment to 
this presence and seeks to make the conflicting roles of researchers 
transparent. The researcher is not just a researcher with a sole focus 
on the soundness of data and knowledge; she is also an individual 
with the lived experiences of class, race, sex and cultural heritage. 
She is also a citizen, affected by the same society as the one she is 
researching. PAR intends to build participation in the process of the 
research, aiming to further that process. The underlying assumption 
here is that social research 'exists to assist society in solving social 
problems' (Greenwood et al., 1993, p. 176), and this concept can be 
achieved through both ongoing mutual learning within the process 
of research and the changes the project sets provide. In contrast to 
the conventional model of research, where the researcher takes on 
the role of an expert, commenting, evaluating the site or even defi
ning it (Law, 2004; Whyte, 1991), PAR argues that 'science is not 
achieved by distancing oneself from the world' (Whyte, 1991, p. 
21). A parallel may be drawn from PAR engagement with the world 
to DiSalvo's (2015) distinction between design for politics and poli
tical design: 
'The distinction between politics and the political shapes the 
purposes of adversarial design, and highlights the difference 
between design for politics and political design. Politics are 

71
the means by which an organization, municipality, or state is 
put and held together. Politics are a series of structures and 
mechanisms that enable governing. These range from laws 
and regulations to unspoken but observed habits of interper
sonal interactions and performances of beliefs and values. 
Different from these means, the political is a condition of li
fe-a condition of ongoing contest between forces or ideals'. 
(DiSalvo, 2015, p. 8)
 
Engaging with the political often means that what we enga
ge with are sensitive questions, infected issues or matters-of-concern 
that have a direct impact on the well-being of one or more groups. 
The tension of knowing when to step in and when to step back 
in a collaborative project became more prominent within the ex
perimental approach, as I deliberately 'took up space'. By asking 
questions relevant to my research programme-questions that were 
often wicked questions (Buchanan, 1992; Rittel & Webber, 1973), 
which are large and complex for the project present-I challenged 
and sometimes strained my relationship with the other participants. 
The other participants already had a trajectory, a process, which 
they were working on. They had objectives to fulfil and responsibili
ties of their own. Within Amiralsstaden, we started asking ourselves 
the question 'who participates in who's process' (Smedberg, 2019): 
A question that was relevant internally within the group, as well as 
in relation to the broader public(s) in the area where we operated. 
To be able to propose preferred new directions or actions within a 
group without abandoning the care work I had performed as a ma
intaining infrastructurer, I needed to develop sensitivity.
 
The PAR methodology, as well as the participatory design 
methodology, suggests that the concerned parties have enough re
asons; in particular, the marginalised voices should take up more 
space. Both the PAR methodology and the PD methodology have 
been inspired by Freire's (1970) pedagogies. This shift brought some 
practical changes, for example, to the methods of documentation, 
which became more impact-oriented than reflective; however, first 
and foremost, it brought changes to the way I related to other par
ticipants within the project. I needed a sensitivity towards my own 
position within the network, and such a sensitivity, I realised, was 

72
Fig. 5.Entrance to Amiralsstaden venue, after the glass door had been broken, and replaced 
with a wooden board. The board became a canvas for some of the project's more creative 
participants (2019).

73
impossible to develop on my own. As in Freire's (1970) methodolo
gy, I needed to find a reflexive way of relating to the other research 
participants. This scheme inspired the second programmatic answer 
of this Ph.D. process: collaborative anecdotalization as a method 
for dialogue involving the whole range from speaking, listening and 
co-creation. Collaborative anecdotalization as a method was explo
red within the project Demokratiambassadörerna as a way of re
hearsing sensitivity together. 
3.2.3 APPROACH C: THE REFLECTIVE APPROACH 
 
The third and final approach of the research programme 
encapsulates both of the abovementioned approaches. The third 
approach, the reflective approach, holds a higher level of abstrac
tion than the previous two, as it deals with the most intangible of 
the three methodologies. The reflective approach has no dedicated 
period, but is present throughout the fieldwork and synthesises the 
work that followed it. 
 
I participated in many different case studies, projects and 
processes over several years, and while many of these had socio-ma
terial overlaps, the empirical data was still disparate. Through the 
open and experimental approaches, I gathered insights, tensions, 
strategies and anecdotes and included them in the research program
me. However, empirical data do not naturally generate theories or 
even make sense by itself. A process can tie matters together and find 
connections and congruences. This process is a sense-making labour, 
which I also approached within the framework of collaborative 
anecdotalization. Furthermore, as a situated practice, it makes sense 
of, for example, an event, but is still anchored to the context of the 
event. For the purpose of my Ph.D., this concept, however, was not 
enough. I needed to find a way to transport the empirical data from 
their grounded positions within the project into the research pro
gramme and translate them into something relevant for participato
ry design. This process means learning from the situation but often 
requires a high level of abstraction by connecting several instances 
together. At times, this approach systematises local knowledge, as 
Krogh et al. (2015) described that the 'serial' way of drifting can be 

74
rewarding for both me and the case study participants, as it can help 
them make sense of, for example, systems that from their perspec
tive was hard to identify. At other times, a high level of abstraction 
can favour a mandate to be creative and push a tension forward into 
a proposal. 
 
Law (2004) suggested that 'simple clear descriptions don't 
work if what they are describing is not itself very coherent. The very 
attempt to be clear simply increases the mess' (p. 2). This perspective 
resounds with my experiences working with open and experimen
tative approaches. Think, for example, of the timeline (Fig. 1) that 
we attempted to construct in the early days of Amiralsstaden. The 
very attempt at a clinical, clean timeline proved that the process 
was not clinical. Law (2004) also stated that 'If much of the world 
is vague, diffuse or unspecific, slippery, emotional, ephemeral, elu
sive or indistinct, changes like a kaleidoscope, or doesn't really have 
much of a pattern at all, then where does this leave social science? 
How might we catch some of the realities we are currently missing? 
Can we know them well? Should we know them? Is "knowing" the 
metaphor that we need? And if it isn't, then how might we relate to 
them?' (Law, 2004. p. 2.).
 
During the open approach, my role was prescribed to Law 
and Singleton's (2012) analogy of the ANT ethnographer as an ant: 
slowly building a sensibility in and of the world through practice. 
It sought to gather empirical evidence by establishing a relationship 
with the situation. By arguing against institutional epistemologies, 
philosopher Schön (1995) indicated the consequences of teaching, 
researching and working around perceived 'legitimate knowledge'. 
The dilemma, as he sees it, is that of rigour or relevance. As Schön 
(1995) stated, 'In the varied topography of professional practice, the
re is a high, hard ground overlooking a swamp. On the high ground, 
manageable problems lend themselves to solutions through the use 
of research-based theory and technique. In the swampy lowlands, 
problems are messy and confusing and incapable of technical so
lution. The irony of this situation is that the problems of the high 
ground tend to be relatively unimportant to individuals or to society 
at large, however great their technical interest may be, while in the 
swamp lie the problems of greatest human concern' (p. 27). With 
this, Schön (1995) argued that the practitioner is confronted with 

75
the choice of addressing less acute issues while maintaining a high 
standard of rigour or whether to intrepidly venture down into the 
swamp. Here, the practitioner will find herself immersed in critically 
important situations, but her methods of enquiry (i.e. trial and error, 
experience, intuition and muddling through) will not translate back 
into the language of the high ground institutional epistemology. 
Schön's (1982) notion of the reflective practitioner implies that 
knowledge-in-action is the elusive kind of knowledge that comes 
with intuition gained from experience. Intuition is, one might say, 
the embodied knowledge of practice. The designer, for example, has 
the kind of know-how of a material that: knowing how it bends, 
behaves and resists through experience. The experience then grows 
into a repertoire of approaches and methods adapted to the work of 
the practitioner. Sociologist Sennett (2009) described the repertoire 
of the professional practitioner as a quiver of arrows: a collection 
of approaches that may look similar to the untrained eye, but to 
the archer, the arrows are all unique. One arrow is designed for 
close-range shots, another for windy conditions, and so on (Sennett, 
2009). This analogy of the quiver and arrows best relates to the 
practice behind the experimental approach. 
 
The third approach, the reflective approach, is more elusi
ve, as previously suggested. It finds its forms in the after-thoughts 
scribbled down on the bus leaving the workshop, the heuristics of 
a de-briefing discussion with a colleague, an added perspective, 
questions left unanswered, inklings and tentative suggestions. This 
is what Schön (1982) described as reflection-on-action, and it is a 
cognitive process most designers will be familiar with. Writing this 
text, I have been tempted to compare it both to Ingold's (2015) de
signed notion of weaving and tying knots or the ANT notion of 
drawing-things-together (Latour, 2017). However, these notions are 
operational and practical although grounded in ethos. The reflective 
approach that I seek to describe is only partially represented by this. 
The reflective approach is boundary and necessarily so. An infra
structure comes in layers and peels like an onion. Each leaf is the 
onion, but the onion is also all of the leaves combined. And so, while 
it was imperative for me, as a researcher, to be present in practical 
situations, it was equally important to take a step back and see the 
infrastructure-onion as a whole. This situation is not privy to the re

76
searchers alone; indeed, most, if not all, practitioners hold dual roles 
within their socio-material infrastructure16. Most belong to many, 
sometimes contradictory, infrastructures. Mandate, I have found, is 
closely tied to how much of your boundary self is allowed to express 
and make use of in the infrastructuring work. 
 
As previously mentioned, the reflective approach existed 
around the previous two, and practically, it took its form through 
sense making. Activities that contributed to it include, but are not 
exclusive to, memo coding and analysis, prototyping and reflective 
illustrations. These activities are some examples where the process 
of thinking has been made transparent. Unlike the previous two 
approaches, the methods here are not part of the programmatic an
swer of feral infrastructures, but what has generated it. 
3.3 PROJECTS AND CASE STUDIES
 
The case studies that this thesis rests on were primarily si
tuated in Malmö and Lund, Sweden, between 2017 and 2020. The 
ecology from which they have sprung is therefore the same for all 
projects (see section 3.1), with the notable exception of a short pro
ject in Orkney, Scotland. The latter is part of what I have been re
ferring to as secondary case studies; however, it is outside the main 
framework of this thesis. The three main-primary-case studies, 
which have been mentioned many times already, are referred to as 
Amiralsstaden, Livskonceptet and Demokratiambassadörerna. The
se three all occurred in Malmö and Lund, within the Scania region in 
southern Sweden. All three projects are initiated by the municipality 
and facilitated by public sector workers, but they have an articulated 
aim to involve and collaborate with citizens. Each project also invol
ves actors from Non-governmental organisations (NGO). Swedish 
municipalities commonly contact and collaborate with NGOs in 
16 This is congruent to Latour's (1999) notion of the circulating reference. In 
his book Pandora's Hope: Essays on the Reality of Science Studies, Latour (1999) 
departed from the old philosophical dichotomy between the word and the thing 
and how the mind perceives and relates to the world. Latour (1999) argued that 
the reference and its circulation back-and-forth is the process by which knowled
ge is produced. 

77
projects where they wish to 'meet' the public. Borrowing an orga
nisation's network or particular expertise can be a helpful bridge. It 
is also a way of outsourcing certain kinds of engagement works to 
organisations outside the municipality, which indicates that the mu
nicipality does not have a long-term engagement strategy of its own. 
In this section and the succeeding sections (sections 3.3.1, 3.3.2 and 
3.3.3), I will mainly focus on the three primary case studies. These 
three are the main sources of empirical data and the situation for 
all the anecdotes represented in this thesis. Each of the primary case 
studies tells us something about infrastructuring: In Amiralsstaden, 
the notions of power, marginalised voices and agency were promi
nent throughout the project's experimental use of knowledge alli
ances. By challenging the often-invisible knowledge, the hierarchies 
of public sector work Amiralsstaden illuminated not only showed 
what was missing but also made a contribution in suggesting how 
we may improve the situation. Within Livskonceptet, the ant-like 
trailing through an unfamiliar terrain, as done by the representatives 
(the so-called safety coordinators) from the NGO Save the Children, 
affords us insight into the labour that infrastructuring often entails. 
It is by learning from their experience that I have considered infra
structuring as care work and for maintenance and restraint, as well 
as for action and interventions. Demokratiambassadörerna (lit. The 
Democracy Ambassadors), through mere presence and action, beca
me a spotlight on marginalised or neglected voices within the city as 
an infrastructure. 
 
Notably, the case studies are not presented as programmatic 
answers in and of themselves, and the programmatic answers have 
been generated from the approaches-empirical data and insights 
drawn from several projects patchworked together (Lindström & 
Ståhl, 2014). In section 3.3.4, I will offer a brief overview of the se
condary case studies. These studies represent the social, material and 
theoretical web in which the primary case studies were situated. For 
example, MIA was a project with its own distinct history and aims, 
but from which Amiralsstaden received some of its funding and as 
such partially answered. The secondary cases remind the reader that 
the cases did not exist within a void-that they were always embed
ded within a broader entanglement of ideas, needs, biases, histories, 
processes and material structures. 

78
3.3.1 AMIRALSSTADEN
 
In front of me: A mess. A broad table buried in coffee cups, 
power chords, a plate of cookies (the cheap kind, but we eat them), 
scattered papers and pens. Drawings. Laptops. Snus. It makes for 
a strange kind of altar, in a way. Gathering around it is a small and 
unsolidified community brought together by the shared belief that 
'Something happens when people talk to each other'. It is not much 
to build a project on, and we know that. Each person around the 
table has walked their own winding path to get there and brought 
with them the languages, tools and knowledge necessary to meet a 
problem. The mess on top of the table is the sum of our negotiation 
as we merge our tools and repertoires into a shared perspective.
 
 
I worked with the Amiralsstaden project for three years 
(2017-2019). The project was run by Malmö Municipality's Urban 
Planning Department, and they also hired the main organisers. Of
ficially, the project was tasked with developing methods for citizen 
engagement, in particular facilitating the collaboration between city 
planners and citizens. A small group of 3-4 architects, communica
tions officers and organisers consisted the core team on the ground. 
Above them, Amiralsstaden had a project board that met regularly, 
comprised of directors from each of Malmö Municipality's depart
ments. The money for the project came from two different funds: 
Vinnova (a Swedish innovation agency) and the European Regional 
Development Fund. This issue meant that national and internatio
nal stakeholders were also involved in the project, but these groups 
remained virtually invisible to the core team working on the ground. 
Amiralsstaden has a long-running history. Several projects of simi
lar nature had run in the area before and many others in the wider 
Malmö. In December 2013, when the report from the Commission 
for a Socially Sustainable Malmö (Malmö Commission, 2013) was 
published, it made several recommendations for the city's future di
rections in these areas, which became a guide for Malmö's municipal 
administrations. The report looked into the work of Malmö muni
cipality, especially regarding health, welfare and justice. In light of 
the report, several projects were set into motion: one of them was 

79
Fig. 6, 7 and 8. Photodocumentation of the Amiralsstaden venue, and some of the 
everyday activities (2018 - 2019).

80
the MIA, which ran from 2016 to 2019 and addressed the recom
mendations around including citizen dialogue at the centre of plan
ning work. In the years prior, Malmö Municipality had been relying 
on the so-called penta helix model for multi-sectorial collaborations 
as a way of minimising exclusion17 (Calzada, 2020). Here, several 
actors from different sectors are invited to work together: usual
ly academia and the public and private sectors, as well as the civil 
sector, are represented. The initial project description of Amirals
staden was very open and stated that the group should establish an 
office in the area and work with knowledge alliances (lit. kunskap
sallianser) to develop a proposed detailed plan for the area. Know
ledge alliances were a new way of approaching engagement work, 
and for the Amiralsstaden core team, it was entirely new. Mikael 
Stigendal, one of the Malmö Commissioners, conceptualised know
ledge alliances as 'partnership between representatives of different 
knowledges' (Stigendal & Novy, 2018, p. 204). Using different types 
of knowledge, Stigendal and Novy (2018) indicated the critical and 
theoretical knowledge of researchers, which must be matched by the 
more grounded knowledge of practitioners. The open brief and the 
unfamiliar method meant that much of Amiralsstaden's aims, which 
developed in the first few months, were an interpretation made by 
the core group. They did this method in small steps, largely through 
trial and error. This tentative approach became part of Amiralssta
den's way of working with knowledge alliances. The first two groups 
that set out were tasked by the Amiralsstaden working group to ex
plore each question; however, out of their work, several new ques
tions were born, which became the starting point of new knowledge 
alliances. Each alliance expanded the Amiralsstaden through know
ledge by adding to its network or by making something tangible. 
Other alliances were ignited by third-party groups who approached 
Amiralsstaden with questions or problems. Ironically, the working 
group who developed this 'inclusive' method never felt confident 
enough to refer to their own method as just knowledge alliances, but 
called it 'bridging' knowledge alliances to make sure no one would 
be offended if they 'did it wrong'. Bridging Knowledge Alliances 
17 For an insight into how the penta helix model was used in Malmö see, for 
example, the report by Grander (2015) on the penta helix model used in the 
project Citispyce. 

81
(BKAs) further emphasised the connection to the local community 
by inviting residents from the local neighbourhoods to participate in 
teams of civil servants and citizens. 
 
Amiralsstaden facilitated, depending on who was counting, 
between 6-11 BKAs over the course of 3 years. These varied in size 
and length, with the longest running for almost a year, and the shor
test being an experiment over a couple of weeks only. The starting 
point for the first two BKAs was the Amiralsstaden project itself, 
specifically the venue. The venue that Malmö Municipality had af
forded the project served as a working space for the architects wor
king on the detailed plan of the area and a meeting place for anyone 
who wished to discuss the area. The idea was that anyone who held 
decision-making power in the matters of Amiralsstaden must, at the 
very least, have to set foot in the area before making any pronoun
cements. 
 
BKAs 1 and 2 ran simultaneously and were pilot initiatives 
orchestrated by the project management team. Group no. 1 (BKA1) 
had been briefed to decorate the Amiralsstaden venue and to do 
this in the most inclusive manner possible. They worked with craft 
workshops and, amongst other things, invited a group of local wo
men with middle-eastern heritage to do workshops in carpet tying 
techniques. 
 
Group 2 worked with mapping and understanding the local 
areas and focused on gathering stories. They worked with documen
tary filming and radio. The two groups were free to define what the 
final outcome of their work should be, but were asked to leave the 
project with a question that had been generated throughout their 
work. In group 2, this question became a question about listening. 
They observed that the people in the area usually engage in telling 
their stories, but whether Malmö Stad was listening is doubtful. 
Thus, this tension-on how can Malmö Stad become a better lis
tener-became the starting point of KA 3. This intention has been 
continuously exemplified, but one notable effort saw a buddy system 
being implemented, where one civil servant would be paired with a 
citizen participant for the duration of the three engagement events. 

82
This particular test caused noticeable stress and worry among the ci
vil servants. One of them called the day before the first event, asking 
to be paired with someone who was also a mother, so that they 
would 'at least have one thing in common' (Author's field notes, 
2018). 
 
The work at Amiralsstaden was evaluated regularly, roughly 
every six months, through reflective workshops with the working 
group, representatives from the board, the citizen participants, 
researchers and people from the Urban Planning Department. At 
the end of Amiralsstaden, several official reports were written (c.f. 
Seravalli, 2019; Smedberg, 2019b) and I have published several pa
pers drawing on anecdotes from the project (e.g. Smedberg, 2019a; 
Smedberg, 2021). 
 
When I was invited into the project, it had been running for 
roughly 6 months and was still in the early stages of defining what 
the project was to be about. I was invited into this negotiation as a 
'critical friend', which I was referred to throughout. For the most 
part, the work I conducted within Amiralsstaden could be described 
as PAR, as I participated in many of the knowledge alliances and in 
many of the working group's meetings, as well as in the meetings 
with the project's board. I conducted nine semi-structured interviews 
and facilitated regular reflection workshops with the BKAs, as well 
as a mandatory summarising workshop at the end of each BKA. I 
hosted numerous dialogue groups, which at the time were referred to 
as workshops but in truth were mostly a traditional Swedish fika18. 
In terms of my approaches, I was working within Amiralsstaden 
during the open approach (2017-2018), the experimental approach 
(2018-2019) and as part of the reflective approach. 
18 A Swedish fika is an opportunity to have a chat in an informal setting, usu
ally with coffee and a pastry or-most traditionally-a cinnamon bun. 

83
3.3.2 LIVSKONCEPTET
 I have been wandering around in the suburb of Linnero for 
the past two hours. It is a grey afternoon in the autumn of 2017, 
and I am very early for a co-lab hosting here later in the day. I 
have been trying to find a reason as to why someone would want to 
come to this place, but alas, I cannot. Aside from the library (which 
today is closed) and the church (also closed), there is only the local 
supermarket and one more shop. There is no life on the streets, no 
homeless people sitting outside the supermarket, as I am used to 
from Malmö. No sign of graffiti or vandalism, but also no sign of 
anything else, really. What do people do here? I think to myself. 
There is nothing to do but go home. 
 
Livskonceptet literally translates to 'the life concept'. The 
project, which began in 2017, aimed to design a temporary accom
modation that held everything a family with young children may 
need to start a life in Sweden. A recent legislative change in the inter
national displacement of landed refugees meant that each municipa
lity must offer a number (depending on the size and resources of the 
municipality) of temporary (two-year) placements to people and fa
milies who have gained permanent residence in Sweden. Previously, 
each municipality had the right to decide independently whether it 
wished to offer placements, indicating that some Swedish municipa
lities had denied all or most 'newly arrived'19. Lund, a municipality 
just a 20-minute drive north from Malmö, has a history of denying 
placements to refugees or people with permanent residences. This 
occurrence meant that Lund and other municipalities were comple
tely lacking in material resources (i.e. having no established housing 
available), as well as lacking the knowledge and experience of how 
to arrange the organisation around their placement. Livskonceptet 
was to be a pilot project, where all the parts of the municipality 
19 The term nyanlända is an established term in Sweden and is defined and 
used by the Swedish Migration Agency to indicate someone who has been re
ceived into a municipality and has been granted a residence permit. Within The 
Swedish National Agency for Education, the definition is however broader, and 
here, the child is considered newly arrived only if they have begun their educa
tion in another country and within their first four years in Sweden (3 kapitlet 12 
a § skollagen). 

84
placement scheme were tied to the accommodations and held to
gether by a so-called social mortar (in Swedish, det sociala kittet). 
The municipality placement scheme here refers to those services that 
the municipality is legally obliged to provide to the newly arrived, 
such as the adult Swedish education programme (SFI), support by 
employment services, education for the children and healthcare. 
Livskonceptet paid special attention to newly arrived children but 
recognised that it was in the children's best interests that the whole 
family was well taken care of. 
The Lund Municipality invited the following to the project:
• The NGO Save the Children
• Lunds Kommuns Fastighets AB (LKF) (Lund Municipality 
 
Housing Association)
• Malmö Kommuns Bolag för Bostadsförsörjning (MKB) 
 
 
(Malmö Municipality Housing Association)
• IKEA
• Archifunture (a Malmö-based design agency specialising in 
 
themed playgrounds)
• Malmö University. 
 
The project inhabited a controversial space. Some believed 
that Lund should not have to accept the newly arrived, and those 
same voices saw the project as an unnecessary 'charity'. Humanita
rian voices then pointed out the flaws in the Swedish refugee system, 
such as the issues of offering temporary accommodation as part of 
the two-year settling-in programme. The latter was a critique ex
pressed by Save The Children in a report released the same year 
(Samzelius, 2017) and made for an internal contradiction within the 
project from day one. On the one hand, Livskonceptet sought to 
give the newly arrived the best conditions to settle in and integrate 
into Swedish society and for the families to thrive in their new ho
metown. On the other hand, many of those involved were aware of 
the limitations of the temporary accommodations and knew that 
Livskonceptet was not an ideal solution. I experienced the working 
group as having a 'making the best of what we have' mentality, and 
for what it is worth, as well-meaning individuals. However, the un

85
derlying critique against the project would mean that Lund munici
pality guarded its project from public scrutiny20. 
 
When I joined the project in autumn 2017, a few factors 
had already been decided. The barracks that would form the basis 
of the accommodation had been ordered, the collaboration partners 
had been invited, and a timeline had been set. After a few meetings 
with the working group, my supervisor, Prof. Per-Anders Hillgren 
and I noted the lack of inclusion in the dialogues around the project. 
For example, no one in the working group had any live experience 
of the Swedish asylum process. In addition, no one (except the two 
representatives from Save the Children) had any experience wor
king with asylum seekers or newly arrived. This factor presented the 
project with an immense knowledge gap. We also noted that no one 
from the local area had been invited, asked or even notified of the 
project. With support from Save The Children's report, the accom
modations must be placed in a residential area, with easy access to 
amenities, such as local shops, healthcare, libraries, public transport 
and schools. However, placing barracks housing up to 20 families in 
a residential area would anger local residents21. 
 
 
As a case study, Livskonceptet exemplifies co-creational 
work between an NGO, public, private and civil sector, which we 
(myself and Prof. Per-Anders Hillgren) sought to exemplify through 
a co-lab. We decided to use it to assist the work done by the two 
safety coordinators appointed by Save the Children. The role of sa
fety coordinators was one of the main factors that set Livskonceptet 
apart from previous housing projects for the newly arrived. Their 
20 The municipality's fear of the public opinion comes through in the 
anecdote presented in section 5.2 of this thesis. Here, I outlined a public 
forum for the residents living in the area where the pilot was located.

21 Not In My Back Yard (NIMBY) is the phenomenon where residents in 
an area oppose new developments, such as homeless shelters, group homes or 
affordable housing. The NIMBY arguments usually revolve around this as a 
down-grading on an area and that this in turn would lead to crimes, theft and 
violence. NIMBY largely ignores the arguments in favour of social welfare, af
fordable housing and decreased segregation (Gibson, 2005; Hermansson, 2007; 
Pendall, 1999).

86
presence acknowledged a social infrastructure outside and around 
the project, and we viewed the safety coordinators as the infra
structuring practitioners of the project. The infrastructuring work 
here entailed the building of connections with local stakeholders in 
the area where the projects were set to take place, from projects in 
the nearby area that had worked successfully with social integration, 
and with people who had live experiences of obtaining permanent 
residents in the region. 
 
Through the co-lab, we brought together these groups in a 
series of workshops, running from December 2017 to June 2018, 
as we sought to open up the dialogue around the project. In this 
particular case, the co-lab and the extended co-ownership of Livs
konceptet were not just a way to improve the project and polish the 
details; it was a way to proactively ensure that the new families were 
well received by the local community. Initially, we also intended to 
invite the first families in the pilot programme to participate in the 
co-lab as well, but a series of delays to the move-in date rendered 
this impossible, as no new families can be invited.
 
We invited the local residents in the area and representatives 
from local organisations, sports clubs, the church and the local li
brary. The primary function of this conference was to fill the know
ledge gap within the creation of the Livskonceptet pilot; however, 
a positive consequence of these meetings was a network of people 
in the area who were happy to welcome their new neighbours. An 
organisation was formed, Together for Linero (lit. Tillsammans för 
Linero), which functions as a civic safety net when the new fami
lies finally arrived. This group worked with the safety coordinators 
from Save The Children and offered support and resources to them 
and the newly arrived. 
 
The concept behind Livskonceptet was adapted by Malmö
