Municipality the following year, and two more Livskonceptet hou
sing units were established. These new installations did not establish 
a co-lab and did not have a civic 'Together for X' network. Several 
reports have been written about Livskonceptet (most notably, the 
official report by Save the Children, 2020), and one account from 
Prof. Per-Anders Hillgren and I was submitted to Lund Municipality 
and Save The Children. This case study was a part of all of the three 
research approaches: open, experimental and reflective.

87
Fig. 9. Photo documentation of a workshop with the Livskonceptet co-lab in Linnero (2018).

3.3.3 DEMOKRATIAMBASSADÖRERNA
 
During 2018 and 2019, I participated in a Do-Think-Tank 
that regularly brought together a number of actors to share and re
flect on ongoing local projects and try to find opportunities for col
laboration. The actors came as individual professionals from Malmö 
Municipality, Save The Children, Sensus (a national study associa
tion) and Malmö University. The Do-Think-Tank raised an enquiry 
about what kind of values our projects produced versus which 
values were measured. Together with a case worker from Save The 
Children, I set out to gather stories of projects that produced imme
asurable social values. We spoke to Rosengård Library, Amiralssta
den and eventually our work led us to the organisation Together in 
Unity (TiF). TIF is composed of a small group of managers and a 
broader board of young adults (ages 17-25 years) who together seek 
to arrange activities for teenagers and young adults in Malmö. In the 
summer prior in 2018, TiF hosted a summer internship programme 
where young adults were hired as 'democracy ambassadors' ahead 
of the Swedish parliamentary election, and this programme beca
me one of our case studies for the Do-Think-Tank's question about 
value production. TiF invited me and the case worker from Save The 
Children to incorporate some of our ideas around social value into 
this.
 
The 2018 summer internship encouraged the democracy 
ambassadors to go out into Malmö and talk to people about the im
portance of voting. The participants were tasked not to support any 
particular party, but rather engage with people on the streets about 
any kind of questions they might have about the election, about the 
practicalities of voting and about the importance of democracy. The 
statistics following the election showed an increase in the number of 
voters in the areas that TiF had targeted (ibid.). Together with TiF, 
we, from the Do-Think-Tank, discussed the value the programme 
would have had for the young ambassadors who were introduced 
to working life with skills like critical thinking, communication and 
political awareness. We asked ourselves how far such assets could 
generate value and how great that value might be for society at lar
ge. We wondered if the notion of democracy ambassadors could be 
taken one step further. As such, the idea of a second iteration of the 

89
summer internship was born. Keeping the same structure as in the 
previous year, we split the summer into two periods. Each period 
would run for 5 weeks, including an introductory course, then th
ree weeks of field work, followed by a week of presentations and 
reflections. Each period hosted approximately 40 democracy am
bassadors, which were divided into smaller groups and assigned a 
couple of supervisors from the TiF board. We worked with the bo
ard during the spring semester to develop the programme's methods 
and research questions through a series of workshops. During the 
workshops, the importance of being able to articulate your own 
value as a group or organisation was discussed as a political act. 
Here, the idea that the young participants in the programme would 
articulate their own perspectives of Malmö and should help others 
in doing the same was introduced. I hosted another workshop with 
the board to prepare them for the task of supporting reflection, and 
we were joined by a freelance journalist who could aid them in the 
art of communication.
 
At this point, we also gained interest from actors within Mal
mö Stad who saw the project as an alternative to the municipality's 
own data gathering. TiF's board members saw attention from Mal
mö Municipality as a positive thing and expressed enthusiasm that 
their stories could potentially be heard and that they might make 
a difference in the city. With Malmö Municipality, a third newco
mer arrived: Cognitive Edge, which is a British software developing 
company that free-of-charge leased us access to a newly developed 
app called SenseMaker. SenseMaker promised to do the work of a 
qualitative researcher by analysing the stories and data points that 
the field work generated. The views on whether to use SenseMaker 
differed, both amongst the board members and among the growing 
group of interested parties that surrounded them. Some saw that the 
effectiveness of standardised data-gathering would affect the chan
ces of democracy ambassadors to be heard within Malmö Stad and 
argued that the app should be the main tool for the programme. 
This was supported by the fact that the app allowed the users to set 
up the 'questionnaire' and that the ambassadors would therefore 
be included in the process of determining the programme's para
meters. Others, including myself, argued that the open-endedness 
of allowing the ambassadors to freely choose which methods they 

90
Fig. 10. Scan of a hand-written narrative from one of the ambassadors (2019).

91
used for communication and documentation was more important 
to the agency of the participants. This was also supported by a con
cern over data ownership in relation to SenseMaker, as Cognitive 
Edge would have free access to the gathered data once the lease was 
done. Unable to agree on one or the other, the programme was split 
into two periods: the first period rests on the methods and questions 
developed with the board during the spring, and the second period 
rests on SenseMaker. 
 
Over the course of the programme, more than 2,000 stories 
were collected by over 80 teenagers from Malmö. I contributed a 
Future Workshop (Jungk & Müllert, 1987) at the beginning of each 
cohort and the reflective workshops at the end of the summer. As 
a case study, Demokratiambassadörerna contributed to the experi
mental and reflective approaches. 
Fig. 11. Photo documentation from the Future Workshop with TiF (2019).

92
3.3.4 SECONDARY CASE STUDIES
 
I have been part of and in contact with several projects 
during the research periods. These projects were not my primary 
source of data nor were they necessarily structured 'case studies', as 
the title of this section suggests. However, I have nonetheless cho
sen to include a note about them in this thesis, as they, in one way 
or another, contributed to my understanding of agency and infra
structuring or because they were so deeply intwined with the pri
mary case studies that they are difficult to overlook. For example, 
MIA, as I have previously mentioned, was one such project to which 
Amiralsstaden was attached. The secondary case studies are inclu
ded here for transparency and context, but I will not explicitly refer 
to them in the theory chapters. 
 
MIA was a three-year project (2017-2019) that sought to 
address the need for Malmö to build more accommodations. With 
funding from Vinnova and the European Regional Development 
Fund, the MIA supported numerous projects around Malmö that 
were either ongoing or already planned. The projects exemplified 
the innovative approach that the MIA valued, taking on old chal
lenges with new methods or trying novel ways of working with city 
planning. Amiralsstaden was one such project, supported by the 
MIA for its innovative approach to city planning through know
ledge alliances. Examples of other projects were a 'smart' mapping 
project for sharing resources, a co-creation project in the reconstruc
tion of Järnvägsverkstäderna, which are the old railway workshops 
in the Kirseberg area. The organisation of the MIA brought together 
many actors, including departments within the municipality, that 
did not usually work together. Finding ways to negotiate between 
the different actors' priorities requires a lot of work. I followed MIA 
from the sideline, conducting three semi-structured interviews with 
the director and two key organisers within the operation. I also par
ticipated in and observed a dozen working meetings and facilitated a 
workshop where I used a design game to facilitate dialogue between 
the groups (Brandt et al., 2008; Eriksen et al., 2014). 
 
The Do-Think-Tank, which was previously mentioned in 

93
Fig. 11. Photo documentation from field study of windturbines in Orkney, UK. (2019).

94
relation to Demokratiambassadörerna, was an unfunded initiati
ve by individual actors from Malmö Municipality, Sensus, Malmö 
University and Save the Children. The group met roughly once a 
month and shared some of their ongoing projects. The group has an 
implicit goal, which is to try to find venues for collaboration among 
the actors, but this was not a requirement per se. Most of the par
ticipants used it as a way to reflect on their everyday practices and 
found that reflecting with others was helpful. 
 
The ANT study of the Orkney Wind Turbines began as 
part of my master's thesis project (Smedberg, 2017) before I came 
to Malmö and Malmö University. The project-inquiring inventive 
methods in exploring socio-material infrastructures-looked into 
the case of Orkney wind energy development. Both the methods 
(i.e. inventive methods, grounded theory and participatory design) 
and theory (i.e. STS, ANT and social design) from this project be
came informative as I wrote the initial research proposal for my Ph. 
D. In 2018, I revisited the case together with Prof. Ann Light from 
Sussex University, which was funded by the EU Shape Energy Fund. 
In our paper, we discussed the entangled nature of people, craft, cul
ture and politics building connections through rocks and wind and 
electrical grids (Smedberg & Light, 2018).
3.4 PROGRAMMATIC ANSWERS
 
Through being with, prodding into, talking to and co-ex
isting with the projects introduced above, the design programme 
that I initially started has evolved. The methodology of this pro
ject rests heavily on plurality and democracy. The approaches and 
methods engaged with the empirics have all been chosen for their 
ability to hold nuance and engage without reduction. This is what 
we might call the forte of programmatic design research-the ability 
to hold big thoughts and stories and mould them together into... 
...something. As a scientific response, what is a programmatic an
swer? What is a good programmatic answer? Well, let me first dwell 
on the question of what good empirical data are. Gabrys and Pritc
hards (2018) worked with a project that investigates citizen sensing 
practices in relation to monitoring air pollution. After following a 
participatory approach (DiSalvo et al., 2015), they used the term 

95
'good enough data'. According to Gabrys and Pritchard (2018), a 
'"Just good enough data" draws attention to attempts to counter the 
reliance on high levels of measurement accuracy as the sole criterion 
by which data are evaluated. What different practices emerge when 
environmental data are engaged in a more indicative register? What 
do these practices enable? And what other relations, connections 
and points of focus might "just good enough data" generate? ́ (p. 6).
If, indeed, social research 'exists to assist society in solving social 
problems' (Greenwood et al., 1993, p. 176), the data we then need 
is perhaps one that can assist us in what Donna Haraway refers to 
as thinking with. In Staying with the Trouble: Making Kin in the 
Chtulucene, Haraway (2016) presented thinking with as an onto
logical shift from when each man thinks for himself. Instead, she 
argued that the way we think, our possibilities to act and our abi
lity to make kin are predicated on how we tell stories. We must, as 
Haraway (2016) said, tell big enough stories that '... gather up the 
complexities and keep the edges open and greedy for surprising new 
and old connections' (p. 101). This storytelling is, in and of itself, 
a care work, but it not only enables care work but also others to 
get engaged (Lindström & Ståhl, 2019). I hope the programmatic 
answers of this thesis will be perceived as the outcome of a mutual 
enquiry and exchanges that generate good enough stories that may 
continue to do. 
 
Through each approach, the field spoke back, offering dif
ferent insights into the programme around agency. The following 
three chapters each represent one such answer. The chapter 'Feral 
Infrastructuring' (chapter 4) should be read as a meeting between 
the original programme and the existing literature around infra
structuring. By settling and anchoring the theories around infra
structuring, the reflective approach (Approach C) revealed a new 
understanding of the limitations of the theory. The answer concerns 
the infrastructures themselves and suggests that the inherent ide
ological basis within any organisation, corporation or group has 
bearings on who we view as actors and stakeholders. This answer 
then alters our understanding of what the 'right' ways of acting are. 
As elaborated on chapter 4, I do not view this as a critique per se 
but rather a reparative reading that allows for more diverse engage
ments. 

96
 
The following answer is represented in chapter 5 and is en
titled 'Affective Infrastructuring'. It feeds of the notion of feral infra
structures and suggests that one 'plane' that we often fail to consider 
is the affective plain. Even after the material turn within the social 
sciences and humanities agency has been prescribed to inanimate 
objects, such as stones, coffee machines and bicycle racks, much of 
the infrastructuring theory still fails to consider the most human 
of forces: emotions. The chapter illuminates the emotional labour 
performed by infrastructuring practitioners and asks how we might 
care in a constructive way. This answer, drawn from empirical ob
servations in the open approach, holds accounts of when emotions 
were involved in more clinical, linear processes and made them mes
sy.
 
The final answer, drawn from the experimental approach, 
holds a proposed methodological approach that may assist infra
structuring practitioners from getting stuck in the stickiness of af
fect. It will not present a toolkit or a roadmap, but a sensitised prax
is of listening that allows the practitioner to think and move forward 
with their extended situation. 

97

98
4. FERAL INFRASTRUCTURING
 
One of the greatest lessons I took away from Amiralsstaden 
was the insight that the city we set out to create was already happening 
around us. Ours was only one in a myriad of processes. Not all of these 
processes would fit into the frame of what we might call a design project 
or even a project; however, these methods might instead be described as 
movements, agendas or trajectories. In the Amiralsstaden project, our di
rectives came from the municipality, and they encouraged 'engagement' 
and 'participation', so we invited others into our project. We reached out 
to a nearby Folkhögskola, who saw us as a way to create live projects 
for their students. We took in a new NGO start-up that volunteered to 
decorate our venue, which was a way for them to get a nice show-case 
project of what they could do ('smart' decorations of children's rooms 
for families with low income). We worked with elderly women who saw 
us as a chance to get out of the house and spend a pleasant afternoon 
gossiping over coffee. We worked with another group of elderly women 
who saw us as a venue for a workshop in traditional craft and a chance 
to pass on their knowledge of carpet tying to the next generation. We 
worked with a group of schoolchildren who wanted to make a podcast 
about their area and with an SFI group who wanted to make connec
tions with Swedish natives for language practice. We made coffee for 
agitators who sought a connection into the municipality, guided students 
in writing dissertations, and humoured a filmmaker who had a crush on 
one of our project assistants. The place we were sent to 'make' was not 
a clean slate. Each person had their own personal reasons for joining the 

99
project, and each organisation has its own agenda. Consequently, 
these divergent inlets could not be congruent with one another or 
complementary even. Furthermore, these agendas cannot be compli
mentary, include shared goals at times, or say that our agendas are 
fixed and unyielding. The concept of feralinfrastructures, as I will 
discuss in this chapter, suggests that the notion of the infrastructure 
is unhelpfully clinical and that which makes an infrastructure move 
and evolve, that is, what keeps it alive, is the messy intersections 
and entanglements with other organisations and actors outside its 
own frames. 
 
This chapter is divided into two parts. In the first part of the 
chapter, I will attempt to distinguish what worldview was formed 
when the programme of this thesis met the theoretical conceptions 
of infrastructuring. I will discuss infrastructures as feral: unruly, 
ungrateful and naive. This discussion is intended as the first pro
grammatic answer; the altered design programme is in and of itself 
an outcome of the Ph.D. project. The changes that the experiments 
we make during a design project are not only manifested in the phy
sical outputs or designed artefacts, but are also found in our altered 
perceptions of the original question or in the things relationships. 
An elevated understanding of a situation or an issue that we gain 
from grappling with it is itself, a receipt of the time we spend on 
it. Is it not? The re-articulation of infrastructuring that this chapter 
provides, which I will soon provide, will give a backdrop and a ra
tionale for the subsequent two chapters (chapter 5: Affective Infra
structuring and chapter 6: Collaborative Anecdotalization). 
 
The re-articulation of infrastructuring provided here will 
first and foremost seek to situate the notion of infrastructuring in a 
different setting than that for which it was originally intended. As 
the case studies that I have drawn upon all reside within the public 
sector domain of work and not in the industries of the 1970s or 
within the programming community of the early 1990s, the complex 
systems that I have observed behaved differently. In the theoretical 
tradition of complex adaptive systems (Dooley, 1997), which pre
dates the discussions of complex systems, such as infrastructures, 
we observe that our understanding of an organisation or system is 

100
always ideologically tainted. Guided by the morals or virtues of the 
dominant ideology, we infuse merit systems into our arrangements-
sometimes unwittingly, sometimes deliberately. This is equally true 
of the prevailing scientific paradigms of each era, as these have also 
been performative in this sense (Ackoff & Emery, 1972; Bowker 
& Star, 2000; Dooley, 1997; Hayles, 1991). In the workplace, the 
Fordists conceptualised an organisation as a machinery (Gramsci, 
1934), giving rise to the notion that 'A centralised, bureaucratic 
structure is key in helping organisation leaders determine proper ac
tions and deploy instructions to the workforce. Control is explicit: 
monetary rewards and punishments are a form of motivation' (Doo
ley, 1997, p. 70). Taylor (1911) took these ideas of the division of 
labour, the interchangeability of parts/workers and standardisation, 
and developed them with scientific methods to create a philosophy 
of management. These ideas not only affect the practical, technical 
organisation of work but also determine how work is valued, how 
workers are valued and by extension, how much a life is valued. 
4.1 GHOST IN THE MACHINE: IDEAS AND IDEOLOGIES 
AS INTRINSIC PARTS OF THE INFRASTRUCTURE
 
Let us take a detour via Bernays, Edward (1891-1995). Ber
nays was Sigmund Freud's nephew and described his own lifework 
as 'Freudianism in blood and spirit' (Olasky, 1985, p. 17). Bernays 
was a pioneer in public relations and drew on his uncle's ideas of 
human nature. According to Freud, the human mind was not as ra
tional as previous psychologists had believed it to be; rather, it had a 
primitive side, an irrational side. This, the subconscious, was guided 
by forces of aggression, desire and fear. Freud reasoned that if we 
did not learn to control these primitive urges-as both individuals 
and as a society-it would lead to chaos (Curtis, 2002). As such, 
Freud's theories created an incentive and a rationale that deny that 
an individual or group agency to act on their own instinct could 
be justified, as people were irrational and their instincts guided by 
desires. Freud's work informed the propagandists during WW1, and 

101
Bernays was impressed with their ability to sway public opinion. In 
an interview, Bernays reflected that 'If you could use propaganda 
for war, you could use it for peace' (ibid.). The quote makes Ber
nays come of as an altruistic person, but what he actually meant 
was that he saw the potential of using propaganda at home during 
peacetime to sell things. However, as he knew that propaganda had 
become a dirty word during the war, he renamed it 'public relations' 
and soon became instrumental in shifting the American population 
from citizens to consumers. Bernays was approached by a cigaret
te concern, which sought to attract women to smoking. This was 
at a time when it was considered unladylike to smoke-a public 
opinion that cigarette companies were eager to change. Bernays fol
lowed his uncle's formula and hired a psychoanalyst who told him 
that cigarettes were actually a symbol of the penis and, therefore, 
of power. Bernays then hired a squadron of young, attractive New 
York debutantes to walk in the Easter Parade and-on a given sym
bol-light up their cigarettes. The newspapers had been tipped off 
by Bernays beforehand, and they reported on the young, rebellious 
women, whom they believed to be suffragettes, sparking up their 
'torches of freedom'. As Curtis (2002) noted in his documentary 
about Edward Bernays, 'The cigarettes did not make the women 
any more powerful, but it made them feel like they were'. Bernays 
successfully tapped into the desire for freedom and injected this into 
a product. Products were previously sold to the American popula
tion based on need, but they were now sold based on desire. Many 
things could and should (and have!) be said about the fetishisation 
of products. Arguably, this shift from need to desire has been one 
of the biggest determining factors of the design disciplines during 
the 20th century: determining the 'rightness' of design's form and 
function (Papanek, 1985). Papanek (1985) famously argued that 
'design is the conscious and intuitive effort to impose meaning' (p. 
4)-a staggering sentence when we consider that the entire modern 
world is consciously designed. As such, the designer's create value: 
'This most obvious is commercial value, but may also include social, 
cultural, environmental, political and symbolic values' (Julier, 2008, 
p. 14). Furthermore, designers provide the foundations for creating 

102
and recreating values as the means of production (i.e. technologies, 
workspaces, work tools, social conditions and protocols), and qu
ite literally are designed. The shift that occurred in the first half of 
the 20th century has moved the design industries away from artisan 
practices and from the ideological pursuits of, say, the Bauhaus mo
vement. The story of Bernays and his 'torches of freedom' illustrates 
how the concept of mankind can be rewritten: The Freudian notion 
of the irrational individual justifies top-down interventions and al
ters the way we think about citizen rights. 
 
Citizenship, tied as it is to notions of democracy, cannot 
stagnate. As both sociologist Barbalet (1988) and later Balibar 
(2015) argued, citizenship is a multifaceted concept and the defi
nition of citizenship waves with the perspective. By contrast, '[C]
itizenship equates the right to equal participation' (Barbalet, 1988. 
p. 68). However, this concept is only true when looking from the 
inside out-for someone who has been denied citizenship, the ques
tion of it becomes 'the architect of legitimate social inequality' (ibid. 
p. 48). Citizenship is an on-going negotiation or struggle for rights: 
industrial rights, civil rights and social rights. Balibar (2015) stated, 
'Actively demanding citizenship brought the permanence of conflict 
into the core of the sphere of the formalised state. This is what could 
lead us to say that, in the extreme, in the state configuration of the 
political there is no "democracy" in the pure or ideal sense of the 
term, but that there could always be a process of democratization-
which in truth is perhaps more important' (p. 23). Citizenship de
mands an active subject, whereas the consumer is a passive one. In 
the industries, during the first half of the 20th century, a similar 
movement was made towards reducing workers from a group of 
citizens, with the mandate to shape their workplace. For example, 
Huybrechts et al. (2018) highlighted the role of Fordism in shaping 
the working conditions of the 20th century. Drawing on Arendt's 
(1958) The Human Condition, they discussed the political dimensi
ons of work or the potential for a more political dimension of work. 

103
4.1.1 THE HUMAN CONDITION
 Arendt's (1958) notions of work and labour are outlined in 
The Human Condition. Arendt (1958) was a schooled academic, 
well versed in Western philosophy, with personal ties to many of 
her contemporary philosophical and political thinkers. At the same 
time, Arendt (1958) is a Jewish woman living in Europe (and later in 
America) under the rise and rule of the anti-Semitic far right. Arendt's 
(1958) particular situation lends itself well to a sober, un-pleading 
re-thinking of the way we think about power and politics. She tra
velled back to the very foundations of Western philosophy and re
visits notions, such as the agora, but does so without falling into a 
nostalgia for the classic Greeks. Arendt (1958) introduced the no
tion of the agora22 -the shared democratic space-and invited the 
reader to consider its function: Should it allow the labourers, such as 
women and slaves, into it? The agora is both the subject of critique 
and, contradictorily, a superlative to strive towards: 
'[T]he natural innate productivity of society was the unques
tionable productivity of homo faber. Conversely, antiquity 
knew full well types of human communities in which not the 
citizen of the polis and not the res publica as such established 
and determined the content of the public realm, but where 
the public life of the ordinary man was restricted to "wor
king for the people" that is [...], a household labourer and 
therefor a slave. The hallmark of these non-political commu
nities was that their public place, the agora, was not the me
eting place of citizens, but a market place where craftsmen 
could show and exchange their products.' (Arendt, 1998, p. 
159-160).
22 Arendt, as mentioned, was grounded in the roots of western philosophy. She 
studied Kant, St. Augustine and Aristotle, and much of her vocabulary has been 
borrowed from the ancient Greeks. The term agora refers to the public part of 
the Greek society: the polis-the city state and the form of political governan
ce-was comprised of an 'agora' and an 'oikia'. The oikia was the private realm, 
the realm within the home and the mind, and the agora was the space where the 
people, mind and body meet. 

104
 
Such contradictions are not the only perplexing thing about 
Arendt's writings; another may be her recurrent insistence on du
alisms: begging the reader to hold two thoughts at the same time, 
as seen in her conceptualising of the common. On the one hand, 
the common, according to Arendt, means that in the public realm, 
'everything that appears in public can be seen and heard by every
body and has the widest possible publicity. For us, appearance-so
mething that is being seen and heard by others as well as by oursel
ves - constitutes reality' (p. 50). This is a reality-affirming common 
sense that, through action and speech, can transport us from the 
innermost private and intimate aspects of life into the world that we 
share with others (Arendt, 1954, 1958). According to Arendt, this 
is where politics happens. On the other hand, a physical space is 
also commonly understood: 'For Arendt, politics needs a "stage" for 
both actors and spectators, for public appearance is an integral part 
of it. Spatialisation provides this space of encounter as a domain for 
mutual exposure and relationality' (Dikeç, 2015, p. 9). 
 
One of the things that made the Amiralsstaden project spe
cial was that it forced a move of its board of directors from the city 
hall to the Amiralsstaden venue. Due to the segregation of the city of 
Malmö, undeniably, many of the people working in the municipality, 
especially in the more senior positions, have never set foot in some 
of the city's more deprived areas. One could, for example, live in the 
more affluent Västra Hamnen and work in the city centre without so 
much having to pass through Rosengård. This inspired the project 
management group to say that not only their meetings, the meetings 
of the architects or the meetings with the citizens should take place 
in the project venue. The idea echoes the words of Dikeç above-it 
provided a space for encounters-and enforced a somewhat radical 
proposition that no decisions should be made about the area or the 
project by someone who had never encountered it. As an initiative, 
it did not last all the way to the end of the project. At first, a small 
exception was made, and at the end of the project, all board me
etings were held in the city hall. It was considered a disadvantage for 
the board members to have to travel and an inconvenience to work 
away from the city hall's installed resources. 

105
 
Arendt (1998) suggested that private and public realms also 
exist in the world, where 'the public gathers us together and yet 
prevents our falling over each other' (p. 52), and by doing so, she 
lays the foundations for how the reader is to understand her three 
key terms in The Human Condition: work, labour and action. The 
reader is asked to follow the idea that the two realms are not in 
competition with one another, but complementary. The value of the 
private realm is seen in the public realm, and the public realm is very 
much in need of the private realm. Political potency, however, lies in 
the public realm. In Arendt's reading, the enlargement of the private 
sphere-consuming things that traditionally have belonged to the 
public sphere, such as work-is becoming part of the private. 
 
Bauhaus may serve as an interesting example here. Bringing 
together 'the two sides of the Enlightenment project: the hard (tech
nology and the natural sciences) with the soft (values, democracy, 
art and ethics)' (Ehn, 1998, p. 207). The first instalment of Bauhaus 
sought to generate a 'third culture' from the meetings of modernity, 
industrialisation, machine and art. Design for the modern and free 
men and women (ibid.) entailed both a re/production of everyday 
life, as well as a production of designed artefacts and goods. We 
reduce what we produce to commodities that can be purchased and 
delivered on the market. In this way, it also illustrates that the way 
design comes into the world is part of this privatisation of the world. 
In contemporary design discourse, Arendt's notion of the wor
ker-who, for the sake of argument, is understood in this text as a 
craftsperson-may appear somewhat outdated. The process is, as 
previously mentioned, a process with a definite beginning and end. 
The craftsperson moves through a process of visualisation to imple
mentation in a swift, almost linear motion. If the idea is rigorous, 
then the outcome will be 'an independent entity [...] to the human 
artifice' (opt. cit., p. 143). Within the discipline of participatory de
sign, the notion of use-before-use (cf. Redström, 2008) is used to 
consider the issue that the designer generally has a minimal (if any) 
chance of understanding what a final product might be or how it will 
behave within its loci. Within participatory design, therefore, the 

106
practice holds that we meet together with future users and initiate 
a prototyping process. By involving participants in imaging coming 
use, the participatory designer(s) can move towards the future arte
fact. However, doing so requires that we know who the forthcoming 
users and subsequent use will be. Here, the strategy of design-af
ter-design entails that what we design-be it a platform, a thing or a 
toolkit-should be designed so that the future users themselves can 
configure it at a later stage when they know what they want to use 
it for (Bjögvinsson et al., 2012). These strategies (use-before-use and 
design-after-design), although not part of Arendt's own conceptua
lisations of making, use or work, are arguably still congruent with 
her notions of plurality (i.e. the action of moving between work and 
labour) in the public spaces, as well as the notions of durability: 'If 
the world is to contain a public space, it cannot be erected for one 
generation and planned for the living only; it must transcend the 
life-span of mortal men' (Arendt, 1998, p. 55). 
4.1.2 WORK
 
Participatory design as a discipline was born as a reaction 
towards the Fordist workplace, and it does not follow the same 
philosophy as the one that Edward Bernays operated under. In his 
world, human beings were irrational and should be organised. In the 
world of ideas that PD draws upon, human beings are rational and 
relevant and should be organised. Emancipation has always been 
imperative within the participatory design tradition-albeit, the in
terpretations of how and what this entails have varied. Huybrechts 
et al. (2018) drew the connection between Arendt's writing and par
ticipatory design based on the shared insistence that the workplace 
is a political metaphysical arena and that work should be afforded a 
stronger political character. 
 
The Human Condition may not have been intended to en
ter into a conversation with feminism or about the female condi
tion, but Arendt certainly did intend to enter into a conversation 
with Karl Marx. Arendt distinguished labour from work, partially 

107
through the durability of the product of the work of the hands and 
through a process with a distinguishable beginning and end. Arendt 
(1998) stated that 'In the process of making [...] the end is beyond 
doubt: it has come when an entirely new thing with enough durabi
lity to remain in the world as an independent entity has been added 
to the human artifice' (p. 143). This feature, along with the human 
artifice, allows work to step into the public sphere and alter the spa
ces-physical and metaphysical-which are common to many. By 
'working on the world', the homo faber acts into it and becomes part 
of that plurality, which is central to Arendt's understanding of the 
human condition. Arendt (1998) suggested that these in-betweens 
constitute relevance: 'Action and speech go on between men [...] 
These interests constitute, in the word's most literal significance, so
mething which interest, which lies between people and therefore can 
relate and bind them together' (p. 182; original emphasis). 
 
To Marx, the notion of freedom under capitalism is, for the 
proletarians, a formal freedom. The worker is formally free but can 
only survive by entering into a contract with the capitalist, and as 
such, a personal relationship of force-as seen under feudal con
ditions-is replaced by capitalist relations (Heinrich, 2004; Marx, 
1970). Arendt (1998) wrote, 'Marx's attitude towards labour, and 
that is towards the very centre of his thought, has never ceased to 
be equivocal. While it was an "eternal necessity imposed by natu
re" and the most human and productive of man's activities, the re
volution, according to Marx, has not the task of emancipating the 
labouring classes but of emancipating man from labour; only when 
labour is abolished can the "realm of freedom" supplant the "realm 
of necessity". For the "realm of freedom begins only where labour 
determined through want and external utility ceases," where "the 
rule of immediate physical needs" ends (p. 104, as cited in Marx, 
1974, p. 873). A workplace and a shared space are available, but 
these labourers do not possess the privileges or prestige that the 
homo faber possesses through work. Through Marx, according to 
Arendt (1998), 'We are left with the rather distressing alternative 
between productive slavery and unproductive freedom' (p. 105). To 
Arendt, the unproductive labour that keeps us alive is necessary and 

108
that work lacks Marx's emphasis on historical necessity. She stated 
that a reconceptualisation of class does not, unlike Marx, seek to 
understand the economic underpinnings of class relations and the 
mode of production but instead seeks to focus on the human con
ditions beyond it. Arguably, as capitalism reinvents itself-as Marx 
predicted it would-and takes on new shapes, Arendt's theories have 
been abstract enough to remain relevant. These critics often point 
towards how the modes of production have changed during neolibe
ralism, from tangible products to cognitive work (see, for example, 
Willis, 2011). This comment is not on the contemporary relevance 
of Marx himself, as the human condition that Arendt describes ex
ists within the capitalist system, which Marx defined. 
 
Furthermore, the nature of work has, as previously noted, 
changed over the last decades. Labour and economic production 
has increasingly become socialised (Hardt & Negri, 2017, p. 91), 
which means that labour would lose some if its material base (Marx, 
1979, p. 928). The landscape of work and labour that Arendt de
fined draws upon a clear divide between housework and blue-col
lar and white-collar works, and to her, this leaves repercussions to 
the public. The organisations that surrounded Arendt in the 1950s 
operated under a clear hierarchical structure, and the rules were 
often rigid but transparent. At present, working arrangements are 
more flexible, as Huybrechts et al. (2018) noted, and the bounda
ries between work and labour are blurred. Indeed, the boundaries 
between our domestic lives and our professional lives have shifted 
dramatically in the past two decades. Arendt's notion that the poli
tical lies in the shared space-that outside our homes and our own 
heads-suggests that this neoliberal shift presents a threat to the 
very nature of democracy. As political spaces disappear from our 
world(s), we risk a democratic deficit. 
4.1.3 LABOUR
 
Introduced-albeit briefly-above were three of Arendt's 
key terms: labour, work and action. By extricating from the world 

109
that is public and private, Arendt laid the foundation for us to un
derstand her distinction between labour and work. Labour, within 
Arendt's writings, consists of fruitless, futile and repetitive actions 
designed to sustain life. It is what we do to feed ourselves and to 
keep ourselves alive. In pre-capitalist society, labour would have 
been work conducted by slaves and poor people. She discussed the
se labouring people as animal labourans. A king does not labour, 
and the prestige of work lies in that it sets itself apart from dreary 
labour. Labour also lacks all kinds of prestige that we associate with 
world-making work, and since it is 'invisible' and not performed 
publicly, it also lacks political agency. Under capitalism, it is per
haps most recognisable, as the labour of the housewife, the plight of 
whom de Beauvoir (1993) has famously described as the torture of 
Sisyphus: 
'[W]ith its endless repetition: the clean becomes soiled, the 
soiled is made clean, over and over, day after day. The hou
sewife wears herself out marking time: she makes nothing, 
simply perpetuates the present [...]. Eating, sleeping, cleaning 
- the years no longer rise up towards heaven, they lie spread 
out ahead, grey and identical. The battle against dust and 
dirt is never won.' (p. 474) 
 Although the description by de Beauvoir (1993), which 
captures a particularly female experience of being trapped in the 
home, seems to compliment Arendt's understanding of labour as 
something futile and devalued, Arendt's writings on public spaces 
have received critique from many modern and contemporary femi
nist scholars. Arendt's work is deeply rooted in the European (The 
Neo-Aristotelian) philosophical tradition, as engaged as she was in 
dialogues with some of its great minds, such as Kant and Heideg
ger, and through that comes an underlying male-centrist ontology. 
Indeed, feminists have criticised the notion of the human condition 
for precisely prescribing the same male-bodied status quo as femi
nist technoscientists, such as Haraway and Harding, opt. cit., have 
sought to challenge23. Arendt sat polemically in relation to much of 

110
Western feminism-some have argued that she does so simply by 
being a political philosopher and a woman without being an outs
poken feminist24. This factor presents a challenge for this particular 
thesis, as a 'neutral' political standpoint does not marry well with 
the ethos of feminist technoscience, where the embodied political 
standpoint is celebrated. However, one mitigating factor within this 
critique around the standpoint is that Arendt's work has concepts 
that can facilitate a discussion around exactly this dilemma. For 
Arendt, the space between people is the politically potent space; this 
is where politics happen. However, this position is not a geograp
hical position, per se, as she noted that 'action and speech create a 
space between the participants which can find its proper location 
almost anywhere and anytime' (Arendt, 1998, p. 198). Through ac
tion and speech, we make space, and we make the political. Butler 
(2011) reflected on this in her lecture Bodies in Alliance and the Po
litics of the Street, where she discussed the demonstrations in Tahir 
Square during the Egyptian Revolution in 2011. She described the 
streets as the political area, as we very well know them: The streets 
are a shared public space, and the scene of demonstrations, occupa
tions and other political manifestations. However, it is also a public 
space made possible by the back streets and alleyways. Tahir Squ
are would not have been possible without the toilets. Butler (2011) 
said that 'Human action depends upon all sorts of support-it is 
always supported action' (2011, N.a) and that 'To rethink the spa
ce of appearance in order to understand the power and effect of 
public demonstrations for our time, we will need to understand the 
bodily dimensions of action, what the body requires, and what the 
body can do, especially when we must think about bodies together, 
23 Critical theories around social reproduction, that is, how society makes 
and remakes the social constructions of people through conditions anchored in 
race, class and gender. A Marxist reading of this might address how the eco
nomic base affects the superstructure (Heinrich, 2004); however, social repro
duction theory expands far beyond this (For an overview see Federici, 2019).

24 This argument-about whether Hannah Arendt defined herself as a feminist, and 
whether she was one anyways-has sparked debates for decades. While this debate is in
teresting, I will refrain from entangling myself with it here (For a more in-dept discussion, 
I recommend Markus, 1987).

111
what holds them there, their conditions of persistence and of power' 
(2011, N.a). This take is problematic in the sense that it paints the 
picture that all bodies are the same, which Butler (2011) suggested 
that they are not. Rather, the body of the public sphere is the neutral 
male body and it, according to Butler (2011), is unsupported. The 
female body in the public sphere is: 
'female, ageing, foreign, or childish, and pre-political. [...] 
Arendt understood this capacity to bring something into be
ing as a function of political speech and action. Indeed, when 
male citizens enter into the public square to debate questions 
of justice, revenge, war, and emancipation, they take the il
luminated public square for granted as the architecturally 
bounded theatre of their speech. Their speech becomes the 
paradigmatic form of action, physically cut off from the pri
vate domicile, itself shrouded in darkness and reproduced 
through activities that are not quite action in the proper and 
public senses.' (Butler, 2011, N.a.)
 
Using Arendt's notions of labour and work illuminates this 
discrepancy between the perceived plurality of the political space 
and the worlds that this space creates-while resting entirely on a 
substructure of exclusion and silenced, marginalised voices. From 
this, we may conclude that the political is impossible to untwine 
from its installed base, or, as feminist scholars have argued for cen
turies, the personal is political.
4.1.4 ANECDOTE: THE WET BLANKET
 
During the Livskonceptet project in Linero (Lund), the 
group of local residents who were invited into the co-lab took inspi
ration from other local groups in other places. They looked at how 
organisations of citizens from near and far had worked with welco
ming and integrating newly arrived into an area and hoped to find 
a strategy that they themselves could copy in Linero. One of the ex

112
amples that the group found inspirational was the model 'Together 
for X' (X being interchangeable for the city or area that the group 
would operate in, for example, Together for Dalby25). The model 
showed that local citizens would come together and create a support 
network for the newly arrived, gathering donations for clothes and 
furniture before they arrived, greeting them with a welcome basket 
upon arrival, introducing themselves and providing a list of telepho
ne numbers that they can call if they needed directions, a lift, help 
with translating official documents and so on. The idea was that the 
local community would come and patch together what they viewed 
as fragmented help from the local municipality. One outcome of the 
co-lab was that a Together for Linero was initiated with the local 
library, the local church and Save The Children. 
 
In an interview with the Livskonceptet project manager, a 
senior staff member from Lund municipality, I asked her what she-
as a representative of the municipality-thought of the initiative. She 
expressed her hearted support. I asked if Lund Municipality could 
offer any kind of support for the groups, financial or otherwise, and 
she declined. She told me that she feared that any involvement from 
the municipality would be a 'wet blanket' on the citizens' engage
ment, that it would dampen their engagement . And that is why, she 
said, it was better if they did their thing, and the municipality did 
theirs. 
 
Considering Arendt's notions of labour and work, as des
cribed in the section above, The project manager's comment about 
the 'wet blanket' poses an interesting question of what kind of re
sponsibility the municipality has regarding maintaining the spaces 
between their services (for example, the employment services, lang
25 In Dalby, which is not far from Linero, a trailblazing group exists, whe
re local residents viewed integration as a two-way street (Nihlén, 2018), and 
they served as inspiration for the group in Linero. Representatives from Dal
by also came to Linero the help them set up their own 'Toghether for'-hub.

113
uage schools etc.). To elaborate, the services provided by the muni
cipality were not deemed sufficient by the local residents to integrate 
new people in the area. The municipality performed work, but ma
intenance to combine the services was also required. The organisa
tions Together For provided infrastructuring labour that facilitated 
the municipality's work. The project manager's comment hints at the 
assumption that people only did this because they had to, and if the 
municipality had stepped in, the local residents would have backed 
out. As a part of the argument around feral infrastructuring, I would 
like to entertain the notion that it might actually be the other way 
around: because the local residents performed the labour, the muni
cipality was able to step back. 
 
In the next section of this chapter, I will discuss the insepa
rability of praxis (a life-leading activity) from poiesis (a generative 
activity) to argue that the substructure of a project is absolutely as 
essential as the superstructure. In other words, the feral is part of the 
infrastructure.
4.1.5 ACTION 
 
In Arendt's writings, the three terms-work, labour and 
action-come together and contribute to making the political. 
However, she strived for a hierarchy between terms (Higgins, 2010). 
A life of sheer labour, she argued, would not be a fully human life. 
And while-boiled down to its very essence-most second-wave fe
minists may agree with this argument, the point of conflict lies in 
what the argument does. To devalue labour with its endless repeti
tions and thankless maintenance is to think that only the building or 
construction of a thing (material and/or social) is what matters. In the 
Anthropocene age, we know that endless production is not always 
the best course forward (Haraway, 2016). Within this thesis, I will 
take hold of these particular concepts of Hannah Arendt's-work 
and labour (and action and speech)-to discuss the political main
tenance labour that is performed in public through infrastructuring. 

114
I argue that praxis (a life-leading activity) is impossible to separate 
from poiesis (a generative activity).
 
Action is the term that we associate perhaps most readily 
with the doing of politics: It can be used 'for anything from leafle
ting in front of a supermarket to shutting down a global summit' 
(Graeber, 2009, p. 359). Arendt's notion of action is one of free
dom, outside the professional/occupational realm (Higgins, 2010). 
However, I questioned the possibility of action as something extra
social. Actions, and the agency to act, are relational and, as such, 
as much part of their socio-material setting as they are producing 
it. As Wainwright (1994) noted about direct action, 'disobedience' 
is not an isolated event but 'clues to underlying structures and re
lationships which are not observable other than through the parti
cular phenomena or events that they produce' (p. 7). I argued that 
action both sit in the spaces around us-as Butler (2011) proposed 
above-action is always supported action and that we change the 
spaces around us through work and labour. As such, I diverge from 
Arendt's hierarchical order of work, labour and action. I do not hold 
action above the other two; rather, the opposite. 
 
Huybrechts et al. (2018) argued that the challenge for par
ticipatory design is to reconnect work with its political dimension 
and suggested that for this to be possible, 'design researchers need to 
actively connect the micro-political scale of work environments with 
these other scales and arenas via involving people in collective pro
cesses of dissentual reframing and sense-making of the public. [...] 
The design object of PD that extends beyond IT artefacts to services, 
systems and communities' (p. 4). A perspective which is congruent 
with that of DiSalvo (2015) who, in his book Adversarial Design, ar
gued that contemporary design has a predominance towards politics 
rather than the political. He distinguished between the two through 
Mouffe's (2000) definition of the political as 'The dimension of an
tagonism that is inherent in human relations, antagonism that can 
take many forms and emerge in different types of social relations' (p. 
101). It is a definition congruent with Arendt's shared public space. 
By contrast, politics is the structure that enables governing, such as 

115
laws or democratic apparatuses (DiSalvo, 2015; Mouffe, 2000). Di
Salvo (2015) argued that design has a preference for engaging with 
the latter kind and provided examples of designing for accessibili
ty to voting stations and information: 'aesthetically compelling and 
functionally appropriate ways to support the means of governance' 
(p. 8). DiSalvo (2015) proposed a political design practice that seeks 
to ask critical questions and provoke and encourage action. I argue 
that the aspects within Arendt's writings discussed above-labour 
and work-hold keys to an understanding of infrastructuring that 
makes political design and action possible. 
4.2. FERAL AS UNRULY
 
The use of the word feral here is, of course, not a coinciden
ce. It is, to many, a provocative word as it comes with colonial con
nections. It is a word used by white Europeans to describe the native 
population of colonised territories. It is the word used by the bour
geoisie to describe the poor children of the proletariat. It is a word 
used to describe hysterical women and neurodivergent people. Feral 
is the opposite of the civilised and domesticated: implied in the word 
are notions of uncleanliness and a certain nod towards violence. Fer
al suggests an 'other' and an other that is less than: Perhaps also an 
'other' that is to be pitied or feared. Unless, of course, the feral can 
become domesticated and, as much as possible, be made part of 
the home (although it will never be pure). Historian Crosby (1986) 
suggested that European colonialism set out to recreate itself. The 
colonial project was (needless to say) not a meet-and-greet; it was 
an attempt to tame the 'new worlds' in the image of the 'old world'. 
A racist project born out of hubris and arrogance is set to fail. The 
'new' lands contained ecologies that the colonisers had not predic
ted, could not understand and could not be contained. Countless ta
les exist of colonisers contaminating an eco-system with new species 
(e.g. Clark, 2003; Salaman, 1985) and bringing some species from 
the new land back home. 'Cereals domesticated humans', as written 
by Tsing (2012, p. 145) in her paper Unruly Edges: Mushrooms as 

116
Companion Species, followed Engels' (1972) argument about cul
tivation, domestication and private property. She trailed the story 
from the first cultivations of wheat and barley, a labour that requi
res much more work and local dedication than the foraging practi
ces that preceded them. The biological transformations give rise to 
social ones, as they create material bounds for social hierarchical 
arrangements. A pater was born and:
'It is within this political configuration that both women 
and grain were confined and managed to maximise fertility. 
The grains selected through domestication had big, high-car
bohydrate seeds; high-carbohydrate diets allowed women to 
have more children. Instead of working to limit fertility, as 
most foragers do, people suddenly wanted as many child
ren as possible-not only because of the fetish of fertility 
but also because the family needed more labour for cereals. 
The cereals did not care whether family or non-family raised 
them, and there was no dearth of people, but state-supported 
property encouraged labour inside the family.' (Tsing, 2012, 
p. 146)
Cultivation, according to both Tsing (2012) and Engels (1972), 
brings the vision of a world of plenty, but monocrops are actually 
a huge liability and a risk for farmers. One contemporary example 
of this is the almost complete monocrop monopoly held by the mul
tinational corroboration of Monsanto. Monsanto specialises in ge
netically modified organisms and sells seeds to farmers in countries 
such as India. What makes Monsanto's modified seeds special is that 
they can resist Monsanto's own highly toxic herbicide and pesticide, 
which kills all except the intended plant. Their seeds are also modi
fied to prevent 'brown bagging' (i.e. the traditional practice of far
mers to save some seeds or nuts from this years' crop to plant next 
year) and in effect keep the farmers dependent on Monsanto. This 
factor prevents farmers from alternating the use of the land from 
year to year, depleting the soil, killing the eco-system, poisoning the 
farmers and creating an immense excess of one crop that is only 

117
desirable as a commodity to sell. This effect then makes farmers vul
nerable to fluctuations in the market, as well as in the climate. The 
promise of plenty, of security and prosperity that the private proper
ty had promised only became true for a very small select group. As 
Tsing's (2012) stated, '[P]lantations were the engine of the European 
expansion' (p. 148). Divisions between race and class created an 
illusion of 'purity', of cleanliness and goodness and of its supposed 
polar opposites as 'impure', 'infected' and 'sick'. 
 
Tsing's paper looks at the practice of foraging as a juxtapo
sition to private land ownership and to the production line mentali
ty. In particular, she celebrated mushroom foraging as a living-with 
practice of the unruly mushroom that does not conform to a single 
space, place, time or taste. Tsing (2012) wrote, 'The mushrooms we 
eat congregate at edges' (p. 151) and continued to note that 'Com
mercial mushroom collecting allows us to see the seams of global 
capitalism. Not only are places differentiated and products specific; 
forms of knowledge and resource management are wildly divergent 
and only tentatively connected to the commodity chain' (p. 151)26. 
 
I propose that the analogy of monocropping versus fora
ging applies to urban infrastructuring projects. I believe, as Tsing did 
with her mushroom picking, that if we approach the outside of our 
projects-the feral cities-without attempting to tame or domesti
cate it, it will show us something new: the edges of our professional 
cultures and the seams between the personal and the professional. 
In the introduction of this chapter, I listed a number of participants 
that contributed to the projects with other motives than, for ex
ample, the Amiralsstaden's internal cause of testing methods for city 
planning ('We worked with a group of schoolchildren who wanted 
to make a podcast about their area; with an SFI group who wanted 
to make connections with Swedish natives for language practice; we 
made coffee for agitators who sought a connection into the munici
26 The citation here is from a paper published in 2012, that is, two years be
fore Tsing's (2015) book on the same subject. For an extended argument around 
the fungi ecology and its connections to capitalism see Tsing, A. L. (2015). The 
Mushroom at the End of the World: On the possibility of life in capitalist ruins. 
Princeton University Press.

118
pality; guided students writing dissertations; humoured a film-ma
ker who had a crush on one of our project assistants'). Arguably, 
infrastructures come with an inscribed culture and, therefore, a ru
ling logic (Dittrich et al., 2002). The ruling logic is something that, 
as you may remember from Star and Ruhleder's (1996) definition 
of infrastructure, is something drawn from an installed base and 
something that we may learn through membership. By defining an 
infrastructure, we define not only what a membership entails but 
also who is a member and, most importantly, who is not a member. 
These groups are not the only two groupings within the infrastructu
re, but let us stay with these two poles for now. Because the mem
bers of an infrastructure want to do something to achieve: Within 
infrastructuring, the important suffix of -ing implies a doing of so
mething. A common definition of infrastructuring is that it entails 
a mobilisation of something, but, as Suchman (1994a) suggested, 
it is a doing that departs from a culture and ideology. Now, while 
infrastructuring is implied to a company or a contained project, the 
implications of the inside-outside relationship may not be catastrop
hic. If the infrastructuring actors all prescribe to the same ideology 
and measure value with the same criteria and culture, this may not 
then be a problem. As soon as we start stretching out into the city or 
into communities that do not share the organisational/institutional 
ideology of the infrastructures, a problem arises-both an ethical 
and a practical problem. The foraging approach allows different re
lations to the world around the project. These different relations 
not only apply to the 'unruly' participation within projects, such as 
the ones with diverging motives listed above, or as seen in the anec
dote about Simon below, but also to the overlap between several 
roles that one participant might hold. For example, in my research, I 
have not only been within projects, but I have also been in between 
them. I have been participating in several projects simultaneously 
and come into connection with the broader ecology of projects that 
connect to them, such as, but not exclusive to, the secondary case 
studies. There is also the matter of sitting on several chairs simulta
neously. Anja, who was mentioned primarily as a part of Amiralssta
den, was also a part of Livskonceptet. This would naturally lead to 

119
a level of dilution: we would discuss Amiralsstaden after a Livskon
ceptet meeting, perhaps refrain from trying something in one project 
that did not work in the other and so on and so forth. These blurred 
boundaries could also be seen as a kind of foraging. 
4.2.1 ANECDOTE: SIMON'S BADGE AND THE UNRULY 
PARTICIPANT
 
One of Amiralsstaden's longest-term participants, Simon, 
told me in an interview how important the Amiralsstaden badge had 
been to him. To me, the badge itself was not particularly impressive: 
a laminated piece of paper that contained his name, Amiralsstaden 
and the Malmö municipality. However, to Simon, this badge was a 
receipt of his work with the municipality. He did not have any for
mal qualifications or any previous experience working with partici
patory design or urban planning. However, he told me how he had 
felt taller walking around his neighbourhood with it and how the 
badge had made it easy for him to approach people around him. He 
described a sense of being elevated from the crowd and part of so
mething bigger. And it was not just in his own eyes, he said because 
others treated him differently as well. He told me that his badge had 
made others respect him and that a neighbour who had heard of Si
mon's 'employment' (although Simon was only paid an hourly wage 
for some of his more demanding assignments, most of his work with 
Amiralsstaden was done on a voluntary basis) with Malmö Stad had 
attempted to set him up with the neighbour's daughter. 'A marriage 
proposal!' Simon told me in an interview, 'How about that!' (Inter
view, August 2019). However, what was a delightful turn of events 
to Simon and an amusing anecdote was a source of great stress for 
the project managers. They were concerned that people would think 
that one of their employees used his position to pick up women. 
Now, if this had indeed been what happened, then most of us would 
agree that it would have been problematic, but it was not. It was 
not a 'pure' action on Simon's behalf, as he mixed his own personal 
agenda with the agenda of the project. However, neither was it the 

120
'sickness' that the project managers described it as.
4.3 ANECDOTE: THE KING'S CHAIR
 In the autumn of 2017, while the first two knowledge al
liances of Amiralsstaden were in a flurry of activities, the manage
ment group was informed by Malmö Stad that the project had been 
selected as one of the sights for the upcoming state visit from the 
Swedish king. The management group themselves would not be al
lowed to participate in the king's visit, nor would any of the project's 
participants be allowed to be present or meet the king. The presen
tations of the project would be held by people in higher positions in 
the Malmö Municipality ranks. Determined to make the honour of 
being selected as a flagship project into something more than just a 
disruption (venue closed for a day, mandatory dusting and scrubb
ing before the king's arrival), two of the active knowledge alliances 
were roped into making a project out of the visit. Soon, the idea was 
hatched that the local school would be invited into a co-creation 
session of a throne. A group of second graders accepted the chal
lenge and set about enthusiastically decorating an office chair with 
glitter and flags and other kinds of ornaments fit-they argued-for 
a king. It was, indeed, a pleasant chair. 
 
By special request from the management group, a picture 
was taken of the king sitting in the specially designed chair. The king 
obliged, and the photo was rushed back to the second graders. The 
second graders then announced that they 'had no idea who the old 
man in the photo was' and that they had assumed that 'the king' 
meant Zlatan Ibrahimovich. They were disappointed. 
4.3.1 ANECDOTE: THE KING'S CHAIR
The story of the king's chair has become something classic within the 
Amiralsstaden group. It has been told and retold many times. The 
plot-twist that the young kids rated the famous football player from 

121
Malmö as higher than the king is, in most people's eyes, amusing 
and harmless. I retell it here because I think it illuminates several 
kinds of 'gratitudes' (or lack thereof) that are often present within a 
project. A superficial expression of gratitude for a job well done is 
embodied in the Amiralsstaden section as a showcase project. This 
supposedly says that the project does something right, in the eyes of 
Malmö Municipality, and that they want to show us off. The group 
lowest in the food chain (the second graders) should fail to see the 
value in the 'price' is perhaps not so strange considering that it is a 
token from a world far removed from their own. 
 
Psychoanalysts, such as Klein (1984), have suggested that 
gratitude is an inborn feeling. Similar to feelings of envy, gratitude is 
guided by a moral understanding of 'good' and 'bad'. Fanon (2001) 
famously described the colonial warfare on the minds of the colo
nised: how the Algerians in the aftermath of the Algerian War had 
their self-identity altered. In his book The Wretched of the Earth 
(1961/2001), Fanon (2001) wrote that 'Europe is literally a creation 
of the Third World, the wealth which smothers her is that which 
was stolen from the underdeveloped peoples. [...] So when we hear 
the head of a European state declare with his hand on his heart that 
he must come to the aid of the poor underdeveloped peoples, we do 
not tremble with gratitude' (p. 81). Fanon (2001) proposed that not 
only should colonised people not feel grateful for access to what is 
rightfully theirs, but they should also demand compensation for the 
time when it was denied to them. The colonised people, he argued, 
only accept the offers of the colonisers because they believe and in
ternalise the colonisers' views on themselves. 
 
The power imbalances present in the anecdote about the 
king's chair are less violent than in the relationship between the co
loniser and the colonised. Still, the power imbalances are there, and 
an implicit understanding suggests that the participant in a project 
should accept the reward of a project based on what is valuable to 
the organiser/facilitator. To do differently would be to 'misbehave' 
or 'overspill' (Michael, 2012b) the intention of the project/event. 
This feature is illustrated by the above anecdote about Simon and 
his badge, in which Simon found other rewards for his work and 

122
was penalised for it. 
Gratitude, at its best, holds within it an appreciation for the other 
part and an appreciation for their deeds. Gratitude may be concep
tualised as a 'moral affect', similar to emotions, such as empathy 
and guilt (McCullough et al., 2001). Moral affects can hold so
cio-psychological functions, such as a moral barometer, a motive 
or reinforcing (ibid, p. 249). However, as Klein (1984) argued, it is 
also an emotion that we use to separate a 'moral good' from a 'mo
ral bad', and these poles are painted by the social world around us. 
Therefore, gratitude, at worst, cements social hierarchies and pushes 
the parties further apart. For better or worse, ponder what would 
happen if we navigated the affective infrastructures with reciprocal 
solidarity rather than generosity and gratitude. Instead of being gra
teful or expecting gratitude from others, reciprocal 'solidarities are 
formed through multidirectional relationships-connections that 
require a type of self-reflexive work on the parts of both the per
son who is directly impacted by the very structures s/he is moving 
against' (Atshan & Moore, 2014, p. 680). Reciprocal solidarity is 
necessary if we take the notion of many world-worlds (Law, 2015) 
seriously and understand that the foundational worldview or ide
ology that our actions depart from is not necessarily shared. What 
we find in the place of singularity is an intersectional, reciprocal 
relationship. We need to learn to navigate the affective infrastructu
res of projects with a sense of allyship that is sensitive towards our 
differences. Later on in this thesis, I will suggest that collaborative 
anecdotalization is one way of symbiotically furthering these kinds 
of bonds.
4.4 ANECDOTE: ABOUT TAKING RESPONSIBILITY FOR 
POTENTIAL FUTURES
 In the early spring of 2020 (only weeks before the COVID-19 
pandemic hit Sweden), I was queuing for coffee when I received a 
phone call from a colleague/acquaintance. Audibly stressed, she is 
asking what I know about the plans for the new development plans 

123
for her residential area. 'Nothing', I say, but as the conversation 
went on, it became clear that I did, in fact, know these plans. As it 
turns out, I was one of the original creators of these plans. Now, my 
colleague on the phone is asking me when the proposed construction 
work is set to start and if it is too late to register a complaint. She 
cannot live like this, she says, the new houses will be much too close 
to her home. She will have to move. 
 
Unknowingly to me, this story began two years earlier at 
a workshop. As part of Amiralsstaden, we were encouraging local 
residents to imagine new futures, to leave behind the known present 
and imagine the area as they wished for it to be in 30 years' time. 
The result of the workshop enumerated several interesting conver
sations about the current state of things; strengthened relationships 
between the public sector workers and the local residents; a few 
visualisations and some concrete suggestions on how the area might 
be improved. As a Ph. D. researcher attached to the project, I wrote 
a quick summary and a reflection of the events and handed them 
over to the project's communication manager, who added them to 
the project's website on the municipality website. They remained 
there until a year later when the project finished: Reports were writ
ten, and presentations were delivered to the municipality, where we 
accounted for the strengths and weaknesses of our work. 
 
Another year later, and I am standing with my phone in 
one hand and a coffee in the other, as my colleague explains to me 
how she has found these plans, and how she has searched for days, 
without luck, for someone within the municipality to speak to. Se
eing as we worked at the same university, she managed to get hold 
of my contact details and was now on the phone, asking how long 
before she had to leave her home. Of course, none of the imagina
tions that the workshop produced two years ago were designed to 
be built. At least not without proper consultation, without meeting 
the regulations in the municipality's detailed development plan or 
without the approval of concerned authorities. However, without 
the context of the project, the intentions of the drawings were uncle
ar. Left, as they were in the municipality's cluttered digital archives, 
the imaginations that we had produced were open to interpretation 

124
by anyone who happened upon them. While I managed to convince 
my colleague that she would not have to move, I could not help but 
wonder how many people, like her, had found the plans-and been 
terrified? People who did not work at the university and no internal 
phonebook to consult who did not have anyone to ask. People who 
may even have made plans based on our imaginations; perhaps some 
of them had already moved? 
4.4.1 FERAL AS A NAIVE NATALITY
 Infrastructuring is already a practice of plenty. It entails 
bringing together visions with physical restraints, merging techni
ques and practices from different disciplines and people-all kinds 
of different people. Bucciarelli (1994) stated that 'infrastructuring 
in these terms is a dense interwoven fabric that is, at the same time, 
dynamic, thoroughly ecological, even fragile' (p. 131). The anec
dote above can teach us many things about co-design. I previously 
published an article about it (Smedberg, 2021a) to discuss the Aren
tian concept of bounded imagination. There, I looked at the original 
workshop and the ideas its participants generated as bounded (i.e. 
grounded in their reality, established through the group's conversa
tions) and that the discovery of the ideas by someone outside of the 
group made the ideas lose their grounding. This, I argued, made the 
ideas lose their grounding and therefore become irrelevant or even 
threatening. In the article, I drew the conclusion that a temporal 
element to co-design workshops is available for those who facilitate 
them. In this thesis, I will focus on another lesson that the anecdote 
highlights, which is that we can only co-design to the best of our 
abilities. That is, we can only conduct ourselves well, ethically, so far 
that we understand what it is we are engaging with. Here, we were 
engaged in political design, challenging dominant practices of citi
zen engagement. That in and of itself is not a 'safe space', indicating 
that either participants or facilitators will likely overstep invisible 
lines or unspoken rules. The participants in the above-mentioned 
anecdote did not overstep any more boundaries than that of taking 
part in a political workshop. In addition, I argue that the co-desig
ners in the story (myself included) reflected on their role towards 

125
the participants. I believe we took several measures to ensure that 
the workshop would be mutually beneficial for both facilitators and 
participants, and I believe that we took several measures to be ac
countable for the outcome. What we failed to consider in this instan
ce was that the outcomes of the workshop might travel and be met 
by other feral participants. 
 
I would like to draw a parallel between naïveté and the 
Arendtian notion of natality. Natality, in Arendt's (1998) definition, 
is the condition of having been born and from this so-called condi
tion stems the 'capacity to begin' (p. 247), to make something, to 
work or to labour. Natality is a condition of being connected to the 
public and to others, but without necessarily having been shaped 
by a membership (yet). It holds a capacity for action, but without 
having learned the rules that come with the membership. Arendt has 
notoriously left the notion of natality somewhat ambiguous (Tot
schnig, 2017)27 but placed next to naïveté, it makes an interesting 
comment on something that is often overlooked: The naïve may be 
described as inexperienced, raw or immature (indeed, these are sy
nonyms to the word), but it also holds a tremendous capacity in 
what it might yet become. 
 
In today's society, which is a networked society (Star & 
Bowker, 2006), infrastructures rest upon infrastructures and plat
forms upon platforms (Light & Miskelly, 2019). These networks, 
infrastructures and platforms are no more neutral than the Fordist 
work lines. Coincidentally, Fordism never disappeared; it morphed 
into post-fordism to new public management and other work lines. 
However, these are no less ideological. Naivety holds the capacity 
to act within ideologically contaminated (see Ahmed,2004) infra
structures. The job of the participatory designer within the system 
27 Some scholars have argued that Arendt never fully explained how we move 
from the 'condition of being born' to having the 'capacity to begin' (Markell, 
2006; Totschnig, 2017). Some have argued that what Arendt is trying to achieve 
with this notion is to establish a link between nature and politics (Birmingham, 
2003; Jay, 1985), whereas yet others treat it as a metaphor (e.g. Beiner, 1984) 
that does not need to be fully explained. I have chosen to read Arendt's words 
crassly and understand them to mean that by being born, we are in the world 
with other people and things, which is the minimum criterium for action and 
thus, we have the capacity to act. 

126
is then to invite the 'feral' participant in, but the human capacity 
to begin is something feral participants already have. Within Ami
ralsstaden, we tried to keep the question 'who participates in whose 
process' in mind (Smedberg, 2019b) to remind ourselves of all the 
various processes going on around us-affecting and inspiring us-
but that were too informal to 'count'. 
4.5 CLOSING REMARKS (AND AN ANECDOTE ABOUT 
THE LIBRARIAN IN ROSENGÅRD)
 In this chapter, I demonstrate the ideological basis of in
frastructures. The inevitable constraints and affordances that their 
ideological basis brings inform what 'good' versus 'bad' participa
tion entails. This concept can serve to invalidate certain groups and 
behaviours from the infrastructuring process. 
 
A fortunate few are granted the privilege of building so
mething entirely new based on the ideology of their choice. They are 
the architects and the 'heroes' that sit above the everyday. However, 
most of us are all trotting our day-to-day lives within structures (so
cial and material) designed by others. Our wages may depend on
