these systems, our social relations may be anchored within them, or 
the agency to affect our surroundings may be tied to them. 
 
The task of the infrastructuring practitioner, then, becomes 
one of maintenance of labour in the Arendtian sense. It becomes a 
commitment to care for all those who-in one way or another-
are attached to a structure. Democracy is a fluid process; once it 
stagnates, it dies. Infrastructuring is the labour required to keep the 
infrastructures democratic, moving, changing and developing. Infra
structuring of workplaces or information systems begs that we keep 
lifting in those participants who can affect it, those whose perspec
tive has been marginalised. To do so, we must recognise all 'tiers' of 
an infrastructure: the social rules as much as the material constra
ints. 
 
In the following chapter, I seek to illuminate the affecti
ve plain of infrastructuring labour as a source of both constraints 
and affordances. This quality is difficult to quantify and validate. I 
remember when I was working with the Rosengård library, where 

127
Rose, one of the managers, explained to me how many roles she acts 
out in a day. She said she would, beyond her job description as a li
brarian, be a social worker, a homework assistant, a career guidance 
councillor, a psychologists and conflict manager, a cleaner, a team 
leader, an organiser, a research assistant and an entertainer. Rose 
told me that one mother would come in to the weekly story telling 
with her young child and said that she would come there with her 
children every week because she herself had come to the storytime 
sessions as a child. 'How do you measure that kind of value?' Rose 
asked me, 'The value that spans over a generation. All Malmö Stad 
sees is the number of people that walk in through the door, or how 
many books have been borrowed in a month'.
 
Rose worked with maintenance and care. Her infra
structuring was a labour, and a proportion of that was emotional 
labour. Her question of why the organisation did not see this labour, 
and in turn did not value it, will serve as a starting point for the fol
lowing chapter.

128
5. AFFECTIVE INFRASTRUCTURING 
(ANECDOTE: SEE YOU TOMORROW)
 
'Really,' Anja says to me, 'The most important lesson I have lear
ned here is "see you tomorrow"'. In the autumn of 2019, I invited her to 
the university to reflect on her time with Amiralsstaden. The project, of 
which Anja had been one of the two project leaders, was drawing to a 
close, and we had set ourselves the task to map and talk through it. I ask 
Anja what she thinks her role has been, hinting at a not-too-uncommon 
discrepancy between official assignments and actual work. Anja tells me 
that when she and her partner first were assigned to the project, there 
was a period of 'translating' the official assignment into something that 
could actually operate on the ground. She tells me they had to do a lot 
of reading to understand terms, such as 'knowledge alliances', but even 
with a definition available, it was not obvious how to practically make 
these happen. She tells me that they have often used the phrase 'building 
the bridge as we walk it' within Amiralsstaden, as they have figured out 
how to proceed one step at the time. I ask her if this, then, has been her 
role, to decide on the next step of the project, but she tells me 'no'. She 
told me that she never was the project-that the core of the project was 
always participation. And so, her role was to invite people in, make 
space for them to be in and assist them in what they wanted to do. And 
she says, 'Really, the most important lesson I have learned here is "see 
you tomorrow"'. She explained to me that one of the biggest parts in 
facilitating participation is to be there, almost unconditionally. Regard
less, if an argument happened during the day or whether today was not 
so productive, 'We build relationships by coming back tomorrow and 
trying again'. 

129
5.1 INFRASTRUCTURING AS LABOUR
 
Anja's work within Amiralsstaden was infrastructuring 
work: she was there as a mobiliser and an organiser, tasked with 
making things happen. In the first section of this chapter, however, 
I will argue that her work as an infrastructuring practitioner was in 
fact not work at all but labour. The distinction is important when 
we consider broad and wicked (Buchanan, 1992) problems, such 
as citizenship and democracy, in particular from the perspective of 
the issue of agency and how this may be addressed when articula
tion is understood as an inherently political action (Arendt, 1958). 
To make this distinction, I will draw upon Arendt's (1958) work, 
particularly The Human Condition. In the subsequent two parts of 
this chapter, I will first demonstrate how affect occurs within infra
structuring processes, following scholars from the field of sociolo
gy of emotions, most prominently Horchchild (2012) and Ahmed 
(2014), and second, I will discuss emotional labour in relation to 
participatory design practice.
 
In a paper, Huybrecht et al. (2018) explored how the reading 
of Arendt's work may reactivate the political nature of work that 
has historically been significant to participatory design. Huybrecht 
et al. (2018) showed their argument by describing what they term 
the 'crisis of Fordism' through Gramsci's (1934) essay Americanism 
and Fordism. Gramsci (1934) explained that 'The real purpose of 
American society [is] - replacing in the worker the old psycho-phy
sical nexus of qualified professional work, which demanded acti
ve participation, intelligence, fantasy and initiative, with automatic 
and mechanical attitudes' (as cited in Huybrecht et al., 2018, p. 2). 
Huybrecht et al. (2018) highlighted Gramsci's (1934) argument that 
'Ford-ism brings an idea of deskilled labour in which workers and 
work environments lose touch with their political role in society'. 
The first half of the 20th century, at the time when Fordism was roo
ting in the western world, was a time of increased mass production, 
continuous industrialisation, and contemporary with a seemingly 
unrestrained technological development. The ideas of how society 
should manage these new challenges were plentiful, and the spectra 
ranged from anarchic to authoritarian. The crisis of Fordism, Huy
brecht et al. (2018) argued, was felt in the 1960s when the system of 

130
operations had been established enough to afford a structural under
standing of it. Also, during the 1960s, we can first speak of partici
patory design emerging from within the same industrial terrain (see 
Ehn, 1988a): The discipline responding in part to the admonition 
of Gramsci (1934)-that the skilled worker was increasingly being 
pacified-and in part to the consequence [sic] of Fordism in the wor
kers movement and the Union movements. To clarify, the worker's 
movement is not to be reduced to a mere consequence of Fordism, 
as the movement has a history dating back to early industrialisation, 
but in particular, challenges were posed by Fordism within the wor
ker's movement.
 
Recognising the issues pinned to post-fordism, Huybrecht et 
al. (2018) found themselves in congruence with Arendt's caution of 
the rapid enlargement of the private sphere. To effectively approach 
this matter, they built on Manzini's (2015) model for mapping acti
ve and collaborative encounters. On the horizontal axis, Huybrecht 
et al. (2018) found the 'creation of agoras' (p. 7), which was much 
consistent with the notion of use-before-use, as described above. 
This concept suggests that the facilitation of a meeting space where 
interaction could lead to defined interests and a shared envisioning 
of the future use (in this case, of a particular space). On the vertical 
axis, Huybrecht et al. (2018) found 'the challenge of this research 
[...] to co-produce instruments for "action"'. The PD researchers 
did this by undertaking concrete democratic design experiments by 
making design maps, design scenarios and design tools (ibid, p. 8).
Through designing, not the thing but the platform and tools, the 
physical and social conditions of those who will do the designing/
infrastructuring will be constituted. According to Huybrecht et al. 
(2018) 'The design process was not a quest for a next solution for 
the controversy of work, to fix societal issues. It intended to cri
tically reframe the relationship between private and public and to 
build the participants' confidence in their ability to act together and 
to care together for their common "interests"' (p. 9). In combining 
Arendt's notions of 'work' and 'action', Huybrecht et al. (2018) pro
vided participatory design with a new vocabulary that the notion of 
the political workplace can be discussed, or in their own words, (re)
activated the political dimension of work. 

131
5.1.1 MAINTENANCE REQUIRES SKILLS
 
Words are important. Within them resides our ability to per
ceive and make sense of the world around us. It follows that when 
a new vocabulary is introduced, we take it with us and re-examine 
the surroundings. In this particular case, a new vocabulary for the 
political dimensions of work has repercussions for how we perceive 
the role of the designer and indeed for infrastructuring in and of it
self. Returning to the definitions of infrastructures provided by Star 
and Ruhleder (1996), we find that its defining features point not to 
the work of an individual artisan or craftsperson but as a collective, 
dialectic labour. Even within the framework of developing digital in
frastructures, such as the WORM community, infrastructuring can 
never be the prestigious work of, for example, one genius program
mer or designer. Arendt's notion of labour, instead, is the more app
ropriate term for the maintenance work that infrastructuring entails. 
In the beginning of this chapter, I shared Anja's reflections around 
her role within Amiralsstaden and how she said that her most im
portant task was to say 'See you tomorrow' to the people who came 
and contributed to the project. Infrastructuring within the public sp
here and the public sector is a craft-but it is a craft of collaborative 
maintenance. 
 
Schön's (1982) notion of the reflective practitioner makes 
the case that knowledge-in-action is the elusive kind of knowledge 
that comes with intuition. The designer, for example, has the kind 
of know-how of a material that: knowing how it bends, behaves 
and resists through experience. The experience then grows into a 
repertoire of approaches and methods adapted to the work of the 
practitioner. The work that Anja performed within Amiralsstaden 
required the same kind of skills as described by Schön (1982) abo
ve: an intuitive knowledge-in-action, a sensitivity to the processes 
around her and a metaphorical quiver of arrows (Sennett, 2009) to 
be used at the appropriate time. However, the craft that she practices 
builds and maintains relationships and keeps the processes around 
her alive-a work, of course, but also a life-sustaining labour. 

132
5.2 ANECDOTE: MEETING THE PUBLIC 
 
The project management group for Livskonceptet knew that 
they would have to meet the public in the area sooner rather than 
later. A new block of houses for newly arrived families, albeit tem
porary, would most notably affect the local school, and as such, it 
seemed fitting to host the public meeting at Vikingaskolan in Lin
nero. 
 
The timing of the meeting was crucial, the management 
group reasoned. If they held the meeting too early in the process, 
many questions that cannot be answered would be asked, and they 
would come off as unprofessional and untrustworthy. Furthermo
re, the public could 'spiral' after the meeting, and in the worst-case 
scenario, mobilise against the construction and establishment of the 
new housing project. 
 
If they held the meeting too late, however, information could 
leak out before they had a chance to speak to the public themselves. 
This would give them considerably less control over the narrative. It 
could also give the public the impression that the municipality was 
trying to hide something, making them suspicious of the project or 
making them think that the municipality did not value the local re
sident's thoughts and feelings. 
 
As I watched the management group prepare for the public 
meeting, I was picturing them like army officers going into battle. 
Between themselves, they prepared strategies for different kinds of 
criticisms that might arise during the meeting, strategies on how to 
keep the tone of the presentation 'warm and professional' so that 
those attending would leave with a sense of trust and confidence in 
their work. If the critique was too harsh, how could they redirect the 
discussion? What kind of fika would they serve? 
 
On the evening of the meeting, a man spoke. He raised his 
hand and talked about how he was worried that the introduction of 
these 20-odd families into the area would affect the study environ
ment for his daughter and how this, in turn, would come to affect 
her future. This kind of question was a code red for the group. Not 
because they were unable to answer him. Rationally, it would be 
easy to disseminate his reasoning apart and to say that his antici
pation was widely exaggerated. Out of 20 families, 5 of them may 

133
have children in the same age group as his daughter, and if so, one or 
two of these children may be placed into her class. However, even if 
these two children would turn out to be the worst of troublemakers, 
although this again seems improbable, this would unlikely affect the 
man's daughter to such an extent that the daughter's 'future' would 
be in jeopardy. But more importantly, beside the man's argument 
was the very act of pleading to his fellow parents through fear and 
with an emotional argument that they could relate to. He spoke out 
as a father with a legitimate, loving concern for his daughter. He 
found a connection with those in the room who shared his experien
ce of being a parent and then let the new unknown families stand 
as signifiers of his fear, leaving the other parents to fill in the blanks 
of what might happen, any anticipated future hurt that this might 
cause them. 
5.2.1 INFRASTRUCTURES AS (PARTIALLY) AFFECTIVE
 The incident at Vikingasskolan in December 2017 brought 
affect to my attention. Undeniably, contradictions occurred within 
the actions of the management group: a desire to work democrati
cally but not too democratic, being approachable but not too app
roachable, and being in control without appearing as the one being 
in control. These dances within the organisational strategy seemed 
to me to be motivated by emotions: the emotions of the management 
group and the emotions of the public. It seemed to consume as much 
time as the more tangible work and, quite rightly, as infrastructuring 
is a practice of creating alignments (Ehn, 2008). Designing democra
tically and understanding that the designer or the design-researcher 
are not roles of authority to be claimed but one actor in the social 
assembly around us (Latour, 1983) as a situated actor is not an easy 
task. This requires a practice that can sit within the communities. 
Feminist scholar Ahmed (2014) suggested that emotions function 
both as a glue or a stickiness (p. 91) that holds social alignments 
together or apart and also as the act of binding. When we come into 
contract with other people and groups, Ahmed (2014) explained 
that 'Things become sticky as an effect of encountering other sticky 
things' (ibid). Ahmed (2014) further argued that this stickiness re

134
produces through movement, and by placing this emphasis on the 
repetition and movement that generates emotional networks, Ah
med (2014) showed the reader how emotions can be understood as 
affective economies. 
 
The incident at Vikingaskolan made me ask how these af
fective economies operate within the labour of infrastructuring. To 
answer that question, one must reassert that emotions are only one, 
of many dimensions that make up the infrastructural entanglements, 
and that emotions come as much-or maybe more!-as affordances 
as they come as problems. The affective tier is therefore seen as a 
matter of concern (Latour, 2005a). Participatory design has worked 
extensively with the challenge of designing from matters of concern 
(e.g. Andersen et al., 2015)-it takes heed of tacit knowledge and 
continuously talks back with its situation. It does not operate in 
a vacuum; it is always relational and 'sunk into' an installed base 
(Ehn, 2008, p. 5). Ehn (2008) stated that 'Infrastructuring can never 
be de-contextualised, even if the context is unclear from the begin
ning' (p. 6). 
 
Emotions are yet another dimension of entanglement, and 
they may in many cases prove to be the glue holding them together. 
Ahmed (2014) wrote that 'Stickiness then is about what objects do 
to other objects-it involves a transference of affect-but it is a re
lation of 'doing' in which there is not a distinction between passi
ve and active, even though the stickiness of one object might come 
before the stickiness of other, such that the other seems to cling to 
it' (p. 91). To fully appreciate Ahmed's (2014) stance on the stick
iness of emotions, the sociology of emotions must be understood. 
While the study of emotions has historically resided in the hands 
of psychologists, 'A sociological theory of emotions must be that 
an extremely large class of human emotions results from real, an
ticipated, imagined or recollected outcomes of social relationships' 
(Kemper, 1978, p. 32). It is a field of study that has proliferated 
over the last decades (Bericat, 2015) and, in turn, has bifurcated 
into theoretical perspectives (for a literature review, see Turner & 
Strets, 2005). Here, we find theories of rituals (drawn from Durk
heim, 1912, elaborated by e.g. Collins, 2014), interactionist theories 
(e.g. Turner, 2002) and the theories of power and status (Kemper, 

135
1978.) to mention a few (cf. Turner & Strets, 2006). Within this 
thesis, I follow the work of R. Collins (1975, 1993), Hochschild 
(1979, 1983) and Ahmed (2004, 2014). Departing from R. Collins's 
(1993) query into the rational actor perspective, which he conjoins 
with Durkheim's notion of 'precontractual solidarity'28, suggesting 
that 'that exchanges based on rational self-interest cannot even take 
place unless there is a prior framework of values that established the 
rules within which such exchanges occur. Thus rational action might 
appear to be only a portion of human social action and possibly 
a subordinate part' (p. 204). R. Collins (1993) presented a theory 
that binds emotions to rationality and rationality to emotions and 
elaborated that the value oriented, which we might disproportio
nally credit the so-called rational actor, is determined by interaction 
rituals (or IRs). Interaction rituals, according to R. Collins, create 
symbols of group membership, allowing a membership to be prac
ticed. A positive, successful interaction ritual will fill its members 
with energy and positive emotions, whereas a failed negative ritual 
drains the members of positive emotions and may cause rifts in the 
relationships (R. Collins, 2014). R. Collins (1993) wrote: 
 
'By recognizing emotional solidarity with a group as the pri
mary good in social interaction, it is possible to see all such 
value-oriented behaviours as rationally motivated towards 
optimizing this good. Because IRs vary in the amount of so
lidarity they provide, and in their costs of participating, there 
is a market for ritual participation that shapes the distribu
tion of individual behaviour.' (R. Collins, 1993, p. 205). 
 In this way, IRs not only become an integral part of com
modified goods and services but also create a market for ritual soli
darity (ibid. p. 210). Hochschild (1979) critiqued R. Collins (1975) 
for equating emotions too much with capacities, which a ritual ap
paratus may 'trigger'. She suggested that this bypasses the indivi
28 Emile Durkheim viewed society as composed of individuals who both 
construct and consume the ruling societal morals, which then create the foun
dations for the collective consciousness of that society. Durkheim believed that 
every society must have some form of pre-contractual solidarity, expressed and 
made tangible by individuals through rituals (Giddens & Sutton, 2010).

136
dual's ability to manage their feelings according to social rules and 
structures (Hochschild, 1979, p. 554). In Hochschild's (1979) paper 
Emotion Work, Feeling Rules, and Social Structure, she elaborated 
on the notions of feeling rules (i.e. the side of ideology that deals 
with emotion) and analysed them from a Marxist-class perspective; 
she does, however, also concur with R. Collins's notion that ideo
logies can be used as a weapon, mobilised by elites through rituals. 
Hochschild (1979) stated that 'Developing his [Collins] view, we can 
add that elites, and indeed social groups in general, struggle to assert 
the legitimacy of their framing rules and their feeling rules' (p. 568). 
Another intersection between Hochschild and R. Collins, although 
perhaps somewhat elusive, is their respective proposals of polarities 
within their theories of emotions. R. Collins, as seen above, propo
sed that interaction rituals are both the source of intra-personal so
lidarity and social mobilisation as well as the source of value-orien
ted behaviour required to mobilise a logic of economic cost/benefit. 
Hochschild (1979) presented this as the 'underside of ideology' (p. 
557), which she explained as an interpretive framework readily defi
ned through framing rules and feeling rules. Hochschild's reading of 
Goffman (1961) suggests that we work to manage our impressions. 
Herein lies one of Hochschild's many dualities: 
'Emotion work differs from emotion "control" or "suppres
sion". The latter two terms suggest an effort merely to stifle 
or prevent feelings. "Emotion work" refers more broadly to 
the actor of evoking or shaping, as well as suppressing, fe
eling in oneself' (p. 561). 
 
Present in the theories of both Hochschild and R. Collins is, 
besides the foundation that emotions are relational, the conceptu
alisation of emotions as something that moves, accumulates and is 
generative. Ahmed (2004, 2014) explained this in terms of affective 
economies. In her book, The Cultural Politics of Emotion, Ahmed 
(2014) used Marxist-feminist economic theories to approach how 
people use emotions to one another (Ahmed, 2004, 2014), under
standing affect as a force beyond emotions 'that serve to drive us 
together' (Ahmed, 2014, p. 207, also cited in Gregg & Seigworth, 
2010, p. 1). Specifically, she has turned to Marx's analysis of the 

137
movement of commodities and money, the M-C-M formula (Marx, 
1976). This is, in Ahmed's (2004) own words, a simple logic: 'The 
movement between signs converts into affect' (p. 120). Through this 
logic, Ahmed traces how emotions, such as hate, love and shame, do 
things: bind us together. She highlighted the accumulative value of 
emotions:
'Objects only seem to have such value, by an erasure of the
se histories, as histories of production and labour. But whi
le Marx suggests that emotions are erased by the value of 
things (the suffering of the worker's body is not visible in 
commodity form), I focus on how emotions are produced.' 
(Ahmed, 2014, p. 11) 
Take, for example, Ahmed's (2014) chapter 'The Organisation of 
Hate' (p. 42-61), where she shows how racialised hate disguised in 
the form of national pride or love for one's country as the glue that 
binds together organisations, such as the EDL or the Ayran Nations. 
The example she gives in the beginning of the chapter is taken from 
the Aryan Nations own website, and it conveys stories from very 
diverse groups that may otherwise not have much in common-the 
mother, the farmer, the white man and so on. The glue becomes 
their hate, as well as their love. Ahmend (2014) said, 'Because we 
love, we hate, and this hate is what brings us together' (p. 43). It 
created an 'us' and 'them' and allowed hate to reside in them. With 
this relational reading of hate, Ahmed argued that the us and the 
them can be read as signs and signifiers of the emotion, further af
fording an understanding of emotions as something that circulates 
and accumulates. In Marx's words, 'The value originally advanced, 
therefore, not only remains intact while in circulation, but incre
ases its magnitude, adds to itself a surplus-value or is valourised. 
And this movement converts it into capital' (Marx, 1976, p. 252, as 
emphasised by Ahmed, 2014, p. 45). To summarise, emotions work 
as affective signs and signifiers that either push us apart or pull us 
together. The movement of these affective values may be read as an 
accumulation of (emotions) capital. Circulating an infrastructure is 
required. This, according to Ahmed, can be shaped and encouraged 
by, for example, the media, by mobilising groups or politicians that 

138
seek to stick certain emotions to specific groups; however, ultima
tely, it becomes an organic process of taking in or on an emotional 
performance. 
 
Looking at Livskonceptet through the sociological lens of 
emotions and affective economies has brought my attention to a 
number of things. One of these has been a perspective on the colab as a place to be infected. A place where publics clash and make 
impressions on each other, build ties and (hopefully) make commit
ments to each other based on their shared ground. By creating a 
group, we place them in front of each other's emotions and, as R. 
Collins (1993) noted, 'Emotions are contagious in a focused group 
situation' (p. 208). From the perspective of the Livskonceptet mana
gement group, emotions had previously been avoided because they 
were concerned that the presumed emotions of negativity from the 
public would be contagious within themselves and that the project 
and its residents would be tainted by it. Arguably, the worry of the 
management group, which prevented them from inviting the public 
into the project in the first place, was one original source of fear 
within itself. Ahmed (2014), in her chapter on The Affective Politics 
of Fear, noted that fear also has a temporal element, which in this 
particular case became apparent due to the continuous postpone
ments of the move-in date. She wrote that 'Fear's relation to the 
object has an important temporal dimension: we fear an object that 
approaches us. Fear, like pain, is felt as an unpleasant form of inten
sity. However, while the lived experience of fear may be unpleasant 
in the present, the unpleasantness of fear also relates to the future. 
Fear involves anticipation of hurt or injury. Fear projects us from 
the present into the future. But the feeling of fear presses us into that 
future as an intense bodily experience in the present' (Ahmed, 2014, 
p. 65). In the case of Livskonceptet, any fear of the public or of the 
management group was strictly this-anticipation of the future-as 
the residents of Livskonceptet were, and in the moment of writing, 
still are yet to arrive. 
 
The father who stood during the meeting in Vikingaskolan 
found a connection with those in the room who shared his expe
rience of being a parent and then let the unknown families stand as 
signifiers of his fear, leaving the other parents to fill in the blanks 

139
of what might happen-any anticipated future hurt. Ahmed (2014) 
described this as an affective economy of fear: 'The movement of 
fear between signs is what allows the object of fear to be genera
ted in the present [...]. The movement between signs allows others 
to be attributed with emotional value, as 'being fearsome' [...] it is 
the movement that intensifies the affect' (p. 67). It was precisely 
this accumulation or movement that the management group was 
fearful of when they refrained from announcing Livskonceptet too 
soon. The preparations made ahead of the first open meeting about 
the project were mostly done to manage and contain any threat of 
fear, barring any movement of it. However, they failed to consider 
that the feelings that separate us also bind us, and the support they 
needed (and the support the residents would eventually need) requi
res these emotions to be in motion. As Mouffe (1998, 2013) previo
usly mentioned, agonism in any democratic space is necessary-an 
agonistic pluralism. What Ahmed described as affective economies 
is not sui generis derived from agonism; however, as agonism also 
sparks movement. While agonism can bind us together and allow us 
to be infected and affected by each other, affective solidarity points 
to a deeper bond or love. Love is not necessarily romantic love for 
one person, but the recognition of what we love as in what matters 
deeply to us: 'It has effects on the textures of everyday life and on 
the intimate 'withness' of social relations' (Ahmed, 2014, p. 140). 
A solidarity built on common interests is how Durkheim's precon
tractual solidarity is formed. Precontractual solidarity elucidates the 
relationship between autonomous individuals and social solidarity. 
Durkheim's theory of solidarity is extensive, and this paper will not 
do it justice, but, notably, he stated that '[Organic] solidarity will 
not be met in isolation' (Durkheim, 1984, p. 148). R. Collins (1993) 
drew on Durkheim's theories of mechanical and organic solidarity 
29 when he proposed that the IRs are determined by value-oriented 
behaviour and that the dynamics of value-oriented behaviour is de
29 Durkheim suggested that solidarity came in two forms: mechanical and 
organic. On the one hand, mechanical solidarity, according to Durkheim, was 
religious or moral homogeneity. It was the most common form of solidarity in 
societies with minimal individuation, such as pre-industrial societies, and was 
used to keep the population in check. Organic solidarity, on the other hand, 
appears in societies with higher interdependence and more diverse groups and is 
maintained through laws (Giddens & Sutton, 2010).

140
termined by IRs. According to R. Collins (1993), 
'By recognizing emotional solidarity with a group as the pri
mary good in social interaction, it is possible to see all such 
value-oriented behaviours as rationally motivated towards 
optimizing this good. Because IRs vary in the amount of so
lidarity they provide, and in their costs of participating, there 
is a market for ritual participation that shapes the distribu
tion of individual behaviour' (p. 205). 
 
In summary, affections in movement were observed when 
Livskonceptet was first conceived and by the time it was announ
ced. Notably, these accumulating affections not only create both the 
main challenges for infrastructuring, but also its necessary precondi
tions. Returning to the characteristics of an infrastructure as defined 
by Star and Ruhleder (1996), we can see that the criteria fits our case 
quite well: Livskonceptet was not found in isolation; it sat embedded 
within social, material and technological infrastructures (that we of
ten take for granted) consisting of the local communities with their 
many attachments (attachments to churches, to family structures or 
social clubs) and the infrastructure of the public sector (with city 
services, offices, employees and materials), as well as infrastructures 
around the city. Star and Ruhleder's (1996) two final characteristics 
ring particularly true in this case: '(6) Infrastructures do not grow de 
novo, they wrestle with the inertia of the installed base and inherit 
strengths and limitations from that base; (7) the normally invisible 
infrastructures become visible upon breakdown' (p. 113). The first 
of these two points ring true towards Durkheim's statement about 
solidarity above, as well the idea that Livskonceptet did not rid it of 
the fears or hate that was mobilized against it by setting an affective 
assemblage into motion. 
 
In terms of a breakdown, this becomes a subjective perspec
tive. Had we been talking about, for example, a black out of a big 
power network, such as the one Bennett (2010) described in her 
book Vibrant Matter, or a bug within an electronic community sys
tem, such as that described by Star and Ruhleder (1996), then a 
breakdown is more readily defined. However, in the social sphere, a 
breakdown can be elusive. Verran (2002) offered an example of this 

141
elusiveness in her paper on Australian bushfires. There, she descri
bed how different groups invested in the land-native Aborigines, 
scientists and pastoralists-and the scorching of the land by fire can 
be seen as a necessary rebirth of the land and as an environmental 
disaster. Instead of trying to define what a breakdown is, I will at
tempt a definition of the opposite, a build-up, if you will. In relation 
to Livskonceptet and the affective economy, there, at least, it is a 
build-up rather than a breakdown in the sense that the problems 
accumulate and spiral out of control more than a sudden 'crash' or 
shut-down. The Cultural Politics of Emotion of Ahmed (2014) of
fers us insight into what a supposed breakdown in the social might 
look like. Ahmed provided the example of when feminist scholar 
Sunera Thobani spoke out against the 'war on terror' in 2001 and 
was met with criticism from community leaders, hate mail and death 
threats. Ahmed (2014) commented that 
'The extremity of the reaction to Thobani's speech demon
strates the risk of speaking out against established "truths". 
Such truths are worlds made through the authorization of 
some views over others. Thobani's speech is describes as 
"hate speech", and is represented as an attack on Americans 
as well as on America. Her speech is understood as repea
ting the injury or wound that had already been inflicted by 
terrorists. To be critical of the "war on terrorism" is to be 
identified as "a terrorist". [...] "You are either with us or 
against us"' (p. 169). 
Thus, Ahmed asked, what does it mean to 'be with?' My conclusion 
and my proposition for the opposite of a breakdown in the social is 
the feminist empiricism described by Haraway (1988): It is the situ
ated approach, the messy approach (Law, 2004), and the affect-able 
approach. This is where one is in danger of being contaminated by 
the emotions of the assembly. It becomes a question of being present 
in the situi and being present enough to be held accountable for it 
(Suchman, 2002). 

142
5.2.2 WHO CARES? ACTING THROUGH AFFECT
 
Many times during my Ph.D., I have fallen into the rabbit 
hole that is the discussion around responsibility and accountability. 
In my experience, when working with design with and for commu
nities or indeed research with and for communities, all discursive 
roads eventually lead to 'responsibility'. In the introduction, as well 
as in the methods chapter, I have mentioned the tensions that arise 
when we design within complex socio-material systems. Now, on 
the one hand, it would be a passive and privileged position to refrain 
from working with these communities (and it would also be questio
nable if participatory designers could then refer to their practice as 
'democratic'), particularly as we have learned from Arendt that it is 
the 'stepping in'-the action that constitutes political action. In her 
own words:
'Action would be an unnecessary luxury, a capricious interfe
rence with general laws of behaviour, if men where endlessly 
reproducible repetitions of the same model, whose nature or 
essence was the same for all and as predictable as the nature 
or essence of any other thing. Plurality is the condition of 
human action because we are the same, that is, human, in 
such a way that nobody is ever the same as anyone else who 
ever lived, lives, or will live.' (Arendt, 1998, p. 8) 
And if indeed the private sphere is steadily expanding (Arendt, 
1958) and moving into the neoliberal workplace (Bjögvinsson et al., 
2012; Gramsci, 1934; Huybrecht, 2018) with a pacifying force that 
leaves us without political agency, which in turn inhibits us from fu
ture action (Arendt, 1958), then surely every kind of activation can 
spark life in the public sector matters in the collective future-making 
process that is the public sector. This challenge is particular for par
ticipatory design, as it is conducted in the ethically infected shared 
space between designers and participants and often does so in colla
boration with the so-called marginalised communities.
 
On the other hand, can we justify building our work on the 
'pain narratives' (Tuck & Yang, 2014) of marginalised communi
ties? Sometimes, refraining from research is the most ethically defen

143
dable thing to do (Tuck, 2009a; Tuck & Yang, 2014). We should not 
publish some stories and issues that are not ours-as researchers-
to define. Light and Akama (2018) phrased the tensions nicely:
'Design comes historically out of industrial contexts, such 
as the workplace and corporate lab (into which sample par
ticipants are invited), which are clearly bounded by role, 
scope and intention. By contrast, in designing with nebulous 
and dynamic clusters of people, classical distinctions, such 
as those between 'designers' and 'users', may collapse, and, 
instead, many people can become collaborators in pursuit of 
a common, often evolving, objective. If we are looking for 
trickiness, we have found it here.' (p. 131)
 
Light and Akama (2018) argued that ethical conduct within 
such trickiness cannot be reduced to a static condition but is instead 
'something created and manifested in the relations of dynamic eco
logies of actions and roles'-a position shared by many feminist 
scholars working with the ethics of care. 
 
The ethics of care, as a philosophical turn, is predicated 
on this reciprocated understanding of relationships. People we are 
entangled with others in our vicinity, and our actions have conse
quences not just for ourselves, but for those around us. Therefore, 
the circumstances of your actions must be acknowledged when you 
make an ethical consideration rather than following a universal rule. 
Ethics of care stems from feminist discourses, and according to some 
(e.g. Rachels, 2003), it has emerged as a counternarrative to what 
traditionally had been considered 'masculine values'. In the first half 
of the 20th century, when the differences between male and female 
genders seemed indisputable to most people, the feminist movement 
placed a lot of emphasis on lifting the strengths and positives within 
the 'female essence' (think, for example, of the suffragist claim that 
'If women voted, there will be no more wars'). This 'woman's mo
rality' stems from being women and from being mothers. By caring 
as women do, by those around us, women represented a kind of 
politics different from the men who exercised it professionally. Lister 
(2003) said, 'A mother of teenage children might join a campaign 

144
for decent facilities for young people on her estate out of concern 
for her own children; but that campaign could benefit the whole 
community if it reduced vandalism and crime, and her involvement 
in it may well have broadened her own concerns' (p. 81). Just as 
Haraway (1988) argued for the embodied 'guilty' perspective (Ha
raway, 1988; Harding, 1986), Tronto (1993) has problematised the 
notion of care as being something women are presumed to be good 
at. The influence of the branch of feminism that emphasised gender 
differences30 faded over the past 100 years and was notably chal
lenged by the notion of intersectionality. The idea of female 'essence' 
is tied to discourses around citizenship, where the model woman is 
the model mother (Lister, 2003; Segal, 2013). This citizenship is gen
der differentiated; it a) ties the woman to her body and by extension 
to the private sphere and b) ignores the differences between women 
in terms of, for example, class, race, sexuality and ability (Lister, 
2003). As a result of this 'women's morality', the sensibilities of be
ing a good mother excluded the struggles of, for example, women of 
colour, lesbians and working class men and women (Tronto, 1993). 
 
Drawing instead on the intersectional black feminism that 
lays the foundation for feminist technoscience, Tronto (1993) and 
Puig de la Bellacasa (2017) argued that the notion of care requires 
more nuance, such as responsibility and competence. For example, 
Puig de la Bellacasa (2017) wrote, 'Listening, like speaking, is not 
neural. Listening with care is an active process of intervening in the 
count of whom and what is ratified as concerned; it affects the repre
sentation of things, adding mediation to mediation' (p. 58). Refer
ring back to the standpoint, the feminist theories of Harding (2008) 
and Puig de la Bellacasa (2017) call for a radical democratic way of 
listening to neglected things. Care does not mean a passive engende
red nurturing role; as sometimes prescribed to it, it means solidarity 
(Tronto, 2013). Tronto (1993) proposed a moral praxis predicated 
on that 'All moral arguments are made in a political context, and fe
minists ignore the political setting of their moral arguments at their 
peril' (p. 3). Tronto (1993) ignored the traditional understanding of 
an ethics of care as a virtue, as advocated by, for example, Noddings 
(2003). Noddings (2003) argued an ethics based on the 'natural' act 
30 In swedish: särartsfeminism.

145
of caring, as she says, a mother does for a child. Caring, according 
to Noddings (2003), must be a nonrational act, and she cautioned 
against structural kinds of care:
'The danger is that caring, which is essentially nonrational in 
that it requires a constructive engrossment and displacement 
of motivation, may gradually or abruptly be transformed 
into abstract problem solving. There is, then, a shift of fo
cus from the cared to the "problem". Opportunities arise for 
self-interest, and persons entrusted with caring may lack the 
necessary engrossment in those to be cared for'. (Noddings, 
2003, p. 25-26)
 
Noddings (2003) ignored the need for public healthcare, 
public education, childcare and so on. The virtue of caring lies on 
an individual level, according to Noddings (2003), and not on a 
structural one. In Nodding's (2003) vision, we care best for those 
closest to us, such as our families; however, what about the elderly 
who do not have a family? What about the mother who has to work 
to pay the bills? It is this line of argument that has caused some 
critics to suggest that an ethics of care stands in opposition to the 
expansion of liberal rights (Tronto, 1993, p. 161). The new wave of 
ethics of care, including but not limited to Tronto (1993), Puig de la 
Bellacasa (2011, 2017) and Mol (2008), suggests an ethical praxis 
that resides in the public rather than the private and that can reveal 
relations of power rather than sedate them. Mol (2008) drew on her 
empirical studies of care practices in her book, The Logic of Care: 
Health and the Problem of Patient Choice, to discuss the difference 
in treating patients as citizens (with rights) or as consumers (with an 
individual choice). She wrote about the separation of care from cure, 
where market-oriented healthcare will prioritise customers/patients 
that can be cured, as this looks better 'in the books'. Once again, ca
ring and maintenance are of lower prestige than curing. This factor 
is reflected in the division of labour between doctors (who cure) and 
nurses (who care).
 
Participatory design is a process-focused discipline, whe
re participants are invited to cooperate with designers, researchers 

146
and developers. It is a pedagogy that we refer to as democracy in 
the workplace, with principles of inclusion and dialogue for mutu
al learning, which suggest that the true experts are not the trained 
designers but those users who understand the situation and the pro
blem. We maintain a principle of what we call design-after-design, 
where users should own and be able to configure the design to such 
an extent that they can continue to develop it over time according 
to need. The foundational principle of participatory design-that 
people have the right to influence their workplace-means that we 
have to identify who will be influenced by a design or intervention 
to engage the 'right' participants. 
 
Within Amiralsstaden, this meant not only engaging with ci
vil servants who worked on the project and who were able to set asi
de paid working hours to participate, but also 'local residents' who 
participated in their free time, unpaid. Here, we can see that the two 
groups are not participating on equal terms, and that is before we 
take into consideration issues such as language constraints, age, edu
cational background and so on. Nodding's (2003) definition of care 
as a personal virtue may suggest that the people who participated as 
local residents performed more genuine care work than the public 
sector workers who were 'hired to care'. I will admit that the caring 
is performed differently in the two groups; we may, for example, 
look at how the 'local residents' often acted freely on affect and may 
have performed care by expressing strong emotions, while the public 
sector workers were tied to the emotional labour of their professi
onal role and may at times have performed care (emotional labour) 
by withholding strong emotions (Smedberg, 2019a). However, I will 
not support the idea that the local residents practiced care more 
virtuously. They were often motivated by personal gain and often 
refused to collaborate if they did not get their will through. This 
expression may of course be of dissonance between the collabora
tion parameters and the roles made available to the local residents. 
Public engagement events are often curated in such a way that they 
frame what kind of participation is welcome (Michael, 2012b). Ac
cording to Michael (2012b) 'Engagement events can entail a range 
of happenings which, in one way or another, "overspill" the empiri
cal, analytical or political framing of the engagement event' (p. 529). 
The imbalance between public and private caring participant groups 

147
can often be predicted and addressed early on in the project, and to 
some extent (although not always easy), we can prevent it from ac
cumulating into a large problem by taking action in time. Interpreters 
could be hired; we can alter the language and methods of the work 
to help balance the scales of constraints. Within Amiralsstaden, we 
attempted to address the imbalances by working with the so-called 
knowledge alliances. Knowledge alliances are a 'multistakeholder 
social platform for dialogue and agenda setting' (Stigendal & Novy, 
2018 p. 204) and a 'partnership between representatives of different 
knowledges' (ibid.). By different types of knowledge, Stigendal and 
Novy (2018) indicated that the critical and theoretical knowledge of 
researchers is matched by the more grounded knowledge of practi
tioners. As an approach to research, knowledge alliances challenge 
the hierarchies of knowledge, in which the established knowledge is 
owned by the knowledge authorities from academia. To challenge 
this, we must also challenge the roles within the research process-a 
philosophy akin to the pedagogies of, for example, Freire (1970). 
Freire (1970) suggested a co-becoming through dialogic action, a 
mutual learning process. Working in this manner places the issues of 
power imbalance at the centre of the work.
 
While the imbalance between public and private caring par
ticipant groups can often be predicted, the political issues that arise 
from merging different groups are far more difficult to foresee and 
prevent. According to Robertson and Wagner (2013), 'Negotiating 
in particular the participation of "normal citizens" [can] be a highly 
sensitive issue, since municipalities feared having to include critical 
views. The question that arises then is to identify those who have 
been excluded from the infrastructure and their views on the project' 
(p. 71). Shamoo and Resnik (2003) argued that it is important to 
'distinguish between ethics and politics [as] political questions tend 
to focus on broad issues having to do with the structure of socie
ty and dynamic groups, whereas ethical questions tend to focus on 
narrower issues pertaining the conduct of individuals within society' 
(p. 11). I argue that this is a reductive understanding of the political. 
Instead, I align myself with the perspective presented in Roberson 
and Wagner's (2013) chapter: that a practice that transcends com
munities (of practice, cultural, social, etc.) requires a politics-in-ac

148
tion and an ethics-in-action31. Navigating complex situations and 
constellations means engaging with different ethical perspectives, 
and arguably, many ethical discourses have been generated to legiti
mise the dominant power structures of those who benefit from them 
(Robertson & Wagner, 2013, p. 66). Ethical case deliberation must 
mediate such opposing ethical and political stances. 
 
Ethical navigation is something that we all practice. Kohl
berg attempted to grade the human ability to determine what is 
'right' through moral dilemmas-among others, the 'Heinz's di
lemma' (Rachels, 2003). Heinz's Dilemma' tells the story of a man 
who cannot afford to pay the full extortionate price for his wife's 
life-saving medicine, and in desperation, considered stealing the 
drug (ibid). Kohlberg suggested that moral development has six sta
ges (ranging from obedience to universal ethical principles), and by 
discussing dilemmas, such as the one above, with children, we can 
determine how children at different ages think and assess which li
nes of reasoning are more sophisticated. Kohlberg's work has been 
criticised, most notably by Gilligan (1982), who returned to the in
terviews Kohlberg had conducted and looked at them in a feminist 
sense. Gilligan (1982) found that the results of the interviews-that 
boys seemed to be more adept moral reasoners than girls (Tronto, 
1993, p. 77)-were based on an original sample of only males. In 
addition, what the dilemma was actually investigating is unclear: the 
development or moral reasoning. Gilligan's (1982) critique objected 
to the 'use of hypothetical moral dilemmas as the correct way to 
understand people's moral experiences and views' (Tronto, 1993, p. 
78). Two of the interviewees, Jake and Amy, have been used to ex
emplify how differently Gilligan and Kohlberg interpreted the moral 
reasoning: Jake reasons that Heinz should steal the drug because 
human life is worth more than money (scoring him a 4 or 5 on the 
development stage), whereas Amy struggles to define a right course 
of action in the scenario, as she is worrying about the long-term 
consequences of stealing and the effects on the people connected to 
Heinz, his wife and other patients who might need the drug (scoring 
31 I write out both politics-in-action and ethics-in-action to avoid reducing 
myself to either one of them, and while they will at times be overlapping, a slight 
difference is noted between the two. Politics-in-action, in this case, refers to 
situation-based assessments, whereas ethics follows a framework. 

149
her a few levels below Jake) (Rachels, 2003, p. 161-164). Gilligan 
(1982) read a morality into Kohlberg's work: a morality framed 'in a 
different voice' (Tronto, 1993, p. 77). Gilligan's (1982) work serves 
as an excellent example of how 'male morality' is favoured above 
'female morality' and could perhaps be read as an urgent call for 
more women in the science of philosophy and ethics. However, Gil
ligan's (1982) work has also received criticism, particularly for len
ding a bias towards middle-class white girls and for using a sample 
that excluded male subjects (Tronto, 1993). 
 
As a researcher, I will be sensitised to some forms of systemic 
oppression, for example, but may initially be blind to others. Inhe
rited power relations and/or conflicts of interest may exist between 
the participants. Navigating complex situations and constellations 
means engaging with different ethical perspectives, and arguably, 
many ethical discourses have been generated to legitimise the domi
nant power structures of those who benefit from them (Robertson 
& Wagner, 2013, p. 66). The ethical case deliberation required to 
mediate such opposing ethical and political stances could perhaps be 
referred to, as does Mol (2008), as ongoing maintenance work. 
 
While Noddings (2003) argued for the personal virtue of 
care, Mol (2008) stated in the book Logic of Care: Health and the 
Problem of Patient Choice for structural care. In Mol's writings, she 
distinguished 'care' from 'cure': '"Care", is used for activities such 
as washing, feeding or dressing wounds that are done to make daily 
life more bearable. The second term, "cure" resonates with the pos
sibility of healing, and is applied to interventions in the course of a 
disease' (Mol, 2008, p. 1). Mol (2008) suggested that the practices 
of each term are not mutually exclusive but that a hierarchy exists 
between them, with 'cure' as the more prestigious one. Mol's (2008) 
writings are, in many ways, a juxtaposition to Nodding's: Mol sug
gested that personal care can be dangerous when emotions cloud 
judgments, and she further argued against personal choice in healt
hcare. She sees the latter as a commodification of healthcare, with 
personalised health-tech as the final frontier. Mol's raison d'être is, 
as the title of the book suggests, to replace the market-driven 'logic 
of choice' with a structural 'logic of care'. Mol described with great 
tenderness the heavy maintenance work that falls upon the carewor
kers. While some health tech could monitor, for example, a heart 

150
condition while the patient freely roams around the city or even the 
countryside, the care worker is tied to the location, resources and 
other commitments of their workplace: 
'Care professionals are not surprised by such things. They 
do not make categories based on a sociological marker or 
two. Instead, at least if they give good care, they inquire into 
a specific situation of a specific person. "How are you?" a 
good professional might have asked me. [...] Everyone can 
come to a consulting room and complain (to an appropriate 
degree, that is). Even people "like you and me". In the logic 
of care fragility is taken to be part of life. But care professio
nals not only accept that sooner or later everyone may need 
help, they also refuse to give up on anyone. Salespeople do. 
A group of people to whom nothing can be sold stops being 
a "target" group. [...] The logic of care, by contrast, does 
not start out from what people know or want but from what 
they need'. (Mol, 2008, p. 25)
 
Mol's portrayal of the relationship between caseworkers 
and patients mirrors the relationship I see between researcher and 
participants within PAR-and further been public sector workers 
and citizens. 'Care' here does not always refer to a 'cure' but rather 
to an ongoing commitment. Public sector workers who leave the 
relative safety of their offices in favour of speaking to citizens often 
find themselves overwhelmed by what they see and hear. A woman 
I interviewed in 2018 suggested that her first public meeting was 
chaotic, as she was the first person from the municipality to venture 
into the concerned community and ask for their input. As a result, 
she not only received answers to the questions she sought to ask but 
was informed of everything that was wrong in the citizens' lives. She 
told me how overwhelmed she felt and that she did not know how 
to process the information, as the form had no room available to file 
them in. In this way, many public sector workers find themselves in 
the paradoxical position of being tasked to care without having the 
mandate or agency to do so. 
 
In The Human Condition, Arendt (1958) defined the public 
sphere as the common spaces and places that humans share. Altering 

151
the shared space is political, and altering it may be done physically 
or intellectually. Through material changes or through the act of 
speaking, we remould the shared public sphere. By doing so, we 
change the conditions not just for ourselves but also for the people 
around us. Through work, we produce the objects that change the 
shared space: the beautiful ceramic vase of the craftsman, the great 
novel of the century, the engineers' new bridge, and the politician's 
motion for a new law. This work is important and prestigious as it 
contributes to the common and creates the world that we all sha
re. However, the world, in all its glory, cannot sustain itself on the 
future-making work alone; behind the scenes, in the private realm, 
resides labour. Labour is the futile and everyday strive. The endless 
and repetitive efforts to keep us alive: to feed us and keep us warm. 
The objects and relations of the man-made world require mainte
nance and care, which is a heavy task that lacks the prestige of work. 
Similarly, I believe that the care work that public servants perform 
in relation to citizens must be understood as ongoing maintenance, 
care labour if you will. 
 
Working with public engagement always requires a certain 
level of responsibility-indeed, working with people always requires 
a certain level of responsibility, if not an ethical obligation towards 
the people affected by the intervention. Caring for the participants 
can, of course, be done in terms of the formalities that form a ba
seline for ethical conduct. I am thinking of 'formalities', such as in
formed consent. These factors are standardisation of ethical condu
ct that ensures the participants' rights. They are also bureaucratic 
tools, and, as with most bureaucratic tools, they come with a cer
tain level of duality: they are placed to ensure the fair treatment of 
each individual. A standardisation of practice performed to counter, 
for example, nepotism, racism and sexism. Feminist scholar Ahmed 
(2006) has written extensively about how the performativity of such 
work can be a nonperformativitity, for example, of how a commis
sion formed to signal that a university cares about antiracism can 
have a pacifying effect, as antiracism has already been cared for. 

152
5.3 AFFECTIVE INFRASTRUCTURING AND AGENCY
 
Participatory design and infrastructuring within public se
ctor processes pose numerous challenges. As we position ourselves 
in a time where boundaries between public and private are incre
asingly becoming blurred, affect poses a particular challenge to 
contemporary infrastructuring professionals. Anja's reflection on 
her own role, as the person who says 'see you tomorrow' to the 
project participants bears witness to the maintenance labour that 
infrastructuring entails. It bears witness to its repetitiveness and to 
the care it requires. It also bears witness to the emotional labour that 
is part of her role. 
 
Emotional labour, as defined by Hochschild (2012), means 
the emotional management and attention to emotional presentation 
that many, especially care workers, perform as part of their work. 
Hochschild (2012) used the example of a flight attendant to illustra
te: 'The flight attendant does physical labour when she pushes heavy 
metal carts through the aisles, and she does mental work when she 
prepares for and actually organises emergency landings and evalu
ations. But in the course of doing the physical and mental labour, 
she is also doing something more, something I define as emotional 
labour' (p. 6). Hochschild (2012) described who the flight attendant 
uses emotions to embody the corporate disposition, and how they, 
through a smile, can convey 'its confidence that the plane will not 
crash, its reassurance that departures and arrivals will be on time, 
its welcome and its invitation to return' (p. 4). The smile is a service 
performed for the benefit of the paying customer. Emotional labour 
has an exchange value, according to Hochschild (2012). This labour 
performed by the flight attendant requires, as with other kinds of 
labour, a certain skill, and although Hochschild (2012) emphasised 
that 'well performed' emotional labour seems effortless, it also takes 
a toll on the labourer herself. 
5.3.1 ANECDOTE: THE CITIZEN EVA AND HER BALCONY
 
I would like to share an anecdote from Amiralsstaden from 
the winter of 2018. I met a woman I will refer to as Eva, as she has 

153
given her consent for me to use the story, but not her full name. Eva 
worked with the Amiralsstaden for approximately 6 months, and 
during this time, she participated in two BKA. She was the kind of 
person who had a certain presence in a group, a woman not easily 
ignored. In my weekly reflections from February 2018, I wrote:
 
'I'd heard about her before I had a chance to meet her myself. 
Because [Eva] begun to develop a reputation for being diffi
cult very soon after she entered the project. The civil servants 
in the project told me that she would come in once or twice 
every couple of weeks and "do her part" - participate in the 
tasks at hand. But while there she would seize every oppor
tunity to talk about her balcony. She wanted an enclosed bal
cony, and she wanted Amiralstaden [Sic] to make it happen 
for her.' (Author's field notes, 2018) 
 
Eva had only lived in Sweden for a few years; hence, she 
spoke only a little English and Sweden and was noticeably restrained 
by the language barrier. This made the civil servants in the project 
believe that her constant insistence in talking about her balcony was 
in fact a misunderstanding on her part. They speculated that Eva 
perhaps did not understand the purpose of the project and explained 
to her that getting her balcony enclosed was a question for her land
lord, not Amiralsstaden. However, she was, of course, more than 
welcome to continue working with Amiralsstaden on other matters. 
Eva still worked on the project, but she did not stop talking about 
her balcony. She was becoming increasingly frustrated; meanwhile, 
the patience of the civil servants' was also wearing thin. This caused 
them to withdraw somewhat from Eva. Formally, they welcomed her 
participation in the project, but informally, the interactions with her 
had become 'high maintenance', and the civil servants avoided any 
unnecessary interactions they could. In particular, those interactions 
in which the infected issue of the balcony might arise. There was, 
they reasoned, nothing that they could do for her; therefore, there 
was no point in continuing the conversations. After all, they had 
a professional responsibility towards all of the participants within 
Amiralsstaden and could not spend a disproportionate amount of 
time on Eva. 

154
 
A few weeks later, a buddy exercise was arranged by Know
ledge Alliance number 3-the knowledge alliance tasked with ma
king Malmö Municipality better at listening. For the exercise, one 
person from the upper tier of the knowledge triangle that Amirals
staden worked with (upper tier contains those with recognised aca
demic knowledge or decision-making mandate; the lower tier has 
those without) would be paired with one of the local resident parti
cipants. Together, each pair would complete a few tasks that prom
pted them to talk and get to know one another. It was my personal 
impression that many of the participants from the upper tier-most
ly civil servants from Malmö Municipality-found this buddy exer
cise stressful. One of the civil servants even called the exercise faci
litators beforehand to ask that she be paired with 'another mother, 
so that [we] at least would have one thing in common' (Author's 
field notes, 2018). The buddy system is, despite the stress it might 
have caused, quite effective for listening. In Eva's case, a one-to-one 
conversation with her civil servant buddy allowed for both formal 
and informal conversations aided this time by an interpreter32. In 
this constellation, with an interpreter and a civil servant who for
tunately happened to be an attentive and empathic listener, Eva's 
story behind the balcony finally unfolded. She had recently had a 
break-in at her ground floor apartment and did not feel safe in her 
home anymore. An enclosed balcony would allow her another lock. 
She told the civil servant of the conditions on the street outside and 
described, amongst other things, the broken streetlight outside that 
left her balcony door shielded from any passers-by and, as such, 
an ideal location for a break-in. She explained that she would not 
know what to do if a burglar came into her house, not because she 
had much value but because she lived alone and had no family in 
the country. Who would she turn to for help? she asked. She expla
ined that she would not be living in the area at all if given a choice 
to move, but as she was not given a choice, she hoped that a glass 
balcony would at least make the situation bearable33. 
32 
 
Note 
that 
the 
same 
interpreter 
had 
previously 
participa
ted in the project work but not as part of informal conversations.

33 This anecdote has previously been presented and published in the procee
dings of the Nordic Design Conference, 2019 (See Smedberg, 2019).

5.3.2 AFFECT AND CAREWORK AS PART OF 
CITIZENSHIP
 
Affect performs in countless ways in the story of Eva and 
her balcony. Below, I will attempt to unfold these by following three 
of the story's actors and propose that they represent three affective 
positions relevant to co-design in the public sphere. Eva represents 
the citizen, who operates as a private person and therefore does not 
perform the emotional labour according to Hochchild's definition. 
It is not part of her professional role to manage her emotions, but 
she is exposed to what Ahmed refers to as stickiness. Public sector 
workers have a responsibility to manage their emotions, and-as 
with the management group in Linero-are also often tasked with 
managing the emotions of participants and stakeholders. Finally, I 
suggest that the participatory designer represents a third affective 
position. This position arises from accountability towards both the 
citizen and the public servant.
 
Eva, in this story, is angry, frightened and frustrated. To the 
others within the Amiralsstaden working group, she is contagious, 
based on Ahmed's words. She is also in a difficult position, as she is 
unable to communicate what she is frustrated about and what she 
believes must be done. As a consequence, she continuously 'over
spills' (Michael, 2012b) the intention of the engagement events by 
behaving in ways that were deemed unproductive. 
 
Eva's personal strength lies in the fact that she kept coming 
back and continued the collaboration, even when others in the group 
began to avoid her. This position is not easy to handle, and many 
would have simply left at this point. When I interviewed her about 
this, she explained that this was the only time since she arrived in 
Malmö that she had any direct contact with people from the muni
cipality and that she saw it as an 'only chance' to speak to someone. 
She felt powerless in her living situation, and this was the only tool 
she had to use to do something about it. 
 
Furthermore, Eva participated in the project as a citizen, not 
in any professional role. It is far from my intention to say how a 
citizen engages 'correctly', quite the contrary. By participating, Eva 
moves her concerns from the private sphere and into the public. This 

156
becomes not only an inherently political move, in light of Arendt's 
notion of the political act, but also as the very core of what democra
tic citizenship is. Barbalet (1988) noted in his book Citizenship that 
'[T]he struggle for citizenship has been the struggle against exclu
sion, and against the inequalities which exclusion produces. But the 
rise of democratic citizenship has not brought an end to inequality' 
(p. 44). The same could be said in this case-to include voices that 
had not previously been heard in the project was a great step away 
from exclusion, but it did, of course, not 'solve' exclusion altogether. 
If we follow Barbarlet (1988) a bit further, we find that embedded in 
the issue of citizenship is the rights (or the lack of rights) to act: 
'The issue of who can practice citizenship and on what terms 
is not only a matter of the legal scope of citizenship and the 
formal nature of the rights entailed in it. It is also a matter 
of the non-political capacities of citizens, which derive from 
the social resources they command and to which they have 
access. A political system of equal citizenship is in reality 
less than equal if it is part of a society divided by unequal 
conditions' (p. 1). 
 
Barbalet (1988) proposed three elements to citizenship (four, 
if the industrial element is included): a civil, a social and the politi
cal, and he suggested that these are not always compatible. Eva here 
practices her civil rights by being in the project. 
 
Yet she somehow does not sit right. Her participation as a 
citizen poses an issue to the project organisers. Her position was 
infected. She poses an issue, and she exposes an issue in how we 
think about participation. Michaels (2012b) suggested that Public 
Engagement with Science is often designed to create a specific kind 
of techno-scientific citizen: the framework of engagement events cre
ates the possibility of a participation 'overspill'. Michael (2012b) 
drew on Strenger's (2005) notion of the idiot: 'a tool for sensitizing 
us to the overspilling of engagement events in ways which allow for 
a revisioning of those events, and the various and heterogeneous 
actors that comprise them' (p. 530). In other words, the idiot re
minds us that the problem is something else. In this manner, Eva's 
overspill can show us, first, that our engagement initiatives have 

157
limits, and second, that we can find more if we persevere. The civil 
servant who became Eva's buddy found much more in the story than 
a single issue, and through dialogue, the two were able to co-articu
late issues (Lindström & Ståhl, 2016) that were relevant to the rest 
of the group. I reiterate: Eva posed an issue, and by doing so, she 
exposed an issue. 
5.3.3 EMOTIONAL LABOUR AND PUBLIC SECTOR 
WORKERS
 
When the incident around Eva occurred, I found her to be 
the most interesting character. However, after having turned the 
anecdote over a few times, the most striking character in the story 
now appears to be the woman who called in on the night before the 
first buddy-meet to ask that she be paired up with another mother34. 
I find this incident immensely telling of the way many civil servants 
feel about citizen dialogues and citizen engagements. They are trap
ped in a position of power without the mandate to make positive 
change. 
 
Over the last decades, citizen dialogue in Sweden has been 
extensively debated: Who are we speaking to? How to do it? Why 
do we do it? However, considerably less research has looked into 
which desks the task lands and which mandate the civil servants had 
to conduct citizen dialogue with and actually do it 'well'. Embedded 
in the task is an ideological grounding: optimistic and pessimistic 
perspectives exist on citizen dialogue (Tahvilzadeh, 2015). The opti
mistic perspective holds on to the potential to deepen democratic di
alogues and increase citizen involvement in relevant questions. The 
pessimistic perspective argues that citizen dialogue is a neoliberal 
pseudo-democratic tactic that removes responsibility from the orga
nisation and places in on the individual. Regardless of what school 
the task of conducting citizen engagements has been designed from, 
its execution ultimately falls upon a civil servant. This grants the civil 
servant a certain amount of agency as the person responsible for fra
34 Note: this is not the same woman as the civil servant who spoke to Eva.

158
ming the question(s), the invitation and the format for engagement. 
However, none of the civil servants present within the knowledge al
liance that included Eva had been formally trained to design such an 
engagement. In fact, it was a recurring theme in my interviews with 
public sector workers from Malmö Stad that they often complained 
about the lack of resources to fully engage with citizens. In some 
cases, no additional time is allocated within the project to speak to 
people; it was not prioritised enough. In many other cases, the civil 
servants dreaded engagement events because of the overspill they 
would inevitably meet. They told me that they would set a meeting 
to discuss one question but would be overwhelmed by the responses 
of the people attending. One person told me that the questionnaire 
the department had issues with was unable to contain the so-called 
data gathered in the engagement: 'They give me so many questions 
that I do not have a box for'. This case, in turn, causes distress on 
both sides: the citizens who take the time to engage feel that they 
are not being heard and may be reluctant to engage in the future. 
Civil servants are placed in a position of impotence: failing to do 
their job both in the eyes of their superiors and the citizens. Many 
public sector workers witness how 'project fatigue' is haunting the 
public sector, and women in the public sector are disproportionally 
suffering from emotional burnout (Perski et al., 2002). 
 
Operating in a role where your responsibility exceeds your 
mandate can certainly be the cause of many negative emotions, such 
as stress, anxiety and frustration, which can then cause public sector 
workers to avoid citizen engagement. When the woman called on 
the night before the buddy pair-up, the participating partners from 
Malmö Municipality had not designed the interaction themselves. 
In fact, until they arrived, they had very limited information about 
what the event would entail or ask of them; all they knew was that 
they would be paired up with a local resident. This makes the phone 
call on the night before, when the woman asked to be paired with 
another mother, easy to understand. She was grasping for something 
she could control. 
 
One of the ideas that sparked the Amiralsstaden project was 
the idea of moving the architects' office from the city hall to the 
area they would be working on. This came from a critique that had 
arisen during the Malmö Commission work, saying that the hete

159
rogeneity amongst those who worked within Malmö Municipality, 
reflected and affected the city's areas that were prioritised. Areas 
that housed the affluent middle class-to which city planners and 
architects belonged-were cared for with more urgency than pre
dominately working-class areas, such as Rosengård. Arguably, this 
contributed to the segregation of the city. By positioning the ongoing 
work on Amiralsstaden in Amiralsstaden, the city planners and ar
chitects would, at the very least, have to travel through the area on 
a regular basis. One of the ideas that developed during the course of 
the project was that the management group would also have to meet 
in Amiralsstaden's venue, meaning that they would also have to vi
sit the place before making any decisions around it. The difference 
that this move, from the city hall to the project's venue, made is not 
quantifiable, and to some extent, the biggest impact it made might 
be symbolic. However, notably, it reflects the project's ambition to 
challenge the ways we work with citizen engagement and illustrates 
how this was done by 'infection' or 'stickiness'. The engagement 
events designed from this premise sought to further agency by for
cing connections between public sector workers and citizens, failing 
to consider the care labour that was disproportionally performed by 
the first of the two groups.
5.3.4 AFFECT AND PARTICIPATORY DESIGN
 
Previously, this thesis has concerned itself with infra
structuring practitioners, whereas participatory designers are so
mewhat invisible. In the story of Eva and the civil servant, however, 
participatory designers are present. A group of three formed the 
knowledge alliance that orchestrated the buddy system, and I was 
with them as their critical friend. They designed the framework 
around the interactions, and they invited, facilitated and documen
ted. This group inherent their 'research question' from the two pre
vious knowledge alliances. While the two previous groups had de
parted from the questions 'How can we learn more about the area?' 
and explored ways to engage with citizens (through, for example, 
interviews and co-creation workshops), they ended with a new pro
blem definition. They explained to the Amiralsstaden management 

160
group that the issue was not that the local residents did not want to 
talk; it was that no one wanted to or knew how to listen. This issue 
was the offset of Knowledge Alliance Three, and from this depar
ting point, they became the infrastructuring practitioners who got 
their hands dirty and began trailing and tracing the issues. They for
mally and informally explored how listening was practiced in these 
contexts and identified key people (or rather key professional roles 
and positions) that could help carry a narrative into the organisa
tion. They sought to understand both the perspective of the civil 
servants and the citizens and designed their experiment, the buddy 
programme, with consideration towards both positions. The idea 
was that, by bringing them closer as equals, listening would come 
more naturally. What the infrastructuring group failed to consider 
was that the positions were not equal. Power imbalances are not 
erased by good intentions alone, and the disruptions they designed 
into the operational ways of interacting disrupted also the already 
established care work of both sides, but primarily the civil servants. 
While the facilitating role certainly can include an aspect of emotio
nal labour-maintaining relationships by managing and performing 
emotions for the benefit of the other as a service-the notion of 
affective infrastructuring seeks to recognise and work with the stick
iness of interpersonal relationships. The most important difference
