between the two is that within affective infrastructuring, emotions 
do not always have to be 'good': happy, safe and fun. The facilita
ting group, in this case knowledge alliance three, sought to bring 
people together and reasoned that if they were not 'happy' in the 
interaction, they would leave the collaboration. Therefore, negative 
emotions, such as the ones caused by Eva, were managed and supp
ressed within the group. 
 
Ahmed discussed happiness in her writings about the femi
nist killjoy and cited de Beauvoir ([1949] 1997): 'It is not too clear 
just what the word happy really means and still less what true values 
it may mask. There is no possibility of measuring the happiness of 
others, and it is always easy to describe as happy the situation in 
which one wishes to place them' (ibid, p. 28). The description of 
something as 'happy' is defensive, and Ahmed (2010) wrote that 
'Happiness translates its wish into politics, a wishful politics, a po
litics that demands that others live according to a wish. When a 

161
happiness wish is deposited, a social norm becomes a social good' 
(p. 572). Ahmed (2010) used the example of a happy housewife as 
a fantasy figure. By depicting her as happy, her position and situ
ation are justified. By saying she performs the labour happily, we 
are also saying that work is easy, which justifies her treatment as a 
second-class citizen. 
 
Ahmed's proposition is a feminist reading of happiness that 
considers those 'idiots' that break the social norm of happiness and 
complacency; in her words, the ́troublemakers, wretches, strangers, 
dissenters, killers of joy ́ (p. 573). The social norm, and through it 
the social good, is already set for us when we enter a space: regard
less of whether this is the world as presented to us by our parents or 
the workplace dominated by white, full-time professional, able-bo
died, heterosexual men. If we cannot partake in the social good, we 
feel alienated from it and are quintessentially faced with two op
tions: either we manage our emotions in the manner of Hochschilds 
air-stewardess or the example she provided of the bride that is un
happy on her wedding day-an 'inappropriate affect' (Hochschild, 
2003, p. 53)-and forced herself into a correct feeling, or the second 
option is that we take on the position of a feminist 'killjoy': 
'Feminists, by declaring themselves feminists, are already 
read as destroying something that is thought of by others 
not only as being good but as the cause of happiness. The 
feminist killjoy spoils the happiness of others; she is a spoil
sport because she refuses to convene, to assemble, or to meet 
up over happiness. In the thick sociality of everyday spaces, 
feminists are thus attributed as the origin of bad feeling, as 
the ones who ruin the atmosphere, which is how the atmosp
here might be imagined (retrospectively) as shared' (Ahmed, 
2010, p. 581-582).
When we design with affective infrastructures, 'good feelings' at the 
end of, for example, a workshop is not always 'good'. We, as infra
structurers, must problematise our understanding of what a good 
meeting or collaboration is and what we are willing to sacrifice for 
the sake of a surface smile of the participants. Furthermore, what do 
we risk doing if we fail to problematise the stigma of positive emo

162
tions: Is all really well if everybody is 'happy'? Or have we simply 
succeeded in reproducing the status quo? Covering bad feelings that 
have been displaced outside the social good? The story of Eva sug
gests we might be.
 
Within participatory design, long-standing strategies are av
ailable for engaging with wicked problems and intangible problems. 
What the facilitating group, knowledge alliance three, in this case, 
might have benefitted from, is a use-before-use and design-after-de
sign approach. These approaches address the need for participatory 
design to expand the conventional understanding of a design pro
cess. Imagining what it means to start the process before the fra
me around the design has been settled and leaving the design con
figurable for the users to modify after the design process has been 
concluded are easy (Ehn, 2008; Redström, 2008) when we think of 
designing objects or tangible things (or even in terms of large-scale 
socio-material infrastructures!). We can easily imagine the designer 
who receives a brief to design, say, a set of chairs for an office, and 
takes a step back to examine what the offices' need actually is. Who 
decided that new chairs were necessary? Why were they necessary? 
How are chairs used in the office (to sit on? As side tables? Stacked 
in piles?)? and by whom? Including the workers of this office would 
perhaps generate a hybrid solution, where two kinds of chairs were 
needed, some of the old loved ones kept and a sofa ordered. The 
defined question or problem that usually initiates the design pro
cess becomes part of the process and affords the users to be part of 
the enquiry and experimentation. Furthermore, leaving the chairs 
(or whatever solutions are generated) configurable means that the 
workers of the office may continue to design and redesign them ac
cording to need, long after the expert designer has left. However, 
use-before-use and design-after-design are more difficult to imagine 
when we consider affective infrastructuring. However, I argue it as 
important, if not more. 
 
An affective infrastructuring, in the case of Eva and the 
public sector worker, would have entailed inviting them into the 
process of co-designing their meeting platform. For the public sector 
worker, this could have afforded a crucial sense of reassurance that 

163
she would not be placed in a situation where she would have respon
sibility without the mandate to shoulder this responsibility. It would 
not guarantee an approach of care over cure (Mol, 2008) on her 
part, but it would have made it possible. For Eva, it would have af
forded her a chance to articulate her needs. One might speculate that 
perhaps she would have articulated the need for an interpreter soo
ner, thus preventing a lot of the miscommunication and the frustra
tion that followed. By inviting them into the space as co-designers, 
the potential for a long-term reciprocal relationship may have been 
made possible, along with the mutual ownership that characterises 
design after design. 
5.4 CLOSING REMARKS
 
Affect is a strong force within the human experience and an 
integral part of the human condition. This factor is something that 
we often fail to consider when designing systems and when mobi
lizing socio-material infrastructures. The systems are designed for 
rational decision making, for upholding rights and responsibilities 
and for judicious actions. The affect that plays into this could be 
considered a 'human error', but through this chapter, I have sought 
to make the case that it is only an error if we define it as such.
 
 
I have sought to make a case for the undeniable presence 
of emotions within socio-material infrastructures and that these af
fective currents can be the source of many laudable aspects of col
laborations and interactions: of care work and solidarity. However, 
it can also be a source of fear, anger and frustration: emotions that 
separate us and make collaboration impossible. We must pay atten
tion to the emotional labour performed within socio-material infra
structures and to who performs it. 

164

6. COLLABORATIVE ANECDOTALIZATION
 
Collaborative anecdotalization is the third programmatic an
swer to this thesis. It relates to and departs from the first programmatic 
answer outlined in the previous chapter as a 'how' to the 'what'. It is a 
methodological contribution justified by the tensions that affective in
frastructuring has illuminated. For example, in the story of Eva from 
the previous chapter, one person emerges as the 'key'-unlocking the si
tuation and bringing together the frustrated Eva with the project mana
gement group. The 'public sector worker with excellent listening skills' 
steps forward as the hero who connects the management team and the 
citizen. My proposition for collaborative anecdotalization within infra
structuring processes is three-fold: that it is a way of being within rese
arch, a way of co-creating knowledge and as a way of communicating 
research. 
 
Learning to listen is an incredibly potent yet often undervalued 
task. It requires the listening party to relinquish, at least for a moment, 
control of the narrative. Anecdotalization, as I will go into further detail 
in the section below, suggests a process and an action beyond the static 
story. The term has been coined by sociologist Michael in the antholo
gy Inventive Methods (Lury & Wakeford, 2012)-who proposed the 
anecdote as something more than just a narrative. It is, he suggested, a 
narrative for the telling, designed to be circulated and retold. It derives, 
of course, from the notion of the anecdote, which is usually described as 
a short narrative of an incident (Webster's New Encyclopedic Dictiona
ry, 1996, p. 36-37), or a story with a point (Epstein, 1989). My under

165
standing of the anecdote resonates with the description of Le Guin 
(1989), who suggested that stories behave like vessels that hold the 
world(s): 
'I would go so far as to say that the natural, proper, fitting 
shape of the novel might be that of a sack, a bag. A book 
holds words. Words hold things. They bear meanings. A 
novel is a medicine bundle, holding things in a particular, 
powerful relation to one another and to us' (Le Guin, 1989, 
p. 153). 
 
The difference between a story and anecdotalization is that 
the action requires a receiver of the message. It requires not only 
someone to tell the story but also someone to hear it. Anecdotali
zation holds reciprocity within it, a reciprocity that is imperative to 
any collaboration. And while the use of anecdotes is widely accepted 
as an everyday occurrence-not something belonging to the scien
ces-I will argue in this chapter what collaborative anecdotalization 
does. The critique against the anecdote is that it is often seen to be, 
at best, a digression, and its presence is not accepted as lending au
thority or legitimacy to scientific reasoning (van Manen, 1989). The 
anecdotal evidence is often portrayed as a cunning rhetorical device 
wrought to manipulate the favour of an account: 'At the hand of 
other examples, other anecdotes, differently arranged factual mate
rial, a second gifted author would be able to "show" the opposite' 
(Strasser, 1963, p. 68). Thus, to the sciences-the natural as well as 
the social-the anecdote lacks rigour. As introduced in chapter 2, 
the feminist technoscientific paradigm questions the claim of univer
sal truths in favour of the partial, situated perspective. The universal 
truth has been described by Haraway (1988) as a 'god's eye view'-a 
perspective from nowhere. This perspective, according to Haraway, 
has never existed: science is culture. It has been shaped by socio-po
litical milieus and is instrumental in shaping the same. Haraway 
(1991), in her book Simians, Cyborgs and Women: The Reinvention 
of Nature, used accounts from socio-biological research to illustrate 
how fragile the notion of nature is and how it is constructed rather 

166
than discovered. She retold the story of a group of socio-biologists 
in the early half of the 20th century, who set out to investigate the 
social hierarchies of a group of monkeys. The researchers removed 
the alpha male from the pack and noted that the remaining mon
keys' social structure fell into disarray. The researchers thus draw 
the conclusion that the alpha male naturally rules over the family 
unit. This finding reinforced the contemporary ruling idea of male 
supremacy and lent merit to the idea of the man as the natural head 
of the household or as a boss in the workplace. Decades later, in the 
second half of the 20th century, the study was repeated, except that 
this time another monkey, and not the alpha male, was removed 
from the pack. Also this time, the pack's social structure fell into 
disarray, suggesting that the monkey's social ties are complex and in
tertwined in such a way that if any of them disappear, the remaining 
relationships will be affected. This second study was conducted at a 
time when male superiority was challenged both in the home and the 
workplace. 
 
Nature, as a pre-existing category, is shaped by man-made 
culture and, as such, not neutral. Haraway (1988) suggested that an 
innocent perspective does not exist and advocates instead for a situ
ated approach to science as a way of contesting the faux neutrality 
of the god's eye view. Neutrality is one of the virtues of science. This, 
alongside qualities such as replicability and transparency, is what 
merits scientific research. Producing replicable results-that is, that 
another team of scientists should be able to follow your methods 
and through that generate the same results as the original study-is 
intended as a marker of rigour. This proves that the results were not 
merely a product of chance. Transparency of the method ensures 
the ethical treatment of research subjects. Transparency and replica
bility can also be challenged. Bowker and Star (2000) outlined the 
politics of categorisation, for example, in their book Sorting Things 
Out: Classification and Its Consequences. Here, they followed the 
International Classification of Diseases to discuss the implications 
of practice classifications and standardisations on the medical scien
ces and health care. When symptoms become standardised, they are 
also isolated, and to classify a disease or ailment, the medical profes

167
sional must disregard the rest of the patient's body. Bowker and Star 
(2000) described how this division in biostatistics is necessary for 
the medical sciences, as it helps clinical practitioners to communica
te and share their work across the globe, which in turn is necessary 
for the development of science. This, further, has produced an ex
pansive information infrastructure, connecting clinical professionals 
and researchers while simultaneously inheriting its simplifications 
and remaining tone-deaf towards global indifferences in resources, 
the international power imbalances of language and data that is 
highly susceptive to interpretation (Bowker & Star, 2000). Such an 
infrastructure is not an innocent or neutral structure as it requires 
constant work and resources to create and maintain (Latour, 1987). 
The clinical nature of data 'from nowhere' also risks overlooking 
how classification and standards are generated within a moral, re
ligious and political context: Bowker and Star(2000) provided an 
example of how Christian morality historically has influenced how 
'tabu' matters, such as abortions, suicides or even birth control, has 
been handled. In terms of politics, Bowker and Star showed how 
class impacts diagnoses and how racial classification affects the 
patients' diagnosis on both an individual level and on a structural 
(think, for example, of the apartheid), and Mol (2008) showed how 
political and moral currents are instrumental in shaping the techno
logy through which the data is generated-and again, shaping the 
practice and the interpretations of the data that it generates. 
 
This, the infected and affected nature of the sciences, is not 
contained in the natural and medical sciences either. Among others, 
sociologist Law (2004) illustrated how social scientists not only des
cribe social realities, for example, through classification, but also 
create them. Law (2004) wrote, 
'Method in social sciences (and natural sciences too) is enac
ted with a set of nineteenth- or even seventeenth-century, 
euro-American blinkers. This means that it misunderstands 
and misrepresents itself. Method is not [...] a more or less 
successful set of procedures for reporting on a given reality. 
Rather, it is performative. It helps to produce realities' (p. 

168
143). 
 
The notion of inventive methods departs from this tension 
within the social sciences: methods are performative and generative. 
In the anthology Inventive Methods: The Happening of the Social 
(Lury & Wakeford, 2012), Lury and Wakeford (2012) curated a 
gallery of approaches that social scientists may use in conversation 
with the situation. Inventive methods, as named, are responsive. 
The editors explained in the introduction of the book that an in
ventive method is not necessarily always new. An inventive method 
is separated from conventional unwavering scientific methods by 
elements of flexibility and adaptability. Inventive methods should 
introduce answerability into a problem (Lury & Wakeford, 2012). 
It is congruent wito ethics of care, which also rests upon a research 
sensibility and sensitivity to be attentive and to perform case-by-case 
deliberations (Tronto, 1993). 
 
Thus far, this text has predominately concerned itself with 
the notions of hermeneutics in the natural and social sciences. Most 
importantly, the anecdotalization that this chapter is about to dis
cuss diverges from such hermeneutics. However, notably, within de
sign research in particular and the humanities in general, anecdota
lization is more established. As an ethnographic practice, anecdotes 
and stories are commonly used to draw insight from experiences 
(Orr, 1986; Orr & Crowfoot, 1992; Suchman et al., 2017; Whyte, 
1991) and to underpin situated actions (Suchman, 1987). Within 
participatory design, anecdotes are a vital part of the practice and 
used as a method for coming together (Whitcomb, 2016) as a way 
of grounding imagination (Büscher et al., 2004) and as an integral 
part of the PD practice, making it at times overlooked. Participatory 
designers use stories and storytelling within almost everything they 
do: Communication is more than agreement and disagreement. In 
this thesis, I have placed considerable emphasis on sharing perspec
tives (and will continue to do so), but anecdotalization is a nod to 
something beyond that. As Ehn reflected in 1988 on his own use of 
Wittgenstein's (2010) language games35 and remarked that by 'focu
sing on how perspectives makes us select certain aspects of "reality" 
as important in a description, I completely oversaw my own pre

169
sumption that descriptions in one way or another are "mirror-ima
ges" of "reality"' (Ehn, 1988b, p. 144). Anecdotalization is more 
than mere description: It is the social practice of making, hearing, 
telling, re-telling, altering and changing with descriptions. For infra
structuring practitioners, the infrastructure becomes an intricately 
woven fabric of resources, history, knowledge and practices (Bucci
arelli, 1994), and one actor rarely-nay impossible-has a complete 
overview (Hewitt, 1986). Without collaborative descriptions, those 
alignments that infrastructuring practitioners seek are impossible to 
identify, understand and create (Neuman & Star, 1996), and this is 
why I have sought to articulate using collaborative anecdotalization 
as a praxis. In this chapter, I will introduce anecdotalization as a 
method within infrastructuring processes: adding the word 'colla
borative' before anecdotalization to differentiate between the social 
sciences' inventive methods and the proposed method for co-design 
and infrastructuring. As mentioned in the beginning of the chap
ter, my proposition for collaborative anecdotalization within infra
structuring processes is three-fold: a way of being within research, a 
way of co-creating knowledge and a way of communicating resear
ch. 
6.1 ANECDOTALIZATION
 
The term anecdotalization was, as mentioned, coined by so
ciologist Mike Michael in the anthology Inventive Methods: The 
Happening of the Social (Lury & Wakeford, 2012). Michael pro
posed the anecdote as doing, emphasising its social affordances. I 
began using the term anecdotalization to describe a reflective praxis 
in 2018 during the time I was working with Amiralsstaden. Shortly 
after the 'incident' with Eva, many voices (from participants, archi
35 Wittgenstein (2010) suggested that the way language is used and understood 
hold meaning only as long as the users understand the rules. He described it as 
partaking in language games (for a more in-dept discussion on language games 
and participatory design see Ehn, 1988a).

170
tects, social workers, a broad selection of civil servants, research
ers and students) were competing to describe the events. Seemingly, 
everybody wanted to chime in with their opinions and perspectives 
on what happened and suggested strategies on what should be and 
should have been done. Interests and interpretations were disparate, 
and while a few people would adapt the interpretation of a group le
ader-the strongest voice within a constellation-the many different 
accounts seemed to make it difficult to actually do something. To 
move forward, we needed to agree on a direction, and as we could 
not agree on what the reality of the situation was, this became nearly 
impossible. I was reminded of Reddy's (1979) 'Conduit Metaphor', 
as he presented in his chapter 'Toolmakers Paradigm'. Here, Reddy 
(1979) asked us to imagine a cake shape, such as the one you are 
seeing in the image. In each slice of the cake, there is a world, each 
with its specific conditions. Imagine that one is a world made of 
stone, another is a world made of trees, and so on and so forth. 
The people in the separate worlds cannot communicate with each 
other in any way other than through a fax machine that is positio
ned at the centre of the cake, and even this fax machine cannot send 
anything but pictures, no words. Reddy (1979) then presented us 
with the scenario that a person in the tree world invents a new tool. 
Say a rake to gather leaves from all the trees. He thinks that this is a 
great invention and wishes to share it with the person in the world 
next to him, and so faxes over a drawing of the rake. The receiver, 
a person in the stone world, however, does not have a lot of leaves 
to gather or the right materials to make the rake according to the 
original design. They assume it must be a lever of sorts to lift stones 
with. Helpful as this person is, they correct the drawing and send it 
back through the fax. Eventually, through this iterative process of 
sending the wordless sketch of a simple tool back and forth, the two 
neighbours begin to learn something about their respective worlds. 
They see that the world they have taken for granted is not necessa
rily shared, and the only way to learn this is to engage in dialogue. 
The conduit metaphor is simplified, but it illustrates the constraints 
inherent in our communication with others. As a researcher, I will 
be sensitised to some forms of systemic oppression, for example, but 

171
may initially be blind to others. Inherited power relations may ex
ist between the participants and/or conflicts of interest. Navigating 
complex situations and constellations means engaging with different 
ethical perspectives, and arguably, many ethical discourses have been 
generated to legitimise the dominant power structures of those who 
benefit from them (Robertson & Wagner, 2013, p. 66). The ethical 
case deliberation required to mediate such opposing ethical and po
litical stances can perhaps be referred to, similar to Mol's (2008), 
as ongoing maintenance work. Within Amiralsstaden, this is what 
I saw. People send one-way 'faxes' with their perspective without 
realising how much of that information was coded in their professi
onal vocabulary or their personal standpoint. Naturally, when, for 
example, a city planner proposed a direction forward-based on 
his or her definition of the situation-their solution did not fit the 
issue as other groups may have seen it. As such, moving on with the 
flat hierarchy that Amiralsstaden sought to apply, that is, without 
resorting to a top-down decision-making process, was made difficult 
at the time. I was also feeling torn and overwhelmed, struggling to 
make sense of the many descriptions circling around. 
 
As a Ph.D. student, it follows that I would bring some of the 
situations I had experienced in the project back to my supervisors 
and would retell them to get help and guidance. Having collected 
some of my supervisors' perspectives, I revised my understanding 
of the situation and returned to the Amiralsstaden venue, where I 
told one of my collaborators, Anja, of how I now view what hap
pened. She, as it turns out, had similarly discussed the events with 
a colleague of hers and shared their views with me. Together, over 
a cup of coffee, we revised the anecdotes of the same incident until 
we merged our narratives into one. This, in turn, we shared with the 
rest of the group at one of the weekly meetings where the definition 
we had made again came up against contrasting perspectives. Slowly 
and iteratively, we began to use the anecdote both as a way of under
standing each other and as a way of moving forward together. 
 
In retrospect, I began to describe this process as anecdota
lization. By this, I mean, in congruence with, that the process of 
telling the anecdote does more than the account in and of itself. It 

172
becomes a way of sense-making and generating different kinds of 
knowledge. In the beginning of this chapter, I discussed the scientific 
aversion towards anecdotal evidence, and much of the same criti
que can be directed towards the knowledge created through anec
dotalization. By proposing anecdotalization as a method, I wish to 
address three critiques in particular: the subjective stance, the ethics 
of the anecdotal practice and the legitimacy of developing theory 
grounded through anecdotalization. 
6.1.1 WEAK THEORIES AND REPARATIVE READINGS
 
In the beginning of this chapter, I delved into the technos
cientific discussion of what and how we can know things and con
cluded that anecdotalization is an inventive research method and, 
firstly, a relational approach. This was also my main focus during 
the anecdotalization process with Amiralsstaden: to find a way of 
being within the mess and finding a method to generate something 
out of it. But then, once we talked and agreed about a direction and 
a next step, I found myself with an outcome that I did not quite have 
the vocabulary to describe. It was comparable to a prototype-a 
what-if, a trial-a comparison that I still sometimes make, but can
not always justify. In the section below, I will elaborate on this com
parison further, but for now, an anecdote is adequately comparable 
to, but is not, a prototype in the manner that most design theory 
would describe a prototype. Furthermore, the anecdotalization pro
cess does not generate anything that could be described as a grand 
unified theory. I tried measuring it against grounded theory (Char
maz, 2014; Glaser & Strauss, 1967), but the method I had used 
lacked the neat management of chaos that grounded theory favours. 
While the grounded theory researcher submits herself to the mess in 
that she does not make a hypothesis of where the research will lead 
her but allows the theory to emerge through empirical and literary 
engagement, she still holds on to the scientific virtue of transparen
cy. Reading a (good) piece of grounded theory research, you will, in 
hindsight, be able to trace the steps of the research backwards and 

173
determine exactly where the ideas and definitions had come from. 
The mess that I was left with was far more entangled, and even if I 
wanted to, I could never have disentangled it; as such, a small part 
of the outcome was 'mine'. This was the issue, but it was also the 
strength of the outcome! 'We figured this out together', I thought, 
'There must be a way to describe this outcome as scientifically valu
able, without pretending that it is something it is not'. In summary, 
my question was: if anecdotalization is a method, what does the 
method do? what is it for?
 
In a paper entitled Weak Theory in an Unfinished World, 
Stewart (2008) suggested that there is value in attending to 'the cul
tural poesis of forms of living. Its objects are textures and rhythms, 
trajectories and modes of attunement [sic], attachment and com
position. The point is not to judge the value of these objects or to 
somehow get their representation "right" but to wonder where they 
might go and what potential modes of knowing, relating and atten
ding to things are already present in them' (p. 71). Stewart (2008) 
argued that we can become deaf, dumb and blind to our surroun
dings and easily become ill-attentive to the ways in which we per
form within them. A weak theory is drawn together by a live game 
of connect-the-dots and composed of small observations. In her pa
per, she illustrated the weak theories with an anecdote about a man 
who walks into a café after being in a bike accident. The people in 
the café hear the man's story, and each of them offers an 'explana
tion' for the accident ('the roads out here are in terrible conditions', 
'since the traffic was moved to the highway the politicians do not 
prioritise that stretch anymore', 'the weather...', 'the bike', etc.)-a 
moment of poesis, which triggers reactions from the people in the 
coffee shop: questions, observations and theories. Connecting the 
dots between the overpopulation of deer, roadworks, legislation and 
weather conditions: 'the poesis of the ordinary' (ibid., p. 74). Weak 
theories in this sense are also more than merely descriptive: They 
make out relations and connections and suggest a next step. They 
are 'good enough' (Gabrys & Pritchard, 2018) to inspire action.
 
The scene that Stewart (2008) described in her coffee shop, 

174
of the worlds being brought together through anecdotal accounts, is 
what I would describe as a kind of anecdotalization. Collaborative 
anecdotalization would be if the people in the coffee shop had retur
ned again next week to continue discussing the matter or when they 
all went back home and continued talking about it to their friends 
and family. 
6.1.2 ANECDOTE: WEAK THEORIES AND AGORAPHOBIA 
 
Weak theories present something to be catious about, which 
I discovered during the Livskonceptet project. Here, we (the orga
nising group from Malmö University and Save the Children) had 
called to a co-lab where we invited different groups in the area to 
workshops or meetings with the organising group and some repre
sentatives from the municipality. During these meetings, we saw 
how these weak theories could be both formed and reformulated. 
A woman, who had been participating in the co-lab in the role of a 
mother with recent live experience of arriving new to Sweden (one 
of the groups invited into the co-lab) told us the stories she had en
countered in her first months in the country. She had spoken to other 
people with a recent permanent placement and heard many horror 
stories about Sweden, Lund and Swedes in general. She told us how 
the world around her seemed hostile and dangerous and that she 
avoided interacting with it as much as possible. She stayed home, 
refrained from seeking contact with authorities if possible and spent 
very little time in and around the city. Eventually, as time passed, 
she could no longer avoid going outside, and once there, she began 
to find things that she liked: a park, a café and a friend. From this, 
possibilities were revealed, and she told us in the co-lab that she now 
goes that extra mile to tell other newcomers of the good things she 
has found, as well as the cautionary tales and the practical details. 
She shows them the nice spot in the park and tells them 'the happy 
stories' of the good times she had. Warnings and information are 
important, she explained, but the good stories are just as much. She 
told us that the warnings she had been given had become self-fulfil

175
ling prophecies. If the world around us seems hostile, we may easily 
collect proof and exemplification to support this.
6.1.3 PARANOID AND REPARATIVE READINGS
 
In the section below, I will return to the question of self-ful
filling prophesies, but before that, I wish to address one final ques
tion regarding weak theories. A reasonable question to ask, with 
regard to any kind of theory, is what does it do? What is it for? Also, 
weak theories seem to provoke these kinds of questions more often 
than other theories. Within academia, we are trained to follow 'aca
demic protocols like maintaining distance, outsmarting (and other 
forms of one-upmanship), ref surprised (or if you are, then not let
ting on), believing the hierarchy, becoming the boss' (Love, 2010, p. 
236). Following queer feminist thinkers such as Love (2010), hooks 
(1984, 1999) and Sedgwick (2003), I wish to question whether the 
relevance of the weak theories does not lie in the stories they tell 
but in the way that we read them. Sedgwick (2003) used the term 
'reparative reading' to describe a practice of approaching theory for 
the sake of experimentation and pleasure rather than deconstruc
tion. This concept is in contrast to what she calls 'paranoid reading', 
where the reader approaches the work defensively. She asked how 
knowledge is performative and wrote: 
'[T]hat an ethic or aesthetic of truthtelling need not depend 
on any reified notion of truth; that the materiality of human 
bodies, of words, and of economic production may misre
present but cannot simply eclipse one another; that pleasure, 
grief, excitement, boredom, satisfaction are the substance of 
politics rather than their antithesis; that affect and cognition 
are not very distant processes [...] By accountability to the 
real I mean in the first place the many, diverse, but very mar
ked turns these essays take away from existing accounts of 
how "one" should read, and back toward a grappling with 
the recalcitrant, fecund question of how one does. It might 

176
even be true to say that the psychological/political ambitions 
of many of the essays take the form of a similar series of 
turns: from the nonsensical but seemingly uncircumnaviga
ble question of how people should feel to the much harder 
ones of how they do and of how feelings change.' (Sedgwick, 
2003, p. 1-2) 
 Sedgwick's (2003) proposition of the reparative approach 
to theory resonates with the critique directed towards PAR as fe
tishising pain narratives. Tuck and Yang (2014) wrote that 'it is a 
misconception that by simply building participation in a project-
by increasing the number of people who collaborate in collecting 
data-ethical issues of representation, voice, consumption, and 
voyeurism are resolved' (p. 10). Anecdotalization differs from other 
forms of participant-researcher interactions in its element iteration, 
and the notion of reparative reading teaches us that this could be a 
subversive method: a way to step out of scrutiny and the critical gaze 
and instead use the knowledge-creating process as a way of mutual 
learning. 
 
Within Amiralsstaden, diversity among the participants and 
their participation was certainly one of the project's key priorities. 
The tension that Eva exposed was that despite good intentions, not 
all voices were equally present and that was not due to Eva's lack 
of effort. The contribution of feminist queer theory and the notion 
of reparative reading here show that the listener, that is, the wo
man who sat down with Eva and an interpreter and made time to 
hear Eva's argument, emerges as the key to resolving the situation. 
She did not solve Eva's problem, but she solved the affective tangle 
within the working group, which allowed the group to come back 
tomorrow and continue working together.
6.1.4 TWO TRUTHS AND A LIE: THE AFFORDANCES OF 
METADATA
The issue described in the section above-of how we filter stories 

177
around us into self-fulfilling prophecies-is one side of an anecdotal 
coin. The other side is the lies we deliberately tell others to sway them 
towards our way of thinking. Many researchers working with PAR, 
participatory design, and most research disciplines that require longterm engagement and relationships with the research participants 
recognise the difficulty of unbiased accounts. First, the notion of the 
researcher 'taking sides' exists, which has been previously discussed 
in this chapter as situated knowledge (Haraway, 1988). Second, the 
issue of 'many truths' is revealed, which was discussed earlier in this 
thesis as a 'multiplicity' or 'many world worlds'. However, several 
grey areas occur regarding the participants' lies: think, for example, 
of the lies they tell you to make you happy because it is what you 
want to hear. Yndigegn (2015) shared an anecdote about this in her 
dissertation Managing resistance and negotiating co-design: Reflec
tions on troublesome and elusive moments when a participant was 
concerned that she may not have performed in the way that the en
gagement event(s) had anticipated. The participant, who had been 
working alongside Yndigegn (2015), was worried that she would 
not have anything to 'write about for your project' (p. 7). An anec
dote that illustrates that care goes in two ways.
 
In a bid to develop the idea of anecdotalization, I worked 
with Save The Children and TiF in 2019 to develop a summer pro
gramme for Malmö Municipality. This project was the Demokra
tiambassadörerna, and the participants were exclusively teenagers 
living in Malmö. The role and the relationship this gave me towards 
the participants differed greatly from what I had previously expe
rienced with Amiralsstaden. Within Amiralsstaden, I worked along
side the same group for several years; we were equals and collabo
rators. Within Demokratiambassadörerna, this was not the case; I 
was only there for the duration of the project, and only four weeks 
were spent with each group. My relationship with these participants 
became more akin to a teacher than a friend, and as the project 
began, I was unprepared for what this would mean. The teenagers 
gathered stories of Malmö, of their lives and of the lives of people 
that they met. Some of these stories were untrue-not all but some. 
There were those who, like in Signe's anecdote, told me things they 

178
thought I wanted to hear. Because they wanted to 'do well', perhaps 
or because they liked me. Or because they thought that the easiest 
way out of the task was to do what they thought we expected of 
them. Others told me lies because they did not like me or perhaps 
because they wanted to appear cool and nonchalant in front of their 
peers. At the end of the project, I was sieving through all the hand
written stories I had received over the summer, literary thousands, 
and I took out those that were obvious lies. The pile of those stories 
was put to the side, and I invested my time and effort in the accounts 
that I considered 'serious' (true). Until one afternoon, a few days 
later (when I was supposed to be working on something completely 
different), my attention drifted back into the pile of discarded sto
ries. Something in those stories held an attraction, and I could not 
stop myself from rereading them. They were entertaining to read, 
oten exaggerated and dramatic. Some of them were thinly veiled 
critiques of the summer programme or the unflattering caricatures 
of their peers. These accounts were not truthful, per se, but in the 
humour, in the critiques and in the refusals, was there not something 
of value?36 
 
Michael (2012a) argued that anecdotalization moves beyond 
other kinds of auto-ethnographic approaches, as it is 'useful for ex
plicitly incorporating the performativity of research-i.e., the way 
that research is not a mere reflection of something (e.g., one's expe
riences in relation to social and cultural processes) out there, but is 
instrumental in, and a feature of, the 'making of out theres' (p. 26). 
As the anecdote is fluid, it is 'combing the real and the constructed, 
and holding them in tension' (Michael, 2012a, p. 27). The anecdote 
has the ability to transcend historical accounts by not only descri
bing or reporting on them but through acting on events. It carries 
a lesson, a piece of life experience, and, as with the stories in the 
"rejected pile", these accounts are often of a more intimate nature. 
36 This thesis does not rest upon discourse analysis, and as such, I will not be 
delving deeper into what these meta-narratives said but seek only to make note 
of their performativity. 

179
These accounts differ from, for example, oath-sworn court testimo
nies as they are constructed and partial narratives. However, I argue 
that semi-fictional quality is often more of an embellishment than 
an outright lie. For example, if an event feels terrifying, we may em
bellish our retelling of the event to communicate the feeling of fear 
to the listener. Even in the 'lying', a meta-data can be found (Fujii, 
2010). To say that the value of an account solely rests on whether it 
is true or false would be to detach it from its political, social and his
torical context. Political scientist Fujii (2010) conducted interviews 
in rural areas of Rwanda 10 years after the genocides and found 
that the interviewees' stories are often shaped by, for example, their 
socio-political belonging; as such, the lies told can be as informative 
as a truthful account. She writes:
'Meta-data can take both spoken and unspoken forms. They 
include rumours, silences, and invented stories. Meta-data 
are as valuable as the testimonies themselves because they 
indicate how the current social and political landscape is 
shaping what people might say to a researcher. By failing to 
attend to meta-data, analysts risk misinterpreting ambigui
ties, overlooking important details, drawing incorrect con
clusions, and leaving informants vulnerable to reprisals for 
having talked to the researcher. Paying attention to meta-da
ta is therefore vital to protecting informants and arriving at 
robust explanations and theories'. (Fujii, 2010, p. 232)
Similarly, the primary concern of the anecdotes that Demokratiam
bassadörerna gathered is not whether they were completely truthful. 
In the process of anecdotalization, the teenagers were able to sieve 
the accounts they had heard and make out the embellishment from 
the reality that they portray. Several accounts on a shared topic could 
reinforce or dispute each other. Furthermore, the process of colla
borative anecdotalization is generative: The teenagers would often 
subtract from the day's anecdotes, wh was more than the sum total 
of each individual anecdote37. When speaking of collaborative anec
dotalization, we may think of the anecdote as a gift we offer to our 

180
friends, foes, family or colleagues, hoping they will find its content 
entertaining or useful. It involves building and maintaining a rela
tionship. If the gift were to remain unreciprocated or unappreciated, 
we would soon stop offering it. By unreciprocated, it is not to say 
that the receiver did not appreciate or agree with our account, but 
rather the opposite. During my observations within Amiralsstaden 
and Demokratiambassadörerna, it seemed that the consensus was 
often the most effective killer of an anecdote, and what reinforced it 
was when it became contested. Through disagreement and constant 
iteration, anecdotalization was kept alive. Star and Strauss (1999) 
described this as an articulation work: A live practice that builds 
connections, building meaning and identity and revising ontologies. 
6.2 COLLABORATIVE ANECDOTALIZATION
 
The term collaborative anecdotalization has been used in 
this thesis to differentiate it somewhat from the concept of anecdo
talization. Collaborative anecdotalization is predominately intended 
as a concept for and within infrastructuring. Working with infra
structuring is not a linear process, but rather one in which oppor
tunities and dilemmas are unveiled and addressed along the way. In 
such processes, bringing together non-heterogenous publics, infra
structuring is often confronted with wicked problems (Buchanan, 
1992; Rittel & Webber, 1973). This places heightened importance 
on coming together and communicating among the participants.
 
 
Within design research, much has been written about pro
totypes and the process of prototyping. Since the 1990s, professio
nal design disciplines have evolved and come into contact with new 
practices and customs, such as social innovation (Thackara, 2006). 
Moving into new spheres prompted the design disciplines to defi
37 This account has been previously published in Smedberg, 2021b.

181
ne themselves-what does design bring to the interdisciplinary ta
ble? Problem solving has long been a defining feature of the design 
principles and prototyping a staple approach. Through prototyping, 
design becomes a material dialogue with a situation (Schön, 1982; 
Schön, 1987), but this prototyping varies indefinitely. Most com
monly, we speak of a material engagement-from the material sensi
tivity of, say, a textile designer, or the socio-material prototyping of 
a cardboard computer (Ehn & Kyng, 1991) and a process of iterati
ve experimentation. Oftentimes, these processes should result in ob
jective products (Sawyer, 1998), but many also depart from the phy
sical artefact and work with design as an event (Wilkie, 2014) and 
a more intangible kind of prototyping. Wilkie (2014) discussed the 
prototype as a relational and processual practice and as 'an inventi
ve problem making'. In the paper The Prototype: More than any and 
less than one, Jiménez (2014) addressed 'the prototype as something 
that happens to social relationships when one approaches the craft 
and agency of objects in particular ways' (p. 381). Jiménez (2014) 
juxtaposed the unwinding for-profit and efficiency systems of pro
duction with the open-endedness of experimentation and emphasi
sed, for example, the incorporation of failure as an important aspect 
of the process. The prototype, in its most essential definition, seems 
to be the development of a design proposal. A proposal is, according 
to anthropologist Miyazaki (2006), something that encompasses 
both the past and the vision of the future-something that may be 
opened, engaged with and attended to through a method of hope. I 
find Miyazaki's (2006) definition of the design proposal noteworthy 
in the discussion of prototype, as it taps into that generative ability 
of the prototype-the will beyond the need to move forward, the 
curiosity for something else. The prototyping process is more than 
mere problem solving-it also has hope. Miyazaki (2006) propo
sed the Fijian understanding of hope as a methodological problem: 
when venturing into 'what has "not-yet" come [...] allowing them 
to discover that reality is still in a state of not-yets' (p. 9). Anecdota
lization, as well as prototyping, grapples with this open-mindedness 
and the moment of poiesis that openness might bring. An anecdote 
that is too 'clean' to rehearse will most likely stagnate: The colla

182
borative performativity of anecdotalization resides in the motion of 
co-defining, challenging the anecdote, drawing alignments with it 
or adding to it. Once this process dies down, the anecdote will no 
longer be an explorative tool, and we might say that it is a receipt 
for something that was rather than a 'not-yet'. 
 
Although the anecdote does not fit every description of a 
prototype, it does encapsulate open-endedness and the moment of 
poiesis. What connects them both with the Fijian understanding 
of hope is the collaborative aspect: the ability of coming together 
around a 'not-yet' and a collective envisioning of what might pos
sibly emerge. The outcome of a prototyping process within a design 
project is both a device and a thing (Telier, 2011, p. 158)-the so
cio-material collective. In the next three sections of the text, I will 
propose three ways that the collaborative anecdotalization process 
may perform within a design research project. First, as a way of be
ing within infrastructures: A way of relating to, forming understan
dings and framing issues and possibilities. I suggest that collaborati
ve anecdotalization can afford the researcher to navigate affectively 
charged socio-material infrastructures with increased sensitivity. Se
cond, as a way of co-creating knowledge: An inventive (collective) 
problem making through dialogue. When working with groups with 
disparate levels of decision-making power, the academic narrative 
always has a risk of falling on the side of those bodies within the 
status quo, which confirms the ruling ideas rather than generating 
new wisdom. 
 
Finally, I propose the collaborative anecdotalization process 
as a methodological approach, which challenges the way we tell the 
thing and the way we communicate research from the thing. 
6.2.1 BEING WITHIN THE INFRASTRUCTURE(S)
 
In 1988, Ehn published his doctoral dissertation Work-Orien
ted Design of Computer Artifacts, where he, amongst many other 

183
things, discussed how language games are an integral part of any 
design practice. Following a pragmatic, Deweyian logic that says 
'We live in communication' with one another (Telier, 2011), Ehn 
looked to Wittgenstein (2010) to understand how a professional 
practice is shaped by the unwritten rules of language games. Ehn 
gives an example of a carpenter labelling his tools (nails, draw-knife, 
chisels, etc.), materials (elm planks etc.) or methods (saddling, stea
ming) and asked, 'Are we as designers of new artefacts [...] helped 
by this labelling of artefacts, materials and activities?' (Ehn, 1988a, 
p. 104). Within Wittgenstein's (2010) theory, we find the answer 
that language is a practice or a use: It is something we participate in 
(Glock, 1996). 'Language is not private but social' (ibid.). Wittgens
tein (2010) stated that we learn to participate in certain language 
games, a rule-following behaviour in which the rules have not been 
previously specified (Telier, 2011). 
 
This perspective-that the practice is something that we 
make and something that, in return, makes us-has become one of 
the fundamental ideas of participatory design. It is also congruent 
with Arendt's notions of the public sphere. To Arendt, the public sp
here has two dimensions: the space of appearance and the common 
world. Shared between these dimensions is that the public sphere is 
constructed, spatial and its inherent quality fluctuates between pri
vate and public interests. The two realms are not in competition with 
one another, but complementary. The value of the private realm is 
seen in the public realm; the public realm is very much in need of the 
private. The political potency, however, lies in the public realm-
and this is something that the modern reader may struggle with. In
creasingly, the modern world has depicted the private as something 
good and desirable. In Arendt's reading, the enlargement of the pri
vate sphere-consuming things that traditionally have belonged to 
the public sphere, such as work-is becoming part of the private. 
What Arendt brings us through this distinction is a participatory 
concept of citizenship and the imperative of collective political agen
cy (Passerin, 2019). Infrastructures, as the networks that connect us, 
are constantly being made and remade. This presence, like Wittgens

184
tein's (2010) language game, is governed by innumerable rules of 
engagement. The affective plain described in the previous chapter is 
one of them that calls for an emotional and social praxis. However, 
many co-existing factors are found in each infrastructure, and many 
of these dimensions (e.g. professional, technical, material, political, 
etc.) have their own rules for engagement. This entanglement of pri
orities is considered to be participatory design's most fundamental 
raison d'être: How do we enter into this entangled mess without 
getting stuck in it? Without getting tied up and hindered from doing 
anything else? How do we push forward, knowing that some of the
se rules contradict one another? We may cause damage in some ways 
(or causes or people) as we elevate others. 
 
Articulating collaborative anecdotalization does not seek 
to infuse participatory design with new rules for engagement, but 
rather to help us make the rules already in place a bit more transpa
rent for everyone present. It invites each of the participants into the 
process of defining the project and as an inventive engagement with 
the situation as well as making it. The act of passing short descrip
tions of live realities back and forth becomes a way of investigating 
the infrastructure and making priorities within the infrastructure 
and reveals the possibility of acting within the infrastructure. 
 
The proposition of collaborative anecdotalization is by no 
means an innocent proposition. In the Harawayian sense, it is a pro
position from somewhere guilty of wanting to do something. It is 
still an attempt, as PD has always been, to work with the marginali
sed voices within the constellation, and it does not erase the need to 
take issues such as class, race, sexuality and ability into account. 
6.2.2 CO-CREATING KNOWLEDGE WITHIN INFRA
STRUCTURES
In the intersection between participatory design and critical femi
nist pedagogies, you find words like 'emancipation'. Ehn (1988a) 
departed from Marxist epistemology and from the critical peda

185
gogies of Freire (DiSalvo et al., 2017) when he conceptualised an 
'emancipatory practice' (Ehn, 1988a, p. 92). Ehn outlined how dia
lectical methods of enquiry are commonplace for transcending our 
understanding of a matter, but he questioned whether this sui ge
neris means that such a dialectical method is a Marxist approach. 
He reasoned that it is not 'because to a Marxist approach it is of 
great importance which questions are asked' (Ehn, 1988a, p. 92). 
Dialectical, agonistic reasoning does not exist outside the world. I 
draw a similar conclusion regarding the 'innocence' of collaborative 
anecdotalization, as described above. The anecdotalization, with its 
dialogical and inventive rhythm, lends itself well to the emancipato
ry pedagogies of, for example, Freire (1970): it encourages recipro
city, mutual learning and talking, as well as listening. These radical 
tools can change the world or at least alter an infrastructure. In the 
words of Freire (1970), 'Just as objective social reality exists not by 
chance, but as the product of human action, so it is not transformed 
by chance' (p. 33).
 
I am proposing collaborative anecdotalization as a method 
of existing within and challenging an infrastructure. This process is 
necessary because every infrastructure comes as a product of those 
that have gone before and is, in the words of Star and Ruhleder 
(1996), built on an installed base. As such, the infrastructure will 
inherent structural ills and reproduce intersectional oppression. Ah
med (2016) showed how intersectional oppression manifests itself in 
the walls and traffic systems. Her antidote is, as previously discus
sed, the killjoy. 
 
In the prologue of this dissertation, I wrote that my rela
tionship to design has always been tainted by the tension between 
the need to act and the illegitimacy of acting. I began writing this 
thesis to try to understand when it is right to step in and when we 
need to step back. Collaborative anecdotalization holds these two 
poles in orbit around each other. The offensive strategies of Ahmed's 
killjoy must partner with the reparative pedagogy of Sedgwick. 

186
6.2.3 COMMUNICATING RESEARCH
 
To use the method of collaborative anecdotalization as a 
means of relating within a project or as a way of co-creating the 
infrastructure, we initially had only two propositions that this thesis 
intended. One day in 2019, when I stood in front of a room full of 
people at the Nordes Conference in Helsinki, I told them an anecdo
te about Eva (Smedberg, 2019). In hindsight, I have wrestled with 
the moral and ethical implications of using this kind of narrative as 
an 'academic output'. My presentation was well received (at least as 
far as I could tell), and I was particularly heartwarmed by two wo
men who worked within the civil sector who expressed their recog
nition of the story. One of them raised her hand in the Q&A session 
after my talk and initiated her question with, 'I wasn't sure I did the 
right thing coming to this conference, as I am not an academic, but 
your story resonated with me'. This account says something about 
the value of anecdotes within academia: They have the power to 
embody and put a face on knowledge that would have otherwise 
remained faceless.
 
 
Arendt used this kind of anecdote and embodied historical 
accounts to tell stories of history and politics. This is visible in, for 
example, her analysis of barbarism, in which she used the life of 
Rosa Luxemburg as an 'anchor' for her analysis (Arendt, 1968). It 
has been suggested (Benhabib, 1990) that as Arendt stepped into the 
ring of European political philosophy, she struggled to concur with 
her Jewish identity. Her own conflicting identity comes through as 
she writes about Europe during the war. Instead of accounting for 
the grand narrative of World War Two, she anchored her histori
cal retelling into Eichmann (Arendt, 1963). Instead of writing the 
grand theory around political resistance, she gave it the face of Rosa 
Luxembourg (1968). With anecdotes from the lives of small people 
in the grand currents of history, Arendt taught us that what has 
happened once to one person is not unimportant. Arendt held that 
political theory is storytelling (Arendt, 1951; Benhabib, 1990). 
 
Arendt distinguished judgment from empathy (Arendt, 

187
1954; Benhabib, 1988), suggesting that 'Historical judgment revea
led the nature of the shared social world by representing its plurality 
in narrative form. At stake in such representation was the ability 
"to take the standpoint of the other", and this did not mean empa
thizing or even sympathizing other, but rather the ability to recreate 
the world through the eyes of others' (Benhabib, 1988, p. 183, as 
cited in Arendt, 1954). The historian, according to Arendt, could 
only recreate these accounts, as far as her own imagination allowed 
her. Collaborative anecdotalization differs from this kind of 'judg
mental' historical account, as the narrative presented is the outcome 
of a collaborative weaving process. The standpoint presented is not 
the projection of the one actor judging the others but the collective 
recreation of the world through the eyes of the 'us'. 
 
The day I presented the anecdote about Eva at Nordes in 
Finland was also the day I was first confronted with the notion of 
'confessional ethics'. I have reflected on this notion that the reason 
why I tell the stories from my field work in this anecdotal manner, 
and often as a self-critique (Smedberg, 2019, 2021), should be a way 
to 'wash my hands' of an ethical or moral dilemma. I disagree, as 
I have outlined previously in this chapter: Innocence was never an 
option; my hands were never clean. I think it is inevitable when wor
king with participation to not get entangled, and it is inevitable that 
this entanglement does not lead to some kind of loyalty. Yndigegn 
(2015) was confronted with a similar concern during her Ph. D. pro
ject and reasoned, 'This means that there is loyalty, which you feel 
you are betraying when putting on the reflexive and critical hat-a 
loyalty that can be difficult to handle in the descriptive and analy
tical work. At the same time, it is necessary' (p. 62). I agree that an 
academic must remain critical and that this critique is usually the 
contribution we make to our co-participants. However, in terms of 
writing history, I am still questioning who I am in the right to judge 
per se. Can I tell a participant how to interact with an engagement 
event (Michael, 2012b)? 
 
I am going to leave here an open suggestion that the anec

188
dote can be viewed as a prototype within a project and that the out
come of the anecdotalization, such as design-after-design, can be an 
open-ended, configurable thing. How it performs must be determi
ned by whomever its user is. I think this was illustrated quite well by 
the teenagers who had been part of the Demokratiambassadörerna 
project. In a reflection workshop after the programme had finished, 
I asked some of them if they felt they had gained anything from the 
summer programme. Have the activities been valuable to them? And 
if so, how? I confess that I asked this question with a slight fear that 
the teenagers might not have been satisfied with the outcome of the 
programme-that they might have hoped for a bigger, more drastic 
impact. One of the young women in the group told me that she did 
not care about what Malmö municipality or academia, made of the 
stories. 'We still use the stories. I use them in my work here [with 
TiF]; when I'm talking to my friends or my family. I'm still learning 
from them'. In respectful disagreement with the young woman's sta
tement above, it does matter how the public sector and academia 
receive and listen to the anecdotes and stories the teenagers collect 
(Smedberg, 2021b). However, maybe it is okay that we use the sto
ries differently? Maybe it is okay that I, as a researcher, use a story 
to discuss research ontologies at a conference in Finland. Meanwhi
le, one of the participants used the same story to contextualise so
mething in conversation with her mother. Maybe both of these uses 
are valuable? Maybe they are 'good enough' (Gabrys & Pritchard, 
2018)? Collaborative anecdotalization, as presented in this chapter, 
brings us a reciprocal, inventive method that follows a 'reparative' 
pedagogy, where listening is as important as talking and sharing is 
valued as a co-becoming or co-creating of definitions. Collaborative 
anecdotalization is concerned with more than just communicating 
research; it becomes performative through how the stories travel 
and what they 'do'. That is what makes them good enough.
6.3 CLOSING REMARKS
Design research has historically posed a challenge to the conventio

189
nal sciences. The markers of 'good science' can at times be difficult 
to translate into the praxis of design research. How can we, for ex
ample, ensure transparency in a situated practice? In this chapter, I 
have discussed meta-data as an alternative way of viewing transpa
rency, suggesting that embellishments and lies may be evidence of 
something beyond the descriptive narrative. The chapter has argu
ed that something is won by looking carefully at these narratives, 
borrowing the term 'reparative reading' from radical queer feminist 
theory. What is generated here may perhaps not be replicable, per se, 
but may be 'good enough' to generate action. The proposal of col
laborative anecdotalization as an approach within infrastructuring 
affords the researcher a dialogical tool and a sensitized case-by-case 
deliberation. As mentioned in the introduction of this chapter, the 
infrastructure is an intricately woven fabric of resources, history, 
knowledge and practices (Bucciarelli, 1994; Neuman & Star, 1996), 
and no one actor can have a complete overview. Collaborative des
criptions, accounts and narrative make it possible to identify, under
stand and create those alignments that infrastructuring practitioners 
seek together, allowing a broader, messier and more comprehensive 
understanding of the infrastructure.

190
7. SUMMARY
 
'This chapter concludes the thesis by summarising the research 
programme and discussing its programmatic answers. It will also review 
the limitations of the study and propose directions for future research. 
This thesis sprung from tension. First experienced as the tension of 
knowing when to step in and when to step back-should you always act 
when you can? As designers, as well as researchers, we hold authority: 
the authority of defining problems and following that, the authority of 
defining directions and solutions based on that problem. The thesis has 
argued that the professional practices of design and design research have 
traditionally been seen as artisan practices with a level of professional 
expertise that grants this authority. The discipline in which this thesis 
makes its primary contribution, participatory design, has historically 
sought to challenge the hierarchical relationship between the designer 
and the user. The issue of agency means that the designer must recognise 
herself as a part of an infrastructure to move beyond the notion of the 
artisan or hero designer. The thesis has argued that agency is relational, 
and the ideologies inherent within the structures we operate grant us 
more or less agency, depending on where we stand within them. Many 
of the civil servants that this thesis has described have a marginal agency, 
in that they are tied to the agency of their 'chair' and therefore are not 
able to act reflexively. The issue of agency suggests that, as designers and 
researchers, we have a political and ethical obligation towards those we 
work with to 'free up' agency and make room for reflexive action when 
possible. 
 
This thesis aims to provide participatory design with a discus

191
sion around the issue(s) of agency, as these issues are imperative 
to practice. Infrastructuring, which is defined in this thesis as the 
long-term mobilisation of socio-material infrastructures, provided 
a theoretical and methodological framework for the discussion of 
agency's relational nature and the question of how and when we, as 
designers, should act became one of how and when we should act 
with others. Infrastructuring became the 'hard core' of the research 
programme, which was then informed by field work and theory. 
 
When we look closely at infrastructuring practices, we see 
that they share many commonalities with conventional design prac
tices, but they also have differences. Like conventional design prac
tices, infrastructuring carries out a 'dialogue with the situation' and 
a reflection-in-action; as Schön (1982, 1995) described, they are ite
rative processes of experimentation and prototyping that inform the 
sense-making of the practitioner. However, while conventional de
sign practices define the scope of the project more neatly, with a be
ginning and an end (often coinciding with a delivered product or im
plemented service), infrastructuring is more readily defined through 
the concepts of use-before-use and design-after-design: a long-term 
process that defies a simple problem formulation and solution. This 
way of working places new demands on the infrastructuring prac
titioners-within this thesis referred to as such to emphasise that 
these practitioners are not only trained design professionals but also 
civil servants, community organisers, activists and so on. In this the
sis, I have drawn on Arendtian concepts of work and labour to ar
ticulate the difference between the role of a designer and the role of 
an infrastructuring practitioner. The key difference, I have argued, is 
that the conventional designer works as a 'hero', solving problems 
and presenting a solution. This corresponds with Arendt's definition 
of the work of a craftsperson: prestigious, influential and public. By 
labour, she meant the life-sustaining maintenance work that for the 
most part happened away from the public eye. It is the latter, the 
obscured, repetitive and often undervalued practice of caring and 
maintaining socio-material infrastructures that I have enquired into 
this thesis and sought to exemplify through its many anecdotes. 
 
The empirical data that the exemplars were drawn from 

192
came from the field work. In this thesis, the field work has been 
presented as three primary case studies and three and a half years 
of moving within the ecology of those case studies. The primary 
case studies were Amiralsstaden (2017-2019), a collaborative ci
ty-planning project run by Malmö Stad and Malmö Innovation 
Arena; Livskonceptet (2017-2018), a pilot housing project initiated 
by Lund Municipality together with a number of NGOs, compa
nies and volunteer organisations; and Demokratiambassadörerna 
(2019), a summer school initiative run by Malmö Stad and the NGO 
TiF. All of these projects involved the mobilisation of infrastructures 
and a bridging of several groups and sectors. This infrastructuring 
work has been the focus of the fieldwork. The research has been 
conducted as programmatic design research, where the programme's 
hard core around relational agency has been explored through three 
methodological approaches: an open approach, an experimentative 
approach and a reflective approach. The findings of each approach 
have, in this thesis, been presented as programmatic answers, that is 
to say, a conclusion to the research approach or, as it has been des
cribed in this thesis, a call-and-response between theory and practi
ce. 
 
Drawing on a methodology from democratic design experi
ments (Binder et al., 2015), the use of programmatic answers within 
this thesis sought to maintain some of the values that the resear
ch process had championed even within the research output. For 
example, while working with anecdotes and stories that followed 
the logic of democratic agonism and pluralism, it would have been 
contradictory to deliver any one definite interpretation or answer 
from this. The programmatic answers are situated, and they are the 
result of a dialogue with the situation. As is often the case with 
design research, however, it was not enough merely to find answers 
that defined the socio-material realities with which the program
me interacted. It was necessary to find those stories or narratives 
that were 'good enough' (Gabrys & Pritchard, 2018)-stories that 
could travel and become performative. This, in turn, also responds 
to the methodological stance that this thesis has held towards demo
cracy as quintessentially being about movement. An ethics of care, 

193
however, informed the thesis that it was not enough to merely set 
a story loose into the world where it may become harmful. An ele
ment of care was made possible through the slower, iterative nature 
of collaborative anecdotalization, as this entailed equal amounts of 
listening and maintaining the affective (as well as practical) conse
quences of the stories. At the same time, as Haraway (2016) war
ned us, it is important to make trouble, to stir which has been left 
dormant for too long and to disturb which is taken for granted. 
However, the stories we tell may also become overwhelming, too 
large, in which case it will not be possible to 'co-become' with the 
stories. The call-and-response practice that underlies the three pro
grammatic answers within this thesis has been a way of rooting the 
stories within their situations. Still, with these strategy 'roots' in pla
ce, it was a balancing act: a case-by-case deliberation of how to 
make sure that these were not the kind of roots that trap a story and 
keep it from moving, but rather the kind of roots that nourish and 
help the story grow. 
7.1 THEORETICAL CONTRIBUTIONS AND FINDINGS
 
The findings in this thesis are primarily contributions towards 
the discipline of participatory design and in particular towards the 
intersection of participatory design and infrastructuring. At the be
ginning of the thesis, I listed the dimensions of infrastructuring, as 
they were defined by Star and Ruhleder in 1996. Since then, I have 
discussed how participatory design has picked up and used some of 
the discourses around infrastructuring more than others, and this fea
ture is true for my practice as well. The notion that an infrastructure 
springs from an 'installed base' (Star & Ruhleder, 1996) was the 
departure point for the concept of feral infrastructuring, and their 
reflection that '[S]trangers and outsiders encounter infrastructure as 
a target object to be learned about. New participants acquire a na
turalised familiarity with the object as they become members' (Star 
& Ruhleder, 1996) made me conscious in my practice that the infra
structure comes with an inside (members) and an outside (non-mem

194
bers). Furthermore, the emphasis on how an infrastructure becomes 
'visible upon breakdown was formative for the first iterations of 
the concept affective infrastructuring, together with the notion that 
through changes in infrastructural relations become central' (Star 
& Ruhleder, 1996, p. 113). This encouraged me to look into the 
social and affective tiers of infrastructuring and to consider what the 
consequences of the affective currents within infrastructuring work 
might have on the agency of actors with and without 'membership'. 
Once conscious of these currents, it followed that I, in my fieldwork, 
began discussing and working to intervene in situations where the 
social and affective infrastructure broke down, and after some itera
tions, this eventually grew into the methodological contribution of 
collaborative aecdotalization. 
 
The three concepts have been expanded upon, and in this 
thesis, a chapter is dedicated to each. Both individually and combi
ned, the concepts and the stories around them seek to address how 
we think of agency about participatory design. The stories that have 
informed the concepts have been situated within the Swedish public 
sector and, as such, also carry relevance to the infrastructuring of 
the city. Together, more broadly, they tell the story of how action 
research 'exists to assist society in solving social problems' (Gre
enwood et al., 1993, p. 176) through inventive problem-making 
(Wilkie, 2014). Below, I summarise the concepts briefly and add a 
reflection on how the concepts intersect. 
 
Feral infrastructuring, the first programmatic answer 
presented in this thesis38, is a reformulation of the research pro
gramme. A key characteristic of programmatic design research is 
that it is not congealed at the beginning of the research process, 
but evolves by 'bringing out' the core (the issues, the thematic) of 
38 As I explained in the methods chapter, the concepts were developed through 
three research approaches that ran, at times, overlapping. As such, the concepts 
were developed simultaneously; however, they have been presented in this the
sis in the order that I deemed would make the most sense to the reader. Feral 
infrastructuring has been presented as the first concept because it addresses the 
foundation of the research programme. 

195
the programme and allowing it to interact with the socio-material 
world the programme changes. The change, here, is not merely a 
confirmation or rebuttal of a question, but a re-articulation of the 
programme itself. In short, programmatic design research suggests 
that by interacting and experimenting within the world, designers 
and researchers change their pre-conceived ideas and, in turn, our 
worldviews. As such, the first programmatic answer presented in 
this thesis is the rearticulated programme. After exposing the core 
thematic of infrastructuring and the issues of agency to three and a 
half years of field work, the programme evolved into the situated 
framework presented in chapter 4: 'Feral Infrastructuring'. Feral in
frastructuring should be read as a re-defining 'why' in relation to 
the original programme and to the additional two programmatic 
answers. Feral infrastructuring seeks to expand on some of the pre
vious theoretical frameworks around infrastructuring by suggesting 
that organisations, as well as projects, cannot be understood in iso
lation but only as part of a broader ecology. While the definitions of 
an infrastructure used by Star and Ruhleder (1996) and many others 
emphasise that the infrastructure is 'sunk into' a broader 'invisible' 
network, which may become visible upon breakdown, and articulate 
it as important for infrastructuring to recognise these attachments, 
feral infrastructuring goes beyond this in embracing the messiness of 
entangled social, material, digital and affective tiers within a project 
or organisation. Recognising such ecologies requires (both political
ly and ethically) that the infrastructuring practitioner become sensi
tised not just to the behaviour of the actors within an organisation 
or project, but also to the positioning of the organisation or project 
within society. A dialectic analysis is necessary: by understanding the 
grander ideological base that shapes the organisation or project, and 
how this shapes the rules (written and unwritten) and values that 
dictate action within the infrastructure. These rules are the norms 
that determine who can participate and how they should act. 
 
However, as the concept of feral infrastructuring sug
gests, the infrastructure is inherently tied to other infrastructures, 
networks, organisations and projects. As I worked with my case stu

196
dies in Malmö and Lund, most projects and organisations clearly 
overlapped. During the Do-Think-Tank, there was even a recurring 
joke between some of the members to ask each other 'Which pro
ject are we today?', as many of them worked in several overlapping 
projects simultaneously. This overlap also had a temporal element 
to it; for example, many civil servants within Malmö had been in
volved with Amiralsstaden over the past 15 years, in various cons
tellations, but taken off it or moved on to other projects. The story 
of Amiralsstaden is difficult to trace because no one knew it from 
start to finish-and that is only referring to the actors from the mu
nicipality! A plethora of ideas and ideologies shaped the foundation 
of the project, as it stood when I first encountered it in 2017. This 
encouraged me to think that an infrastructure is never pure and is 
always infected. Navigating this means that the dialectical analysis 
of the infrastructuring practitioner must also include the social and 
affective tiers of the organisation. 
 
This thesis has formulated many requests for the infra
structuring practitioner and the collective/collaborative designer. To 
be sensitized is one of the questions asked, but this is, of course, a 
subjective question. Sensitization, I have argued, is like literacy. Lear
