ning how to read means that you may know how to sound letters 
into words, but developing literacy includes everything from reading 
signs to appreciating nuance in well-translated mid-century French 
prose. Sensitization is not a skill that you arrive at; it is something 
that you can always become better at. It is also something that deve
lops with practice; to use the literacy metaphor, we might say that a 
medical doctor has a need for far more medical terms and jargon to 
be considered litreate within their profession than the average per
son would need. I have studied infrastructuring practitioners within 
the public sector and in public, and the recommendations for a sen
sitized practice depart from these situations. However, a few traits 
of sensitized infrastructuring practices have been highlighted in this 
thesis, primarily through the distinction between infrastructuring as 
work and infrastructuring as labour. Arendt argued that her three 
notions of work, labour and action are all political and necessary. 

197
Arendt wrote: 
'Action would be an unnecessary luxury, a capricious inter
ference with general laws of behaviour, if men were endlessly 
reproducible repetitions of the same model, whose nature of 
essence was the same for all and as predictable as the nature 
or essence of any other thing. Plurality is the condition of 
human action because we are all the same, that is, human, in 
such a way that nobody is ever the same as anyone else who 
ever lived, lives, or will live.' (Arendt, 1998, p. 8)
 
Through actions, we make the world again and again. 
However, undeniably, society-in every society-has divisions, indi
cating that this action is reserved disproportionally to those actors 
who possess the mandate, power or resources to act. Most impor
tantly, they are rarely the same individuals that labour to maintain 
life: to care for the bodies in the world. Labour is a position of 
considerably less esteem and prestige than action and work, despite 
the fact that it keeps us alive. In this thesis, I have argued that design 
practices, traditionally, could be seen as work, particularly as artisan 
work. This distinction means that the design practices are politically 
potent, but that they favour those with the privilege to perform it. 
This concept elucidates the labour of the collective designer and the 
infrastructuring practitioner as an actor who deliberately steps back 
from being the producer to a maintenance role-instead of creating 
objects into the world, she creates socio-material alignments. 
 
This foregrounds the second programmatic answer, affective 
infrastructuring, which elaborates on the issue of 'what does affect 
do within infrastructuring processes'. Affective infrastructuring is a 
programmatic answer to a tension that arose in the final program
me of fral infrastructuring. In doing so, a connection between the 
two answers will exist, although they should be seen as complimen
tary and not indivisible. By looking at my data through the lens 
of feral infrastructuring, I observed that many of the participants 
acted upon an 'alien' logic and that this caused an 'overspill' (Mi
chael, 2012b) in the engagement event. Affective infrastructuring 

198
elaborates on the undeniable presence of emotions and the way they 
operate within socio-material infrastructures. By recognising affect, 
the thesis sought to reveal the 'hidden' emotional labour that infra
structuring entails and the imperative for the care and maintenance 
of social retaliations that this in turn brings. 
 
Drawing on Ahmed's (2004, 2014) writings, the thesis views 
emotions as something sticky. Ahmed speaks of the mobility of af
fect through the imagery of something sticky that travels from hand 
to hand via a handshake. Importantly, she also suggested that affect 
not only travels through its hosts but also accumulates. The thesis 
offered an example from a public meeting in Lund, where the facili
tators 'managed' emotions of fear, frustration and anger by contai
ning and shutting down discussions (see p. 104). The fear was that 
the emotions would travel and that the accumulated emotions of the 
group would become destructive. This was presented in the thesis as 
a careless approach to emotions and should be read in contrast to 
the emotional labour performed by an attentive listener in the case 
of Eva and her balcony (see p. 123). The thesis proposes that the 
concept of affective infrastructuring urges us to acknowledge and 
embrace affect in design. 
 
The third programmatic answer, collaborative anecdotaliza
tion, presents a 'how' in relation to the previous two answers. How 
can the infrastructuring practitioner work with these intricate mat
ters of concern that arise when we recognise the affective currents in 
collaborative work and mobilisation? Collaborative anecdotaliza
tion is more than a method; however, it is a methodology-a way of 
relating to and responding to affective tangles and knots. It does not 
seek to say that a universal way of "solving" such tangles exists, but 
rather that it requires a sensitive, patient and empathic approach. In 
this thesis, the concept is presented as three-fold: as a way of being 
within infrastructures, as a way of co-creating knowledge within in
frastructures, and as a way of communicating the knowledge of the 
infrastructuring (research). As anecdotalization is a way of narrating 
and defining a situation, collaborative anecdotalization becomes an 
iterative co-creation of such narratives. Drawing on the ethical dis

199
cussions in this thesis, collaborative anecdotalization has been des
cribed as an ethics of care, an ethics-in-practice through case-by-case 
deliberations. 
 
Together, these three concepts present three perspectives on 
the issue of agency within infrastructuring processes. Together, they 
teach us the fundamental lesson of this thesis: that socio-material in
frastructures are complex, living entities with an even more complex 
and sometimes contradictory logic. Infrastructuring requires sensi
tivity to the unwritten rules and intangible forces, such as affect, 
as well as the more tactile elements and written rules. The thesis 
has operated from a standpoint of a designer who wants to work 
with the people attached to an infrastructure, not as a designer who 
wants to leave his mark upon the construction or build new. A po
tent takeaway from Arendt is that labour is a repetitive, low-status 
maintenance work, which despite being near invisible, is what keeps 
us alive. Labour, as required within infrastructuring work, is a longterm care-labour that does not fit within the frame of conventional 
design 'projects'. The thesis has worked with anecdotes as a way 
of furthering two-way communication, collaborative description-la
bour (the labour of defining social realities) and sense-making that 
amounts to stories worth more than the sum of what happened. 
7.2 REFLECTIONS AND FUTURE DIRECTIONS
 
Twelve years have passed since I stepped off the plane in 
London, and I have spent eleven of those twelve years pondering 
the intersection between design and the political. I sincerely hope 
that the journey does not end here, but this thesis still represents a 
significant milestone along the way. This seems an appropriate time 
to come to some kind of conclusion. 
 
This thesis is a receipt of 5 years of work39. Many of my 
case studies have been of the nature where 'being around', and as 
Anja has taught me, returning the next day to do it again has been 

200
part of the practice. I have found that many conversations cannot 
be rushed. A person might tell you A in the morning, C in the af
ternoon, and finally B three days later. Patience and dependability 
are two qualities that I have found to be as important, or perhaps 
more so, than creativity and intelligence. As this thesis has argu
ed, agency is relational and as such, the greatest challenge within 
infrastructuring is about making relations that can afford agency. 
This is how we move structures and how we move forward. Arendt 
(2005) argued that the movement of the individual is the 'substance 
and meaning of all things political' (p. 129). This perspective echoes 
what has been described in this thesis: the political, from democracy 
to citizenship, resides within the movement. Infrastructuring seeks 
to organise and mobilise socio-material infrastructures so that they 
move forward together, and this is inherently political. 
 
By connecting the stories and concepts that this thesis has 
told, a model of design emerges. Designing, as done by the infra
structuring practitioner, involves, as previously mentioned, a delibe
rate step back from the role of the 'hero' designer. However, desig
ning is a verb, and through actions, assemblies are done, alignments 
are created, and new movements are sparked. In this thesis, I have 
previously asserted that design's claim to democratisation lies in its 
ability to spark momentum by bringing people and things together. 
 
In this thesis, I have previously discussed the negotiation 
between standardised and configurable solutions. In chapter 5, I 
noted that a standardised solution is universal and that by being 
designed for anyone, it is crassly spoken and designed for nobody. 
However, its universality affords that it may be effectively mass pro
duced. A configurable solution requires more of the user: insight 
into the solution's specific applicabilities, a broader set of technical 
skills, the ability to troubleshoot and so on. This dilemma of landing 
somewhere between accessible, on the one hand, and broader, more 
39 These five years have produced, aside from this thesis, several scholarly 
papers (Smedberg, 2019a, 2021a, 2021b; Smedberg & Light, 2018) and two 
official reports, among which one was published by Malmö Stad (Smedberg, 
2019b). During these five years, I have also contributed to several conferences, 
participated in panels and produced three podcasts.

201
open-ended designs on the other. This, I think, is interesting in light 
of the infrastructuring practitioners' work. It begs the question of 
what an 'infrastructuring society' might look like, and this is a ques
tion that I hope I might be able to study further in the future. 
 
An infrastructuring society stretches beyond silos and is 
slow, tentative and configurable. An infrastructuring society is the 
opposite of new public management. It foregrounds learning over 
efficiency and does so by training both public servants and citizens 
in infrastructuring sensitivities. In an infrastructuring society, the 
role of the participatory designer is to engage in labour so that the 
citizens may contribute to the work. Participatory design practi
ces, such as use-before-use and design-after-design, seek to create 
something lasting, but how this 'something' will be managed, ma
intained and configured is beyond the scope of most participatory 
designers' engagement. I believe that the shift this speaks to is a city 
that functions to serve its citizens rather than generate revenue or 
profit. Imagining public sector work within this framework raises 
many questions: How might we rethink efficiency? How might mu
nicipalities reallocate resources to afford a more distributed agency 
when it comes to city planning? 
 
In addition, I believe that an infrastructuring society speaks 
to the feminisation of the political. And I mean this in both practi
cal terms (creating the conditions for equal participation) and in a 
rethinking of those structures that society takes for granted: whose 
voices are heard? Who is considered an expert? What hierarchies 
are we blind to? Infrastructures are, as Star and Ruhleder (1996) ar
gued, something we learn through membership. Working the other 
way around and teaching the infrastructure something are difficult, 
but not impossible. 
7.2.1 LIMITATIONS 
 
As with any study, this thesis has its limitations. The 
COVID-19 pandemic struck Sweden in March 2020 and life as we 
knew it, inside and outside the university halls, changed. So much 

202
of participatory design relies on networks, and a significant part 
of my fieldwork relied on those informal spaces of interaction: the 
lunchrooms, the minutes before and after a meeting when we diver
ge from the set, formalistic agenda. Zoom meetings have, during 
this time, not afforded us those everyday 'pockets' of time where 
relationships can be cared for or where we feel safe to ask 'stupid' 
(often the most important!) questions. I was lucky to have already 
completed about three years of field work before the pandemic hit 
and, as such, had enough empirical data to work with to complete 
the study. However, arguably, the study would not have benefitted 
from another round of experiments, particularly during the 'expe
rimental approach'. The latter is heavily reliant on the democracy 
ambassador project-an experiment that was not designed to stand 
on its own. 
 
Aside from the pandemic, there have been many other un
foreseeable changes during the past 5 years, some of which have 
altered the Swedish academic universe. The GDPR was introduced 
in Sweden when I had been working in the field for about a year and 
a half, and this encouraged me to focus on low-resolution data col
lections. In this thesis, I refrained from using too many photos, for 
example, which is a direct consequence of the fact that I refrained 
from taking photographs as much as possible. During my time in the 
field, I have reflected upon the way that we-in particular within the 
scholarly design disciplines-use photos and images. Taking a pictu
re of a table full of post-it notes and drawings but editing out all 
the potentially sensitive data sends an incomplete image that could 
politically and ethically be a complex issue to navigate. The same 
goes for photographs of groups: rendering the faces blurred could 
be as inappropriate as leaving them unblurred. In this thesis, I have 
attempted to use anecdotes to illustrate the fieldwork and bring it to 
life, following the logic that while both anecdotes and photographs 
are a curated and narrated kind of storytelling, the anecdotes make 
this transparent. In this way, the anecdotes can be seen as situated 
deceptions of an event. 

203
 
Finally, notably, the original scope of this research was large. 
'The issue of agency' as an investigative area is undeniably broad, 
and as a basis for a programmatic worldview, Redström (2017) and 
other researchers have recommended that it should be restricted. 
Looking back on my experience working with this broad outset, I 
can certainly see that this recommendation has merits, particularly 
during my first year when the enquiry often felt too large to manage. 
There is also an imperative placed on programmatic design research 
that says it must speak to more traditional scientific disciplines, so
cial and natural, and speak their language. I was stressed in the early 
stages that I would not be able to deliver a unified theory or that I 
would end up delivering something that could be viewed as 'unsci
entific'. However, as Redström (2017) noted, 'Unlike science, where 
unified theory is a legitimate objective, design is about the possible, 
about how things could be' (p. 99). By keeping the programme 
open-ended, I allowed for an enquiry that did something other than 
reproduce already unified theories. Through its intersection with 
participatory design, the concern of agency was given a framework, 
and through a slow, two-step-forward, one-step-back approach to 
concept development, the theory and practice of this project began 
to mould what would later become the three programmatic answers 
of this thesis. Stengers (2018) argued that conventional sciences 
keep questions small and rigid so that they may become producti
ve. I have thought a lot about this assertion, and the question that 
always pops up in my head is, 'productive for whom?' The use of 
anecdotes in this Ph. D. project and, in this thesis, has sought to keep 
the findings grounded, still attached to their context (in this case 
Malmö, Sweden, city planning processes and public sector public 
engagement), still messy, still embodied-yet still productive. With 
or without this thesis, the anecdotes can travel like vessels for the 
lessons I and my collaborators learned during the process, and my 
hope is that these narratives will function as less categorical catalysts 
for action. 

204
7.3 EPILOGUE
 
Over the course of my Ph. D. I have come to re-evaluate my 
definition of design. This thesis departed from the sum of my fear 
of overstepping boundaries as a designer-a fear of taking over, of 
steamrolling. I arrived in Malmö with a distaste for design, thinking 
that design at worst was capitalism's idle work or, at best, an im
potent white-washing. In these early days, a senior scholar listened 
to me rant about the inherent privileges of academics and desig
ners and how it was impossible for us to do anything in the world 
without pushing someone else aside. The only option I claimed then 
was to refrain from doing anything as a designer. However, he asked 
me, is it not also a privilege to refrain from acting? 
 
I came to the discipline of participatory design because it 
held a commitment to altering power dynamics between 'designers' 
on the one hand and 'users' on the other. And since then, I have 
followed my colleagues around Malmö, Sweden and Scandinavia, 
and I have watched them engage with people, issues and places with 
empathy and respect for the situations they met. This experience in
formed and reshaped my understanding of what design is and what 
designing should be. And ultimately, such a practice is what this 
thesis has been about: finding ways to act without discouraging oth
ers from doing the same. Finding ways to acknowledge multitude 
without becoming overwhelmed by it.
 
 
Inquiring into the relationship between the municipal agen
cy to act and the agency of individual citizens has taught me more 
about design's ability to change the world than I learned as a design 
student. I now regard design akin to the quiver of arrows that Sen
nett (2009) described: A repertoire of approaches that we acqui
re through experience, which helps us meet each situation with the 
appropriate amount of force and motion. Furthermore, I have found 
that the metaphorical arrows of participatory design are shared. By 
coming together, we also draw on the experience of a group rather 
than an individual, giving us a greater range of tools from which 

205
to choose when we act. I am not denying the difficulties in this; 
however, having more tools at your disposal does not necessarily 
mean we pick the right one, after all. Having more people involved 
does not necessarily make the negotiation over what the 'right one' 
is easier. However, this process of sharing perspectives and experien
ces, of negotiating and of moving forward together is what I have 
come to understand design as-a process, not an outcome. This, 
too, distinguishes design research from many other scholarly practi
ces, as it is imperative within design to do something. As Karl Marx 
famously said, 'Philosophers have only interpreted the world, but 
the point is to change it'. 
And so, I answer the question that this thesis has revolved around, 
the questions of when we should act, with this: always. Always with 
care. 

206
SAMMANFATTNING
 
Varje organisation, samarbete, project, eller social rörelse är 
i huvudsak ett kluster av förbindelser mellan människor, platser, och 
ting. Genom dessa förbindelser skapas nätverk och genom nätverken 
kan handlingar möjliggöras, eller begränsas. Dessa socio-materiella för
bindelser förstås inom denna avhandling som infrastrukturer, och det 
är avhandlingens syfte att granska hur infrastrukturer kan mobiliseras. 
Att mobilisera socio-materiella infrastrukturer över tid är vad jag kallar 
infrastrukturering.
 
Denna avhandling, som ligger inom disciplinen Deltagande 
Design och de teoretiska traditionerna Science and Technology Studies 
(STS) och Feminist Technoscience, undersöker frågan om agency inom 
infrastruktureringsprocesser. Avhandlingen utgår från föreställningen 
att all agens är relationell och skapad genom relationer. Dessa kan vara 
materiella relationer, maktrelationer, eller affektiva relationer. Den rela
tionionella faktorn medför ett politiskt imperativ för dessa infrastruktu
rutövare: de individer som arbetar för att skapa nya anpassningar och 
flytta infrastrukturen framåt - att ta hänsyn till de marginaliserade rös
terna inom infrastrukturen. Arbetet som utförs här är, hävdar avhand
lingen, inte ett prestigefyllt hantverksarbete utan snarare ett långsamt, 
omvårdande, och repetitivt underhållsarbete. Informerad av Hannah 
Arendts teorier skiljer avhandlingen mellan dessa två kategorier av ar
bete: work som det slagkraftiga, prestigefulla och inflytelserika; labour 
som det långsamma, underhållsarbete som är nödvändigt men sällan 
högt värderat. Labour innefattar den kategori av arbete som historiskt 
sett tilldelats kvinnor. Arendt har definierat jobb (work),arbete (labour) 

207
och handling som tre inbördes sammanflätade men ändå distinkta 
begrepp som definierar sätt att vara i världen, sätt att agera politiskt. 
De tre begreppen förstärker och kompletterar varandra, men den
na avhandling lägger särskild vikt vid arbete (labour). Arbetskraft 
osynliggörs ofta, feminiseras och undervärderas och denna avhand
ling undersöker arbetet inom infrastrukturprocesser och föreslår 
metoder för att belysa och stödja det.
 
Avhandlingen bygger på tre fallstudier i Malmö och Lund, 
Sverige. Alla tre studier genomfördes inom ramen för offentlig sek
tor, och inom projekt som betonade medborgarengagemang och dia
log. Fallstudierna har en gemensam knutpunkt i infrastrukturerings 
processer och presenteras både som ett studieämne, och som en me
tod för deltagare och forskare. Metodologiskt har Ph.D. projektet 
genomförts genom praktikbaserad, deltagande, och programmatisk 
design forskning, som sammanför fallstudierna till en undersökning, 
och som slutligen föreslår tre "programmatiska svar" som hanterar 
frågan om agens inom infrastruktureringsprocesser.
 
Det första programmatiska svaret, Feral Infrastructures 
(otämjda infrastrukturer), omformulerar programmets initiala 
världsbild, och utvecklar begreppet infrastrukturer som rörig och 
föränderlig terräng, som motstår försök att kategorisera dem. Infra
strukturernas gränser sträcker sig långt bortom de socio-materiella 
begränsningar som kommer av att vi definierar ett projekt eller en 
organisation. Avhandlingen hävdar att detta utgör ett imperativ för 
infrastrukturutövaren att bli inlyssnande genitmot sin terräng, och 
att utveckla en reflexiv praxis för att interagera med den.
 
Det andra programmatiska svaret, Affective Infrastructuring 
(affektiv infrastrukturering), utgår ifrån att det finns en affektiv på
verkan inom infrastrukturarbetet. Känsloarbete och affektiva eko
nomier tas upp här som faktorer med förmåga att skapa eller bryta 
samarbeten, som argument för en vårdetik.
 
Det tredje och sista programmatiska svaret, Collaborative 
Anecdotalization (Kollaborativ anekdotalisering), är en föreslagen 
metod för att interagera med den röriga, affektiva terrängen av in
frastrukturer. Anekdotalisering presenteras här som en ömsesidig 

208
praxis bortom beskrivningar: ett tillvägagångssätt som innehar so
cialvetenskapens förmåga att definiera sociala realiteter; men som 
vidare kan återberätta och utmana dem; främja och anpassa dem. 
Begreppet Collaborative Anecdotalization syftar på att ingen aktör 
kan ha en fullständig överblick över en infrastruktur och att det utan 
samskapade beskrivningar är omöjligt att identifiera, förstå och ska
pa de anpassningar som infrastrukturutövare söker.
 
Genomgående använder denna avhandling anekdoter som 
situerade, förkroppsligade redogörelser för empirisk data. Berättel
serna som återberättas i den här boken har valts ut för att bjuda in 
läsaren till det praktiska arbetet som ligger till grund för de begrepp 
som presenteras ovan, och, i överensstämmelse med projektets me
todik, tar hänsyn till att varje händelse eller interaktion kan ses ur 
flera perspektiv och berätta flera berättelser.

MALMÖ UNIVERSITY

E
very organisation, cooperation, project or social 
movement is quintessentially a cluster of align
ments between people, places and things. Through 
these alignments, networks are made, and through 
these networks action can be made possible or be 
constricted. This thesis examines infrastructures as 
feral entities: unruly and impossible to tame. The 
task of the participatory designer is to work with 
the infrastructure, to hone its particularities and to 
develop a sensitivity towards it. Grounded in three 
primary case studies in the public sector, this thesis 
proposes a participatory design praxis which pla
ces empathy and affect at the forefront, and does 
so through the use of collaborative storytelling and 
anecdotalization.
